Here is the beginner's picture of the event loop. (Part 9.16 to 9.18 cover the event loop phases, microtasks, macrotasks and libuv in full.)

  Your synchronous code runs top to bottom on the one thread.
        |
        |  hits a slow operation (query, HTTP, timer, file read)
        v
  Node hands it to the OS / a thread pool and registers a callback
        |
        |  your code keeps running; the thread is free
        v
  [ Event loop ] keeps checking: "is any result ready?"
        |
        |  when a result arrives, its callback is queued
        v
  The callback runs on the one thread when it is free again

Three things to take away:

  1. Your own JavaScript is never interrupted mid-function. A function runs to completion before anything else runs. This makes a lot of reasoning simple — there are no surprise interruptions in the middle of your code.
  2. Slow I/O does not block the thread, because Node hands it off and comes back via a callback.
  3. Slow computation does block the thread, because computation is your JavaScript, and your JavaScript holds the one thread until it returns.

Practical example

console.log('1: start');

setTimeout(() => {
  console.log('3: timer fired after everyone else');
}, 0);

console.log('2: end');

// Output order:
// 1: start
// 2: end
// 3: timer fired after everyone else

Even though the timer is 0 milliseconds, 3 prints last. The setTimeout callback is handed to the event loop and can only run after the current synchronous code (1 and 2) finishes. This surprises every beginner and is the clearest demonstration that "asynchronous" means "later, when the thread is free", not "right now in parallel".

Step-by-step trace: two requests sharing one thread

Let us slow the event loop right down and watch it work. Below, two "requests" each send a database query. We fake the queries with timers: request 1's query takes 20 ms, request 2's takes 10 ms.

console.log('A: request 1 arrives, query sent');
setTimeout(() => console.log('D: request 1 query result'), 20);
setTimeout(() => console.log('C: request 2 query result'), 10);
console.log('B: request 2 arrives, query sent');
A: request 1 arrives, query sent
B: request 2 arrives, query sent
C: request 2 query result
D: request 1 query result

Here is what the one thread does, moment by moment:

Time What the thread does Waiting in the background
0 ms Prints A. Registers a 20 ms timer. Registers a 10 ms timer. Prints B. The script's top-level code ends. 20 ms timer, 10 ms timer
0 to 10 ms Nothing. The thread is idle and free for any other work. both timers
10 ms The event loop sees the 10 ms timer is due and runs its callback: prints C. 20 ms timer
10 to 20 ms Idle again. 20 ms timer
20 ms Runs the 20 ms timer's callback: prints D. Nothing is left, so Node exits. nothing

Three lessons are hiding in that table:

  1. Both queries were "in flight" at the same time, even with one thread. The waiting happened in the background; the thread only did the tiny bits of work at the start and the end.
  2. Results come back in the order they finish, not the order you asked. Real database and HTTP calls behave the same way; never assume a reply order.
  3. The total time was about 20 ms, not 30 ms. The waits overlapped, which is the whole reason Node can serve many users on one thread.

Seeing the block for yourself

Now the bad case. In this script a "heartbeat" timer fires every 100 ms; think of each beat as "other users being served". At about 250 ms, one request does 600 ms of pure computation, like the CSV parser in the failure story below.

// Every 100 ms the "heartbeat" stands in for other users being served.
const start = Date.now();
let last = start;
const heartbeat = setInterval(() => {
  const now = Date.now();
  console.log(`t=${now - start} ms  other users served (gap ${now - last} ms)`);
  last = now;
}, 100);

// At about 250 ms, one request does 600 ms of pure computation.
setTimeout(() => {
  console.log(`t=${Date.now() - start} ms  CSV request starts a synchronous parse`);
  const busyUntil = Date.now() + 600;
  while (Date.now() < busyUntil) {
    // pretend to parse rows; the thread never gets a chance to do anything else
  }
  console.log(`t=${Date.now() - start} ms  CSV parse finished`);
}, 250);

setTimeout(() => clearInterval(heartbeat), 1150);
t=101 ms  other users served (gap 101 ms)
t=203 ms  other users served (gap 102 ms)
t=251 ms  CSV request starts a synchronous parse
t=851 ms  CSV parse finished
t=851 ms  other users served (gap 648 ms)
t=952 ms  other users served (gap 101 ms)
t=1053 ms  other users served (gap 101 ms)

Look at the gap of 648 ms. The heartbeat wanted to run at 300, 400 and so on up to 800 ms, but the thread was stuck in the while loop. Those beats were simply skipped, and the next one ran the moment the loop let go. In a real server, every request that arrived during those 600 ms waited up to 600 ms extra, no matter how simple it was. That delay is called event-loop lag, and production teams graph it (Part 9.27 shows how).

Production scenario

A NestJS endpoint handles 2,000 requests per second on a single Node process. Each request does three database queries (about 2 ms each) and one call to a pricing service (about 40 ms). The thread is busy with actual JavaScript for maybe 0.5 ms per request; the other ~46 ms is waiting on I/O, during which the thread serves other requests. That is how one thread serves 2,000 requests per second: it is almost never computing, almost always waiting, and the event loop fills the waits with other requests' work.

Failure scenario

At 14:20 an endpoint that validates uploaded CSV files starts timing out for all users, not just the ones uploading. The cause: a new feature parsed each uploaded file with a synchronous loop that, for a large file, ran for 800 milliseconds of pure computation. During those 800 ms the one thread was held, so every other request — logins, product pages, checkouts — queued behind it. The dashboard showed the whole service degraded whenever anyone uploaded a big file. The fix was to move the parsing off the main thread (a worker thread, Part 9.20) and to stream the file instead of loading it whole. The lesson: on Node, one slow synchronous operation harms everyone.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • A long synchronous loop or heavy computation blocks every request (CPU-bound work needs worker threads or a separate service; Part 9).
  • A synchronous file read (fs.readFileSync) in a request handler blocks the thread for the duration of the read; always use the asynchronous versions in a server.
  • A catastrophic regular expression (ReDoS) can block the thread for seconds on a crafted input — both a performance and a security problem (Part 39 — Security).

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior carries one rule from this section into everything: do not block the event loop. They know Node's superpower is handling many concurrent waits cheaply and its weakness is CPU-bound work, so they keep request handlers light on computation, push heavy work to background workers (Part 35 — Background Jobs and Schedulers), and measure event-loop lag as a first-class metric (Part 9.27). When someone proposes doing image processing or large-file parsing inside a request handler, the senior's first question is "how long does that hold the thread, and whom does it block while it runs?"

Common mistakes

  • Thinking setTimeout(fn, 0) runs fn immediately; it runs after the current synchronous code.
  • Doing heavy computation in a request handler and blocking all other requests.
  • Using synchronous file or crypto functions (...Sync) in a server.
  • Assuming Node is multi-threaded for your code; your JavaScript runs on one thread.

Interview questions

  1. Node runs on one thread — how does it handle thousands of concurrent requests? It never blocks the thread on I/O; it hands slow operations to the OS and returns to them via callbacks through the event loop, filling the waits with other work.
  2. Why does setTimeout(fn, 0) not run fn immediately? The callback is queued and can only run after the current synchronous code finishes and the event loop reaches it.
  3. What is the danger of a CPU-bound loop in a request handler? It blocks the single thread, freezing every other request until it finishes.

Practical exercise

Write a script that prints A, schedules a setTimeout(..., 0) that prints C, and prints B, then predict and verify the output order. Then add a synchronous loop that counts to 1 billion between A and B and observe that C is delayed by the whole loop, demonstrating event-loop blocking. Acceptance criteria: explain in two sentences why C is delayed.

Worked answer to the exercise

const start = Date.now();
console.log('A');
setTimeout(() => console.log(`C (after ${Date.now() - start} ms)`), 0);

let count = 0;
for (let i = 0; i < 1_000_000_000; i++) {
  count++;
}

console.log(`B (loop counted to ${count} in ${Date.now() - start} ms)`);
A
B (loop counted to 1000000000 in 298 ms)
C (after 299 ms)

The two-sentence explanation: the timer callback can only run when the thread is free, and the thread is not free until all the top-level synchronous code (including the whole loop) has finished. So a "0 ms" timer waited about 300 ms, and in a server every other request would have waited the same 300 ms.

Try it yourself

Predict the output of each script before you run it. Answers follow.

1. Two timers with the same delay.

console.log('order received');
setTimeout(() => console.log('email sent'), 0);
setTimeout(() => console.log('invoice generated'), 0);
console.log('response returned');

2. A slow query and a fast query.

setTimeout(() => console.log('slow query done'), 50);
setTimeout(() => console.log('fast query done'), 5);
console.log('handler finished');

3. A timer stuck behind a busy loop. How many milliseconds will the message report?

const start = Date.now();
setTimeout(() => console.log(`timer ran after ${Date.now() - start} ms`), 10);
const until = Date.now() + 200;
while (Date.now() < until) {
  // busy: nothing else can run
}
console.log('loop done');

Answers.

  1. order received, response returned, email sent, invoice generated. Synchronous lines first. Timers with the same delay run in the order they were registered.
  2. handler finished, fast query done, slow query done. The synchronous line runs first; then results arrive in the order they finish, not the order they were asked for.
  3. loop done, then timer ran after 201 ms (about 200 on your machine). The timer asked for 10 ms but could not run until the busy loop gave the thread back. A timer's delay is a minimum wait, never a guarantee.

2.2 Callbacks

2.2.1 The callback pattern

Concept

A callback is a function you give to another function to be called later, when some work finishes. "Here is my phone number; call me back when the result is ready." Callbacks were the original way JavaScript handled asynchronous results, and they still appear in older Node APIs and some libraries.

Why does it exist?

Because the result is not ready yet. A function that reads a file cannot return the file contents immediately — the file takes time to read. So instead it says "give me a function, and I will call it with the contents once I have them." The callback is how the slow function delivers its result after the wait.

Mental model

You order a coffee and the barista takes your name. You do not stand frozen at the counter staring at the machine (that would be blocking). You sit down and do other things. When the coffee is ready, the barista calls your name (the callback) and you come get it. Your name is the callback; the barista calling it is the asynchronous completion.

How it works

Node's traditional callbacks follow the error-first convention: the callback's first argument is an error (or null if all went well), and the second is the result. This convention is everywhere in older Node code.

import { readFile } from 'node:fs';

// readFile is asynchronous: it returns immediately and calls the callback later.
readFile('order.json', 'utf8', (err, data) => {
  if (err) {
    console.error('failed to read file:', err.message);
    return;                          // must return so we do not use data
  }
  console.log('file contents:', data);
});

console.log('this prints BEFORE the file contents');

The console.log at the bottom runs first, because readFile returns immediately and the callback runs later when the file has been read. Checking err first, and returning on error, is mandatory — forgetting it leads to using an undefined result.

A callback example you can run without any files

The readFile example needs a real file on disk. This one fakes a database lookup with a timer, so you can run it anywhere. It follows the same error-first convention that Node's own APIs use.

type User = { id: string; email: string };
type Callback<T> = (err: Error | null, result?: T) => void;

const users: Record<string, User> = { usr_7: { id: 'usr_7', email: 'alice@example.com' } };

// A pretend database call: answers after 50 ms, error-first.
function findUser(id: string, callback: Callback<User>): void {
  setTimeout(() => {
    const user = users[id];
    if (!user) {
      callback(new Error(`user ${id} not found`));
      return;
    }
    callback(null, user);
  }, 50);
}

findUser('usr_7', (err, user) => {
  if (err) return console.error('lookup 1 failed:', err.message);
  console.log('lookup 1 found:', user!.email);
});

findUser('usr_404', (err, user) => {
  if (err) return console.error('lookup 2 failed:', err.message);
  console.log('lookup 2 found:', user!.email);
});

console.log('both lookups started');
both lookups started
lookup 1 found: alice@example.com
lookup 2 failed: user usr_404 not found

Read it as a story. Both lookups start straight away and return immediately. The last line prints first. About 50 ms later both timers are due; each callback runs once, checks err first, and either uses the result or reports the error. Notice the ! in user!.email: TypeScript cannot know that user is set when err is null, so we tell it. That small awkwardness is one more reason the promise style, where success and failure take different paths, is nicer.

Practical example

Chaining two dependent asynchronous steps with callbacks: read a config file, then read the file it points to.

import { readFile } from 'node:fs';

readFile('config.json', 'utf8', (err, configRaw) => {
  if (err) return console.error('config read failed:', err.message);

  const config = JSON.parse(configRaw);
  readFile(config.dataPath, 'utf8', (err2, data) => {
    if (err2) return console.error('data read failed:', err2.message);
    console.log('data:', data);
  });
});

Notice the nesting. Each dependent step goes one level deeper. With two steps it is fine. With six, it becomes the problem described next.

Failure scenario (callback hell)

A real order-processing flow had seven dependent asynchronous steps: validate, check inventory, reserve stock, charge card, create shipment, send email, write audit log. Written with callbacks, it became a pyramid eight levels deep, with error handling duplicated at every level and a growing rightward drift that engineers nicknamed "the christmas tree". A bug in the refund path went unnoticed for weeks because the error handling at level six silently swallowed it — a return was missing, so after logging the error the code fell through and continued as if nothing was wrong, charging the card after inventory had failed. The deep nesting hid the control-flow bug. Promises and async/await were invented largely to make this class of bug visible and rare.

doA(function (err, a) {
  if (err) ...
  doB(a, function (err, b) {
    if (err) ...
    doC(b, function (err, c) {
      if (err) ...
        doD(c, function (err, d) {    // the pyramid keeps growing right
          ...
        });
    });
  });
});

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Forgetting return after handling an error, so the code continues with a bad or undefined result (the refund bug above).
  • Calling the callback twice (for example in both a success and an error path), causing downstream logic to run twice — a real source of double-charges.
  • Errors thrown inside a callback are not caught by a try/catch around the function that scheduled it, because the callback runs later, on a different turn of the event loop. This is a major reason promises are better.
  • Unhandled errors in a callback with no error handling crash the process.

Seeing the two classic bugs

Reading about a bug is not the same as seeing it. Run these two scripts.

Bug 1: try/catch cannot catch an error thrown inside a callback.

function chargeCard(amountCents: number, callback: (err: Error | null, chargeId?: string) => void) {
  setTimeout(() => {
    if (amountCents <= 0) throw new Error('invalid amount'); // thrown LATER, inside the timer
    callback(null, 'ch_1001');
  }, 10);
}

try {
  chargeCard(-500, () => {});
} catch (err) {
  console.log('caught:', (err as Error).message); // never runs
}
console.log('try/catch block finished');
try/catch block finished
file:///.../trycatch.ts:3
    if (amountCents <= 0) throw new Error('invalid amount'); // thrown LATER, inside the timer
                          ^

Error: invalid amount
    at Timeout._onTimeout (file:///.../trycatch.ts:3:33)
    at listOnTimeout (node:internal/timers:685:17)
    at process.processTimers (node:internal/timers:618:7)

Step by step: chargeCard registers a timer and returns at once. Nothing has gone wrong yet, so the catch has nothing to catch, and the try/catch block finishes. Ten milliseconds later the timer callback runs and throws. By then the try is long gone; there is no handler anywhere on the stack, so Node prints the error and the whole process exits. In a server that means every user's request dies, not just this one. The fix is to never throw inside a callback; pass the error to the callback instead (callback(new Error('invalid amount'))).

Bug 2: a missing return calls the callback twice.

function reserveStock(
  sku: string,
  qty: number,
  callback: (err: Error | null, reservation?: { sku: string; qty: number }) => void,
) {
  setTimeout(() => {
    if (qty > 5) callback(new Error(`only 5 of ${sku} left`)); // BUG: no return
    callback(null, { sku, qty });
  }, 10);
}

reserveStock('mirror-01', 8, (err, reservation) => {
  if (err) return console.log('reservation failed:', err.message);
  console.log('reservation ok, charging card for', reservation!.qty, 'items');
});
reservation failed: only 5 of mirror-01 left
reservation ok, charging card for 8 items

The caller was told "failed" and then, a moment later, "ok". If the success path charges a card, the customer is charged for stock that does not exist. This is the refund bug from the failure story above, in eleven lines. The fix is one word: return callback(new Error(...)).

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior reads callback-based code carefully for the two classic bugs — missing return after an error, and double invocation — because both are invisible in deeply nested code and both cause money bugs (double charges, continuing after a failed step). In new code they never write raw callbacks for multi-step flows; they promisify the callback API (util.promisify, or use the fs/promises version) and use async/await. Callbacks remain fine for genuine event streams (an event emitter firing many times), which promises do not model well.

Common mistakes

  • Deeply nested callbacks ("callback hell") that hide control-flow bugs.
  • Missing return after an error check.
  • Mixing try/catch with callbacks and wondering why errors are not caught.
  • Calling a callback more than once.

Interview questions

  1. What is the error-first callback convention? The callback's first argument is an error (or null), the second is the result; you must check the error before using the result.
  2. Why does try/catch not catch errors thrown inside an asynchronous callback? The callback runs on a later turn of the event loop, after the try/catch has already finished; the error has no surrounding try at that point.
  3. What is callback hell and what replaced it? Deeply nested callbacks for dependent async steps; promises and async/await replaced it with flat, readable, properly-catchable code.

Practical exercise

Take a three-level nested callback flow (read file, parse, write a derived file) and identify where a missing return after an error check would cause the next step to run with bad data. Then note which line a double callback invocation would break. Acceptance criteria: you can point to the exact failure for each bug without running the code.

Worked answer to the exercise

Here is the three-level flow, with the two dangerous spots marked.

import { readFile, writeFile } from 'node:fs';

readFile('orders.json', 'utf8', (err, raw) => {
  if (err) console.error('read failed:', err.message);  // (1) BUG: no return

  const orders = JSON.parse(raw);                         // runs even after a failed read
  const totalCents = orders.reduce((sum: number, o: { totalCents: number }) => sum + o.totalCents, 0);

  writeFile('daily-total.json', JSON.stringify({ totalCents }), (err2) => {
    if (err2) return console.error('write failed:', err2.message);
    console.log('daily total written');                  // (2) a double call would print this twice
  });
});
  1. Missing return. If orders.json does not exist, the error is logged, but the code carries on. raw is undefined, so JSON.parse(raw) throws a SyntaxError inside the callback, and, as you just saw, an error thrown in a callback crashes the process. Worse versions of this bug do not crash: they quietly write a wrong file. The fix is if (err) return console.error(...).
  2. Double invocation. If writeFile (or any function you wrote in its place) called its callback twice, the "daily total written" step would run twice. Here that is a duplicate log line. If that step sent the finance team an email or charged a card, it would be a duplicate email or a double charge.

From callbacks to promises: util.promisify

You will meet callback APIs in older libraries. You do not have to keep writing callbacks to use them. Node ships util.promisify, which wraps any error-first callback function and gives you back a version that returns a promise (2.3). Most of Node's own APIs also have a promise version already, such as node:fs/promises.

import { promisify } from 'node:util';

type User = { id: string; email: string };

function findUser(id: string, callback: (err: Error | null, user?: User) => void): void {
  setTimeout(() => {
    if (id !== 'usr_7') return callback(new Error(`user ${id} not found`));
    callback(null, { id, email: 'alice@example.com' });
  }, 50);
}

// promisify turns an error-first callback function into one that returns a promise.
const findUserAsync = promisify(findUser);

const user = await findUserAsync('usr_7');
console.log('found:', user?.email);

try {
  await findUserAsync('usr_404');
} catch (err) {
  console.log('failed:', (err as Error).message);
}
found: alice@example.com
failed: user usr_404 not found

Do not worry yet about await; 2.4 explains it. The point here is that the error now lands in an ordinary try/catch, which is exactly what the callback version could not do. The real-world rule: when you find a callback API, promisify it once at the edge of your code and use promises everywhere else.

Try it yourself

1. Predict the order.

function loadCart(userId: string, callback: (err: Error | null, items?: string[]) => void) {
  setTimeout(() => callback(null, ['mirror-01', 'probe-02']), 10);
}

console.log('1: handler starts');
loadCart('usr_7', (err, items) => {
  if (err) return console.error(err.message);
  console.log(`3: cart has ${items!.length} items`);
});
console.log('2: handler returns');

2. Find the bug. A user applies the coupon FREEBIE, which does not exist. What does this print, and what is wrong?

function applyCoupon(code: string, callback: (err: Error | null, percentOff?: number) => void) {
  setTimeout(() => {
    if (code !== 'SAVE10') {
      callback(new Error(`coupon ${code} is not valid`));
    }
    callback(null, 10);
  }, 10);
}

applyCoupon('FREEBIE', (err, percentOff) => {
  if (err) {
    console.log('coupon rejected:', err.message);
    return;
  }
  console.log(`coupon applied: ${percentOff}% off`);
});

Answers.

  1. 1: handler starts, 2: handler returns, 3: cart has 2 items. The callback always runs after the current synchronous code.
  2. It prints coupon rejected: coupon FREEBIE is not valid and then coupon applied: 10% off. The if block calls the callback but does not return, so execution falls through to the success call. The caller's return inside its own if (err) does not help; that only leaves the callback, not applyCoupon. The fix is return callback(new Error(...)) inside applyCoupon.

2.3 Promises

2.3.1 What a promise is

Concept

A promise is an object that represents a value that is not ready yet but will be (or will fail) in the future. It is a placeholder for an asynchronous result. Instead of handing a callback into a function, the function hands you back a promise, and you attach what to do on success (.then) and on failure (.catch).

A promise is always in one of three states: pending (still working), fulfilled (succeeded, has a value), or rejected (failed, has an error). Once it settles (fulfilled or rejected) it never changes again.

Why does it exist?

Promises exist to fix the problems with callbacks: the nesting pyramid, the duplicated error handling, and the fact that try/catch could not catch asynchronous errors. A promise is a first-class object you can pass around, return, store and combine. That object-ness is what lets you flatten the pyramid into a chain, handle all errors in one place, and run several asynchronous operations together.

Mental model

A promise is a tracking number for a parcel. The moment you order, you do not have the parcel, but you have a tracking number — a handle to the eventual delivery. You can check its status (pending, delivered, failed). You can say "when it is delivered, do this" (.then) and "if delivery fails, do that" (.catch). You can even say "when all five of my parcels arrive, start the party" (Promise.all). The tracking number exists now; the parcel comes later.

How it works

stateDiagram-v2
    [*] --> pending
    pending --> fulfilled: resolve(value)
    pending --> rejected: reject(error)
    fulfilled --> [*]
    rejected --> [*]
// A promise that resolves after a delay. This is the promise version of setTimeout.
function delay(ms: number): Promise<void> {
  return new Promise((resolve) => {
    setTimeout(resolve, ms);
  });
}

// Using .then / .catch
delay(1000)
  .then(() => console.log('one second passed'))
  .catch((err) => console.error('something failed', err));

A promise that already comes from a library (a database driver, an HTTP client) is the common case; you rarely build one with new Promise yourself. When you do, you call resolve(value) on success or reject(error) on failure.

A promise you can watch change state

This script builds a promise-returning version of a product lookup and prints the promise itself at two moments, so you can see the state change with your own eyes.

type Product = { id: string; name: string; priceCents: number };

const catalog: Record<string, Product> = {
  '42': { id: '42', name: 'Dental Mirror', priceCents: 1299 },
};

// A pretend database call that returns a promise instead of taking a callback.
function findProduct(id: string): Promise<Product> {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    setTimeout(() => {
      const product = catalog[id];
      if (product) resolve(product);                       // pending -> fulfilled
      else reject(new Error(`product ${id} not found`));   // pending -> rejected
    }, 30);
  });
}

const found = findProduct('42');
console.log('right away:', found);                 // the promise exists, the value does not yet

found.then((product) => console.log('fulfilled with:', product.name));

findProduct('999')
  .then((product) => console.log('never printed:', product.name))
  .catch((err) => console.log('rejected with:', err.message));

setTimeout(() => console.log('50 ms later:', found), 50);
right away: Promise { <pending> }
fulfilled with: Dental Mirror
rejected with: product 999 not found
50 ms later: Promise { { id: '42', name: 'Dental Mirror', priceCents: 1299 } }

Walk through it:

  1. findProduct('42') runs the function you passed to new Promise (it is called the executor) straight away. The executor only starts a timer, so the promise comes back pending. That is the first line.
  2. .then(...) does not run anything yet. It registers "when this promise fulfils, call me", like leaving your name with the barista.
  3. After 30 ms the timer calls resolve(product). The promise becomes fulfilled, and the registered .then callback runs with the product.
  4. The second lookup calls reject(...). Its .then is skipped and its .catch runs.
  5. At 50 ms we print the first promise again. Node now shows the value inside: it is fulfilled and will stay that way forever.

Compared with the callback findUser in 2.2, the work is the same, but the caller gets an object back immediately, and success and failure travel down separate paths (.then and .catch).

Chaining and flattening

The key improvement over callbacks: .then returns a new promise, so you chain instead of nesting. Dependent steps go down, not right.

fetchOrder(orderId)                       // returns Promise<Order>
  .then((order) => fetchCustomer(order.customerId))  // returns Promise<Customer>
  .then((customer) => sendReceipt(customer.email))   // returns Promise<void>
  .then(() => console.log('done'))
  .catch((err) => console.error('any step failed:', err.message));

Two things to notice. First, the flow reads top to bottom even though each step depends on the previous — no pyramid. Second, one .catch at the end handles a failure in any of the steps. If fetchCustomer rejects, the chain skips the remaining .thens and jumps straight to .catch. This is the single biggest practical win over callbacks: error handling in one place.

A subtle but critical rule: you must return the inner promise from a .then (return fetchCustomer(...)), or the chain does not wait for it. Forgetting the return is a classic bug where the next step runs before the previous one finished.

The three rules of .then

Everything about chaining follows from three rules about what you do inside a .then callback:

Inside the .then you... The next step in the chain...
return a plain value receives that value straight away
return a promise waits until that promise settles, then receives its value
throw an error (or return a rejected promise) is skipped; the chain jumps to the nearest .catch

Here are all three in one runnable chain, using an order subtotal in cents:

function wait(ms: number): Promise<void> {
  return new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, ms));
}

Promise.resolve(2000)                          // start with an order subtotal in cents
  .then((subtotal) => subtotal + 599)          // rule 1: return a value -> next step gets it
  .then((total) => wait(20).then(() => total)) // rule 2: return a promise -> next step waits for it
  .then((total) => {
    console.log('total with shipping:', total);
    if (total > 2500) throw new Error('over the guest checkout limit'); // rule 3: throw -> skip to catch
    return 'charged';
  })
  .then((status) => console.log('this line is skipped:', status))
  .catch((err) => console.log('caught:', err.message))
  .then(() => console.log('a .then after .catch runs again'));
total with shipping: 2599
caught: over the guest checkout limit
a .then after .catch runs again

Promise.resolve(2000) is a shortcut for "a promise that is already fulfilled with 2000". The last line shows one more detail: .catch also returns a promise, and once it has handled the error the chain is healthy again, so a .then after it runs. That is how you "recover" from an error and continue.

Seeing the forgotten return

Now the classic bug, made visible. The customer must only be told "all done" after the receipt has been sent.

function wait(ms: number): Promise<void> {
  return new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, ms));
}

function saveOrder(): Promise<void> {
  return wait(30).then(() => console.log('order saved'));
}

function sendReceipt(): Promise<void> {
  return wait(10).then(() => console.log('receipt sent'));
}

saveOrder()
  .then(() => {
    sendReceipt();          // BUG: no return, so the chain does not wait for it
  })
  .then(() => console.log('step 3: told the customer "all done"'));
order saved
step 3: told the customer "all done"
receipt sent

Because the second step's callback returns nothing (undefined, which is rule 1, a plain value), step 3 runs at once. The receipt arrives afterwards. If sendReceipt had failed, its error would have escaped the chain entirely, because nothing was waiting on it. The fix is return sendReceipt();, or the arrow shorthand without braces: .then(() => sendReceipt()). An arrow function with no braces returns its expression automatically; with braces it returns nothing unless you write return. That one pair of braces causes this bug more often than anything else.

Promise callbacks run before timers

One ordering rule is worth learning now, because it explains outputs that otherwise look random:

console.log('1: sync');
setTimeout(() => console.log('4: timer'), 0);
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('3: promise callback'));
console.log('2: sync');
1: sync
2: sync
3: promise callback
4: timer

Synchronous code always runs first. After that, callbacks from already-settled promises run before timer callbacks, even a 0 ms timer. The beginner's version of the rule: promise callbacks go in a small priority queue that is emptied completely before the event loop moves on to timers and I/O. The proper names are the microtask queue (promises) and the macrotask or task queues (timers, I/O); Part 9.17 explains them in full. You rarely need this in day-to-day code, but you will need it the first time a test prints lines in an order you did not expect.

Combining promises

// Promise.all: run several in parallel, wait for all, fail if any fails.
const [order, inventory, pricing] = await Promise.all([
  fetchOrder(id),
  fetchInventory(id),
  fetchPricing(id),
]);
// All three ran concurrently. Total time ~= the slowest one, not the sum.

// Promise.allSettled: wait for all, never short-circuit; get each result or error.
const results = await Promise.allSettled([sendEmail(a), sendEmail(b), sendEmail(c)]);
// results[i] is { status: 'fulfilled', value } or { status: 'rejected', reason }

// Promise.race: settle as soon as the FIRST one settles (used for timeouts).
const data = await Promise.race([
  fetchFromSlowService(),
  rejectAfter(2000, new Error('timeout')),
]);

// Promise.any: resolve as soon as the first SUCCESS; useful for redundant sources.

The difference between Promise.all and Promise.allSettled matters in production. Promise.all short-circuits: the moment one promise rejects, the combined promise rejects, and you lose the other results (they still run, but you do not see their outcomes). Use Promise.all when you need every one to succeed (fetch three things to build a page — if one fails, the page fails). Use Promise.allSettled when you want all outcomes regardless (send three notifications — report which succeeded and which failed, do not abandon the rest).

Watching all and allSettled side by side

Here are the same three notifications sent both ways. SMS is "down" and fails after 10 ms; email takes 40 ms and push takes 20 ms. (Top-level await, used here, is explained in 2.4; read it as "wait for this promise".)

function send(channel: string, ms: number, fails = false): Promise<string> {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    setTimeout(() => {
      if (fails) reject(new Error(`${channel} provider is down`));
      else resolve(`${channel} delivered`);
    }, ms);
  });
}

const start = Date.now();

// Promise.all: rejects as soon as ONE input rejects.
try {
  const results = await Promise.all([send('email', 40), send('sms', 10, true), send('push', 20)]);
  console.log('all:', results);
} catch (err) {
  console.log(`all: rejected after ${Date.now() - start} ms with "${(err as Error).message}"`);
}

// Promise.allSettled: waits for every input and reports each outcome.
const settled = await Promise.allSettled([send('email', 40), send('sms', 10, true), send('push', 20)]);
for (const r of settled) {
  if (r.status === 'fulfilled') console.log('allSettled:', r.value);
  else console.log('allSettled: failed ->', r.reason.message);
}
all: rejected after 11 ms with "sms provider is down"
allSettled: email delivered
allSettled: failed -> sms provider is down
allSettled: push delivered

Promise.all gave up after 11 ms, when SMS failed. The email and push were still sent (nothing cancels them), but your code never learned that, which is how the duplicate-email incident below happened. Promise.allSettled waited for all three and told you exactly which one to retry.

Practical example — parallel vs serial

// SERIAL: three queries one after another. Total ~= sum of all three.
async function serial(id: string) {
  const a = await fetchOrder(id);        // wait 5 ms
  const b = await fetchInventory(id);    // then wait 5 ms
  const c = await fetchPricing(id);      // then wait 5 ms
  return { a, b, c };                     // ~15 ms total
}

// PARALLEL: three queries at once because they are independent. Total ~= slowest.
async function parallel(id: string) {
  const [a, b, c] = await Promise.all([
    fetchOrder(id),
    fetchInventory(id),
    fetchPricing(id),
  ]);
  return { a, b, c };                      // ~5 ms total
}

This is one of the most common real performance wins. Three independent 40 ms calls take 120 ms serially and 40 ms in parallel. On an endpoint that does several independent lookups, switching from serial awaits to Promise.all can cut latency by 3x with a one-line change. Only parallelize independent work; if step B needs step A's result, they must be serial.

Production scenario

A product-detail endpoint originally did six await calls in sequence — product, inventory, reviews, recommendations, price, shipping estimate — for a p50 of 95 ms. Four of the six were independent of each other. Grouping those four into a Promise.all and keeping the two dependent ones serial dropped p50 to 38 ms. No new infrastructure, no caching, just stopping the thread from waiting serially on things that could wait together.

Failure scenario

A notification service sent three messages with Promise.all([sms, email, push]). One day the SMS provider had an outage. Because Promise.all short-circuits on the first rejection, the whole call rejected, the error handler logged "notifications failed", and the email and push were treated as failed too even though they had actually succeeded — and worse, a retry then re-sent the email and push, so users got duplicate emails. Switching to Promise.allSettled fixed it: each channel's outcome was handled independently, only the failed SMS was retried, and the duplicates stopped. The lesson: Promise.all is for "all must succeed"; for independent best-effort work, use allSettled.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Forgetting to return the inner promise in a .then, so the chain does not wait and later steps run too early.
  • An unhandled rejection (a promise that rejects with no .catch and no surrounding try/catch) crashes the Node process in modern versions. Every promise chain needs error handling.
  • Promise.all loses sibling results on the first rejection; use allSettled when you need them all.
  • Creating promises in a loop and not awaiting them fires them all at once with no concurrency limit, which can open 10,000 database connections and exhaust the pool. For large batches, limit concurrency (a batching helper or a library like p-limit).
  • A promise never settles (a bug where resolve/reject is never called) hangs that chain forever; always have a timeout on external calls.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior sees await a; await b; await c on independent operations as a latency bug and reaches for Promise.all. They choose all vs allSettled deliberately based on whether partial success is acceptable. They never fire an unbounded number of promises in a loop against a shared resource (database, downstream service) without a concurrency limit, because that turns a batch job into a self-inflicted denial-of-service. And they put a timeout on every call that leaves the process, because a promise that waits forever on a hung dependency is how one slow service takes down yours (Part 30 — Reliability Engineering covers timeouts, retries and circuit breakers in depth).

Common mistakes

  • Serial awaits for independent work (slow endpoints).
  • Forgetting return inside .then.
  • Unhandled rejections crashing the process.
  • Unbounded Promise.all over a huge array, exhausting connections.
  • Using all where allSettled is needed, abandoning or duplicating sibling work on one failure.

Interview questions

  1. What are the three states of a promise? Pending, fulfilled, rejected; once settled it never changes.
  2. What is the difference between Promise.all and Promise.allSettled? all rejects as soon as any input rejects and loses the other results; allSettled waits for all and reports each outcome. Use all when every one must succeed, allSettled for independent best-effort work.
  3. How do you convert serial awaits into parallel execution, and when must you not? Group independent operations into Promise.all; you must keep operations serial when one depends on another's result.
  4. What happens to an unhandled promise rejection in modern Node? It crashes the process; every chain needs a .catch or a surrounding try/catch.
  5. Why is firing thousands of promises in a loop against the database dangerous? It opens all those operations at once with no concurrency limit, exhausting the connection pool and overwhelming the database.

Practical exercise

Given functions fetchUser(id), fetchOrders(id) and fetchRecommendations(id) (all independent, each ~30 ms), write a getDashboard(id) that runs them in parallel with Promise.all and returns a combined object. Then write a notifyAll(users) that sends an email to each user with Promise.allSettled, limiting concurrency to 10 at a time, and returns counts of succeeded and failed. Acceptance criteria: the dashboard completes in ~30 ms not ~90 ms, and notifyAll never has more than 10 emails in flight at once.

Worked answer to the exercise

The dashboard is a direct use of Promise.all, with pretend 30 ms lookups:

function wait(ms: number): Promise<void> {
  return new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, ms));
}

// Three independent pretend lookups, about 30 ms each.
async function fetchUser(id: string) { await wait(30); return { id, name: 'Alice' }; }
async function fetchOrders(id: string) { await wait(30); return [{ id: 'ord_1001', totalCents: 2000 }]; }
async function fetchRecommendations(id: string) { await wait(30); return ['probe-02', 'gloves-10']; }

async function getDashboard(id: string) {
  const [user, orders, recommendations] = await Promise.all([
    fetchUser(id),
    fetchOrders(id),
    fetchRecommendations(id),
  ]);
  return { user, orders, recommendations };
}

const start = Date.now();
const dashboard = await getDashboard('usr_7');
console.log(`dashboard for ${dashboard.user.name} built in ${Date.now() - start} ms`);
dashboard for Alice built in 30 ms

notifyAll is the harder half, because Promise.allSettled(users.map(sendEmail)) would start all the emails at once. The trick is a small pool of "workers": start 10 of them, and let each one keep taking the next user from a shared list until the list is empty. At most 10 are ever busy.

function wait(ms: number): Promise<void> {
  return new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, ms));
}

let inFlight = 0;
let maxInFlight = 0;

// A pretend email provider: 20 ms per email, and every 7th address bounces.
async function sendEmail(userId: number): Promise<void> {
  inFlight++;
  maxInFlight = Math.max(maxInFlight, inFlight);
  try {
    await wait(20);
    if (userId % 7 === 0) throw new Error(`mailbox for user ${userId} is full`);
  } finally {
    inFlight--;
  }
}

// Run `task` for every item, but never more than `limit` at the same time.
async function mapWithLimit<T, R>(
  items: T[],
  limit: number,
  task: (item: T) => Promise<R>,
): Promise<PromiseSettledResult<R>[]> {
  const results: PromiseSettledResult<R>[] = new Array(items.length);
  let next = 0;

  async function worker(): Promise<void> {
    while (next < items.length) {
      const index = next++;               // safe: no await between the check and the ++
      try {
        results[index] = { status: 'fulfilled', value: await task(items[index]) };
      } catch (reason) {
        results[index] = { status: 'rejected', reason };
      }
    }
  }

  // Start `limit` workers; each one keeps pulling the next item until none are left.
  await Promise.all(Array.from({ length: limit }, () => worker()));
  return results;
}

async function notifyAll(userIds: number[]) {
  const results = await mapWithLimit(userIds, 10, sendEmail);
  const succeeded = results.filter((r) => r.status === 'fulfilled').length;
  return { succeeded, failed: results.length - succeeded };
}

const start = Date.now();
const userIds = Array.from({ length: 100 }, (_, i) => i + 1);
const summary = await notifyAll(userIds);
console.log(summary, `max in flight: ${maxInFlight}`, `took ${Date.now() - start} ms`);
{ succeeded: 86, failed: 14 } max in flight: 10 took 209 ms

Check it against the acceptance criteria. 100 emails at 20 ms each, 10 at a time, is 10 rounds of about 20 ms, so roughly 200 ms; we measured 209 ms. The counter proves no more than 10 were ever in flight. The 14 failures (every 7th user) did not stop the other 86. Why is next++ safe when ten workers share it? Because JavaScript runs one piece of code at a time and there is no await between reading next and increasing it, so no other worker can sneak in between. In production you would usually reach for a small library such as p-limit instead of writing this yourself, but now you know what it does inside.

Try it yourself

1. When does the executor run?

const p = new Promise<string>((resolve) => {
  console.log('A: inside the executor');
  resolve('B: resolved value');
});
p.then((value) => console.log(value));
console.log('C: after .then');

2. Why undefined?

Promise.resolve(3)
  .then((qty) => { qty * 1299; })
  .then((total) => console.log('total:', total));

3. How long does each half take?

function wait(ms: number): Promise<void> {
  return new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, ms));
}

let start = Date.now();
await Promise.all([wait(100), wait(50), wait(80)]);
console.log(`together: ${Date.now() - start} ms`);

start = Date.now();
await wait(100);
await wait(50);
await wait(80);
console.log(`one after another: ${Date.now() - start} ms`);

Answers.

  1. A: inside the executor, C: after .then, B: resolved value. The executor runs immediately and synchronously, inside new Promise(...). Only the .then callback is delayed, and it always runs after the current synchronous code, even when the promise is already resolved.
  2. It prints total: undefined. The arrow function has braces, so it needs an explicit return. Without one it returns undefined, and rule 1 passes undefined to the next step. Write (qty) => qty * 1299 or (qty) => { return qty * 1299; }.
  3. together: 100 ms and one after another: 233 ms. Together takes as long as the slowest (100 ms). One after another takes the sum (100 + 50 + 80 = 230 ms, plus a few milliseconds of timer overhead).

2.4 Async and await

2.4.1 Writing asynchronous code that reads like synchronous code

Concept

async/await is syntax built on top of promises. An async function always returns a promise. Inside it, await pauses the function until a promise settles, then gives you the value — or throws if it rejected. The result is asynchronous code that reads top to bottom like ordinary synchronous code, with normal try/catch for errors.

Why does it exist?

Even with promises, .then chains get awkward for anything with conditionals, loops or several intermediate values. async/await lets you write the same logic as plain-looking sequential code. It is not a new mechanism — under the hood it is still promises and the event loop — it is a much more readable way to write them. Today it is the default for all asynchronous backend code; .then/.catch is reserved for a few specific spots.

Mental model

await is a bookmark. When your function hits await someSlowThing(), it places a bookmark, steps away so the one thread can serve other work, and comes back to exactly that line when the result is ready — picking up with the value in hand. The function appears to "wait", but the thread does not; only this particular function is paused, bookmarked, while everyone else carries on.

How it works

// The same order flow from the promises section, now with async/await.
async function processOrder(orderId: string): Promise<void> {
  const order = await fetchOrder(orderId);          // pause until order arrives
  const customer = await fetchCustomer(order.customerId); // then until customer
  await sendReceipt(customer.email);                // then until receipt sent
  console.log('done');
}

Compare this to the .then chain in 2.3 and the callback pyramid in 2.2 — same logic, progressively more readable. Three rules:

  1. await can only be used inside an async function (or at the top level of a module in modern Node).
  2. An async function always returns a promise, even if you return a plain value — return 5 becomes Promise<number>.
  3. await unwraps the promise: const x = await fetchOrder(id) gives you the Order, not a Promise<Order>.

Step-by-step trace: what await really pauses

The phrase "await pauses" confuses beginners, because it sounds like the whole program stops. It does not. Run this:

function wait(ms: number): Promise<void> {
  return new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, ms));
}

async function handleRequest(name: string, queryMs: number): Promise<string> {
  console.log(`${name}: start, sending query`);
  await wait(queryMs);                       // bookmark: this function pauses here
  console.log(`${name}: query done after ${queryMs} ms`);
  return `${name} response`;
}

const first = handleRequest('req 1', 30);    // runs until its first await, then returns a promise
const second = handleRequest('req 2', 10);
console.log('both handlers started; the thread is free');

console.log(await first, '|', await second);
req 1: start, sending query
req 2: start, sending query
both handlers started; the thread is free
req 2: query done after 10 ms
req 1: query done after 30 ms
req 1 response | req 2 response

Trace it line by line:

  1. handleRequest('req 1', 30) starts running synchronously. It prints its first line, reaches await, and pauses. At that moment the call returns a pending promise, which we store in first.
  2. handleRequest('req 2', 10) does the same: prints, pauses, returns a promise.
  3. The top-level code carries on and prints both handlers started. Two functions are paused, and the thread is free.
  4. At 10 ms request 2's wait finishes. Request 2 resumes from its bookmark, prints query done, and returns. Its promise fulfils.
  5. At 30 ms request 1 resumes the same way.
  6. The final line was waiting on both promises, so it prints last.

This is exactly how a NestJS server handles two requests at once: each handler is an async function, each await on a query is a bookmark, and the one thread hops between whichever handler has work ready.

Error handling with try/catch

The big win: asynchronous errors are caught by ordinary try/catch, which callbacks could not do.

async function processOrder(orderId: string): Promise<void> {
  try {
    const order = await fetchOrder(orderId);
    const customer = await fetchCustomer(order.customerId);
    await sendReceipt(customer.email);
  } catch (err) {
    // Catches a rejection from ANY of the awaited calls above.
    console.error('order processing failed:', (err as Error).message);
    throw err;                      // rethrow if the caller must know
  }
}

One try/catch covers every await inside it, just as one .catch covered the whole chain. In TypeScript the caught value is typed unknown (since anything can be thrown), so you narrow it with err instanceof Error before reading .message.

Parallelism with async/await

await is sequential by default — each one waits for the previous. For independent work, combine await with Promise.all, exactly as before.

async function getDashboard(userId: string) {
  // WRONG for independent work: three sequential waits.
  // const user = await fetchUser(userId);
  // const orders = await fetchOrders(userId);
  // const recs = await fetchRecommendations(userId);

  // RIGHT: start all three, then await them together.
  const [user, orders, recs] = await Promise.all([
    fetchUser(userId),
    fetchOrders(userId),
    fetchRecommendations(userId),
  ]);
  return { user, orders, recs };
}

Writing await three times in a row is the most common beginner performance mistake. await does not mean "do these in parallel" — it means "wait here". Independent work needs Promise.all.

Practical example — NestJS service method

Every NestJS service method you write will look like this: an async method that awaits a repository and handles errors.

import { Injectable, NotFoundException } from '@nestjs/common';
import { InjectRepository } from '@nestjs/typeorm';
import { Repository } from 'typeorm';
import { Order } from './order.entity';

@Injectable()
export class OrdersService {
  constructor(@InjectRepository(Order) private readonly repo: Repository<Order>) {}

  async findOne(id: string): Promise<Order> {
    const order = await this.repo.findOne({ where: { id } });
    if (!order) throw new NotFoundException(`Order ${id} not found`);
    return order;
  }

  async summary(userId: string) {
    // Independent queries run together.
    const [open, completed] = await Promise.all([
      this.repo.count({ where: { userId, status: 'pending' } }),
      this.repo.count({ where: { userId, status: 'delivered' } }),
    ]);
    return { open, completed };
  }
}

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Serial await for independent work (the main performance trap).
  • await inside Array.forEach does not work — forEach ignores the returned promise, so the loop does not wait. Use a for...of loop (serial) or Promise.all(map(...)) (parallel).
  • Forgetting await on a promise-returning call means the function continues before the work finishes, and any error becomes an unhandled rejection. TypeScript with the right lint rule flags "floating promises".
  • Awaiting in a loop over a large array runs serially and can be very slow; parallelize with a concurrency limit if the work is independent.
  • A missing return await inside try/catch: if you write return somePromise() instead of return await somePromise(), a rejection escapes the try/catch because the function has already returned. Use return await when you want the catch to handle it.

Seeing three traps

Trap 1: await inside forEach. An order has three lines to save.

function wait(ms: number): Promise<void> {
  return new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, ms));
}

async function saveLine(sku: string): Promise<void> {
  await wait(10);
  console.log(`  saved ${sku}`);
}

const skus = ['mirror-01', 'probe-02', 'gloves-10'];

console.log('forEach version:');
skus.forEach(async (sku) => {
  await saveLine(sku);
});
console.log('  "all lines saved" (a lie: nothing is saved yet)');

await wait(50); // let the forEach work finish before the next demo

console.log('for...of version:');
for (const sku of skus) {
  await saveLine(sku);
}
console.log('  all lines saved (true this time)');
forEach version:
  "all lines saved" (a lie: nothing is saved yet)
  saved mirror-01
  saved probe-02
  saved gloves-10
for...of version:
  saved mirror-01
  saved probe-02
  saved gloves-10
  all lines saved (true this time)

forEach calls your async callback three times and throws away the three promises it gets back. It cannot wait for something it ignores. So the "all saved" line runs first, and in a real handler the HTTP response would go out before the rows were written. If one save failed, nothing would catch it.

Trap 2: return without await inside try.

async function chargeCard(): Promise<string> {
  throw new Error('card declined');
}

async function withoutAwait(): Promise<string> {
  try {
    return chargeCard();               // returns the promise; the try block is already over
  } catch {
    return 'fallback: ask for another card';
  }
}

async function withAwait(): Promise<string> {
  try {
    return await chargeCard();         // waits here, so a rejection lands in the catch
  } catch {
    return 'fallback: ask for another card';
  }
}

console.log('with await   ->', await withAwait());
try {
  console.log('without await ->', await withoutAwait());
} catch (err) {
  console.log('without await -> escaped the catch:', (err as Error).message);
}
with await   -> fallback: ask for another card
without await -> escaped the catch: card declined

Without await, the function hands back the promise and leaves the try block before the promise rejects. The rejection then travels to the caller, skipping your fallback.

Trap 3: a floating promise. The order is placed, and an audit log write is "fired and forgotten".

async function writeAuditLog(orderId: string): Promise<void> {
  await new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, 10));
  throw new Error(`audit table is full, could not log ${orderId}`);
}

async function placeOrder(orderId: string): Promise<string> {
  writeAuditLog(orderId);              // BUG: floating promise, nobody awaits it
  return `order ${orderId} placed`;
}

try {
  console.log(await placeOrder('ord_1001'));
} catch (err) {
  console.log('caught:', (err as Error).message); // never runs
}
console.log('request finished');
order ord_1001 placed
request finished
file:///.../floating.ts:3
  throw new Error(`audit table is full, could not log ${orderId}`);
        ^

Error: audit table is full, could not log ord_1001

The request "succeeded", the try/catch caught nothing, and 10 ms later the rejected promise had no handler, so Node treated it as an unhandled rejection and stopped the process, killing every other in-flight request too. The fix is await writeAuditLog(orderId), or, for deliberate background work, writeAuditLog(orderId).catch((err) => logger.error(err)). The @typescript-eslint/no-floating-promises lint rule catches this at build time.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior writes async/await as the default and uses Promise.all the instant two awaited operations are independent. They never use await inside forEach. They enable the "no floating promises" lint rule so a forgotten await is caught at build time rather than becoming a 2 a.m. unhandled rejection. They put try/catch where they can actually do something useful with the error (translate it, add context, decide to retry) and let it propagate otherwise, because catching an error only to re-log and rethrow it at every level just produces noise (Part 42 — Observability covers logging that helps).

Common mistakes

  • Serial awaits for independent work.
  • await inside forEach (silently does nothing useful).
  • Forgetting await, creating a floating promise and an unhandled rejection.
  • Wrapping everything in try/catch and swallowing errors instead of handling or propagating them.

Interview questions

  1. What does an async function return? Always a promise, even if the body returns a plain value.
  2. Why does await inside forEach not work? forEach ignores the promise returned by its callback, so it does not wait; use for...of or Promise.all(map(...)).
  3. How do you run two independent awaited calls concurrently? const [a, b] = await Promise.all([callA(), callB()]).
  4. What is a floating promise and why is it dangerous? A promise-returning call with no await and no .catch; its errors become unhandled rejections and its completion is not awaited, causing race conditions and crashes.

Practical exercise

Convert the callback-based config-then-data flow from 2.2 into async/await with a single try/catch. Then write a loadAll(paths: string[]) that reads many files; provide two versions — one serial with for...of, one parallel with Promise.all — and measure the difference. Acceptance criteria: the parallel version is dramatically faster for many files, and both handle a missing file with a clear error.

Worked answer to the exercise

First, the config-then-data flow from 2.2, now with node:fs/promises and one try/catch:

import { readFile } from 'node:fs/promises';

async function loadData(configPath: string): Promise<unknown> {
  try {
    const config = JSON.parse(await readFile(configPath, 'utf8'));
    const data = JSON.parse(await readFile(config.dataPath, 'utf8'));
    return data;
  } catch (err) {
    throw new Error(`could not load data via ${configPath}: ${(err as Error).message}`);
  }
}

console.log(await loadData('config.json'));
try {
  await loadData('missing-config.json');
} catch (err) {
  console.log((err as Error).message);
}
{ orders: 3 }
could not load data via missing-config.json: ENOENT: no such file or directory, open 'missing-config.json'

(To run it, create config.json containing {"dataPath":"data.json"} and data.json containing {"orders":3}.) Compare it with the callback version: no nesting, one place for errors, and the error message now says which step failed. That is the "add context" kind of catch a senior writes.

Second, loadAll, run against 500 small invoice files:

import { readFile } from 'node:fs/promises';

async function loadAllSerial(paths: string[]): Promise<string[]> {
  const contents: string[] = [];
  for (const path of paths) {
    contents.push(await readFile(path, 'utf8'));     // one file at a time
  }
  return contents;
}

async function loadAllParallel(paths: string[]): Promise<string[]> {
  return Promise.all(paths.map((path) => readFile(path, 'utf8'))); // all at once
}

const paths = Array.from({ length: 500 }, (_, i) => `invoices/inv_${i + 1}.json`);

let start = performance.now();
await loadAllSerial(paths);
console.log(`serial:   ${(performance.now() - start).toFixed(1)} ms for ${paths.length} files`);

start = performance.now();
await loadAllParallel(paths);
console.log(`parallel: ${(performance.now() - start).toFixed(1)} ms for ${paths.length} files`);

try {
  await loadAllParallel([...paths.slice(0, 3), 'invoices/inv_9999.json']);
} catch (err) {
  console.log('missing file:', (err as Error).message);
}
serial:   10.9 ms for 500 files
parallel: 3.3 ms for 500 files
missing file: ENOENT: no such file or directory, open 'invoices/inv_9999.json'

On a laptop SSD the parallel version was about 3 to 4 times faster, not 500 times: local reads are quick, and Node reads files with a small pool of 4 helper threads (Part 9.18), so only a few truly overlap. With network calls of 30 to 100 ms each, the gap is far bigger. And remember 2.3: Promise.all over a huge list against a shared service needs a concurrency limit.

2.5 Error handling and pitfalls in asynchronous code

2.5.1 Errors, timeouts and cancellation

Concept

Asynchronous code fails in ways synchronous code does not: the thing you are waiting for can be slow, can hang forever, can fail, or can succeed after you have given up. Robust asynchronous code plans for all of these. The three tools are: proper error propagation (try/catch around awaits), timeouts (never wait forever on the network), and cancellation (AbortController) to stop work you no longer need.

Why does it exist?

Without a timeout, one hung downstream service makes every request to your endpoint hang too, until your own connection pool and thread capacity are exhausted and your whole service goes down — a cascading failure. Without cancellation, when a user navigates away or a request times out, you keep doing expensive work for a result nobody will read. These are not edge cases; they are how real outages spread.

How it works

Timeout on any promise using Promise.race or, in modern Node, AbortSignal.timeout:

// Generic timeout wrapper.
function withTimeout<T>(promise: Promise<T>, ms: number): Promise<T> {
  let timer: NodeJS.Timeout | undefined;
  const timeout = new Promise<never>((_, reject) => {
    timer = setTimeout(() => reject(new Error(`timed out after ${ms}ms`)), ms);
  });
  // Whichever settles first wins; either way, clear the timer so it does not linger.
  return Promise.race([promise, timeout]).finally(() => clearTimeout(timer));
}

const order = await withTimeout(fetchOrder(id), 2000); // fail if it takes > 2s

Here it is running, with wait(ms) standing in for slow work:

function withTimeout<T>(promise: Promise<T>, ms: number): Promise<T> {
  let timer: NodeJS.Timeout | undefined;
  const timeout = new Promise<never>((_, reject) => {
    timer = setTimeout(() => reject(new Error(`timed out after ${ms}ms`)), ms);
  });
  return Promise.race([promise, timeout]).finally(() => clearTimeout(timer));
}

function wait(ms: number): Promise<void> {
  return new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, ms));
}

const start = Date.now();
console.log(await withTimeout(wait(50).then(() => 'price: 1299'), 2000));
try {
  await withTimeout(wait(5000).then(() => 'too late'), 100);
} catch (err) {
  console.log((err as Error).message);
}
console.log(`script done after ${Date.now() - start} ms`);
price: 1299
timed out after 100ms
script done after 153 ms

The first call finished in 50 ms; the second gave up after 100 ms, as asked. But the script does not exit until about 5 seconds have passed: the 5-second "work" is still running, because withTimeout only stopped us from waiting. Keep that in mind for the next paragraph.

Cancellation with AbortController, the standard way to cancel fetch and many Node APIs:

const controller = new AbortController();
const timer = setTimeout(() => controller.abort(), 2000); // cancel after 2s

try {
  const res = await fetch('https://pricing.internal/quote', {
    signal: controller.signal,      // fetch stops if aborted
  });
  const data = await res.json();
} catch (err) {
  if ((err as Error).name === 'AbortError') console.error('request cancelled');
  else throw err;                   // never silently swallow other failures
} finally {
  clearTimeout(timer);              // always clean up the timer
}

The difference between the two: a timeout wrapper makes your code stop waiting, but the underlying work may keep running and finish later (wasting resources). AbortController actually cancels the underlying work (closes the socket, stops the fetch). Prefer real cancellation where the API supports it.

A real timeout against a hung server

You can watch real cancellation work on your own machine. The script below starts a tiny HTTP server that accepts requests and never answers, which is exactly what the hung pricing service in the production scenario did. Then it calls it with AbortSignal.timeout(500), the one-line shortcut for "an AbortController that aborts itself after 500 ms".

import { createServer } from 'node:http';

// A pretend pricing service that accepts the request and never answers (a "hung" dependency).
const hungServer = createServer(() => {
  /* never call res.end() */
});
await new Promise<void>((resolve) => hungServer.listen(4010, resolve));

const start = Date.now();
try {
  const res = await fetch('http://localhost:4010/quote?productId=42', {
    signal: AbortSignal.timeout(500),   // give up AND cancel the request after 500 ms
  });
  console.log('price:', await res.json());
} catch (err) {
  const e = err as Error;
  console.log(`gave up after ${Date.now() - start} ms: ${e.name}: ${e.message}`);
} finally {
  hungServer.closeAllConnections();
  hungServer.close();
}
gave up after 507 ms: TimeoutError: The operation was aborted due to timeout

Notice the error's name. When you call controller.abort() yourself, fetch rejects with an error named AbortError. When AbortSignal.timeout fires, the name is TimeoutError. If your code checks err.name === 'AbortError' only, it will miss timeouts, which is a common beginner bug. Check for both, or check signal.aborted.

Why the timer in withTimeout must be cleared

Many tutorials show a shorter withTimeout without the .finally(() => clearTimeout(timer)). It still returns the right answer, but the timer stays alive after the race is won: a script that got its answer in 1 ms cannot exit until the 2-second timer fires, and a server handling 1,000 requests per second with a 2-second timeout carries about 2,000 useless timers at any moment. Always clean up the timer, whichever side wins.

The result-type alternative to throwing

For expected failures (a payment declined, a validation error), some teams return a discriminated union (a Result type, Part 129.7.2) instead of throwing, reserving exceptions for truly exceptional situations. Both approaches are valid; the key is consistency.

type Result<T> = { ok: true; value: T } | { ok: false; error: string };

async function charge(amountCents: number): Promise<Result<string>> {
  try {
    const chargeId = await provider.charge(amountCents);
    return { ok: true, value: chargeId };
  } catch (err) {
    return { ok: false, error: (err as Error).message };
  }
}

Practical example — retry with backoff and timeout

Putting it together: call a flaky service with a per-attempt timeout and exponential backoff between attempts. This is the shape of nearly every resilient external call (Part 30 — Reliability Engineering goes deeper, especially 30.2 to 30.4).

async function sleep(ms: number) {
  return new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, ms));
}

async function callWithRetry<T>(
  fn: () => Promise<T>,
  attempts = 3,
  perAttemptMs = 2000,
): Promise<T> {
  let lastError: unknown;
  for (let i = 0; i < attempts; i++) {
    try {
      return await withTimeout(fn(), perAttemptMs);
    } catch (err) {
      lastError = err;
      if (i < attempts - 1) {
        // exponential backoff with jitter: 200ms, 400ms, 800ms (plus randomness)
        await sleep(200 * 2 ** i + Math.random() * 100);
      }
    }
  }
  throw lastError;
}

Production scenario

A checkout endpoint calls a pricing service. One afternoon the pricing service deployed a bad build and started hanging — accepting connections but never responding. The checkout code had no timeout, so every checkout request hung too. Within four minutes, thousands of checkout requests were stuck waiting, holding their sockets, memory and database connections, and checkout went fully down even though its own code and database were healthy. The post-incident fix was a 1.5-second timeout on the pricing call with a sensible fallback (use the last cached price and flag the order for review). After the fix, a pricing outage degrades one feature for 1.5 seconds per request instead of taking down checkout entirely.

Failure scenario

A search endpoint fired a database query on every keystroke. Users typing fast generated bursts of requests; the late ones returned after the early ones, so results flickered and sometimes showed stale matches for a query the user had already changed. The fix was an AbortController per request: when a new keystroke arrived, the previous in-flight query was aborted, so only the latest query's results ever rendered. Cancellation is not only about saving resources; it is sometimes about correctness.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • No timeout on an external call; one hung dependency cascades into your whole service going down.
  • Timeout without cancellation, so the underlying work keeps running and you waste resources even after giving up.
  • Swallowing errors (an empty catch), hiding failures until they cause a bigger, more confusing problem later.
  • Catching and rethrowing the same error at every level, producing noise with no added context.
  • Not cleaning up timers or listeners in a finally, leaking them.
  • Race conditions from out-of-order responses (the search flicker) when a later-but-stale response overwrites a newer one.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior puts a timeout on every call that leaves the process, full stop — the network is never trusted to respond. They prefer real cancellation (AbortController) over bare timeouts so abandoned work actually stops. They distinguish expected failures (declined payment, not found) from unexpected ones (database down) and handle each appropriately — expected failures become clean responses, unexpected ones are logged with context and surfaced as 5xx. They never write an empty catch; an error that is truly safe to ignore gets a comment saying why. And they think about out-of-order responses whenever a client can issue overlapping requests.

Common mistakes

  • No timeout on network calls; the classic cause of cascading outages.
  • Empty catch blocks that swallow errors silently.
  • Treating all errors the same instead of separating expected from unexpected.
  • Not cancelling superseded requests, causing flicker or wasted work.
  • Forgetting clearTimeout/cleanup in finally.

Interview questions

  1. Why must every external call have a timeout? A hung dependency with no timeout makes your requests hang too, exhausting threads and connections until your service goes down — a cascading failure.
  2. What is the difference between a timeout wrapper and AbortController? A timeout wrapper stops your code from waiting but the underlying work may keep running; AbortController actually cancels the underlying operation.
  3. When would you return a Result type instead of throwing? For expected, routine failures (declined payment, validation error) where throwing is heavy-handed; reserve exceptions for exceptional situations. Consistency across the codebase matters most.
  4. What is wrong with an empty catch block? It swallows the error silently, hiding the failure until it causes a larger, harder-to-diagnose problem later.
  5. How do out-of-order async responses cause bugs, and how do you fix them? A slower earlier request can resolve after a newer one and overwrite fresh data; cancel superseded requests with AbortController or ignore stale responses by sequence number.

Practical exercise

Build a resilient fetchPrice(productId) that calls a mock pricing function with a 1-second per-attempt timeout, up to 3 attempts with exponential backoff, and falls back to a cached price if all attempts fail. Then add an AbortController so that calling fetchPrice again for the same product cancels the previous in-flight call. Acceptance criteria: a hung pricing function never blocks the caller longer than ~3 seconds total, and only the latest call's result is used.

A complete, runnable answer is worked through step by step in 2.6.

2.6 Worked example: a resilient price lookup, step by step

2.6.1 Building fetchPrice from the 2.5 exercise

Concept

This section is the full answer to the 2.5 practical exercise. It pulls together almost everything in this Part: async/await, try/catch, retries with backoff, a timeout per attempt, a cached fallback, and AbortController to cancel an older call when a newer one arrives. If you can explain every line, you understand asynchronous JavaScript well enough to write real backend code.

Practical example

The requirements, restated:

  1. Each attempt to call the pricing service may take at most 1 second.
  2. Make up to 3 attempts, waiting a little longer between each (backoff).
  3. If every attempt fails, use the last known price from a cache.
  4. If fetchPrice is called again for the same product while an older call is still running, cancel the older one. Only the newest answer counts.

A mock pricing service lets the script run anywhere. It can be healthy (80 ms), slow (500 ms) or hung (a full minute, which to a user is never). Like fetch, it gives up the moment its signal aborts.

type Quote = { productId: string; priceCents: number; source: 'live' | 'cache' };

const lastKnownPrice = new Map<string, number>([['42', 1299]]); // a tiny cache
const inFlight = new Map<string, AbortController>();            // one live call per product
let pricingMode: 'healthy' | 'slow' | 'hung' = 'hung';

// A mock pricing service. Like fetch, it stops when its signal aborts.
function callPricingService(productId: string, signal: AbortSignal): Promise<number> {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    // healthy: 80 ms, slow: 500 ms, hung: a full minute (as good as never)
    const delay = pricingMode === 'healthy' ? 80 : pricingMode === 'slow' ? 500 : 60_000;
    const timer = setTimeout(() => resolve(1349), delay);
    signal.addEventListener('abort', () => {
      clearTimeout(timer);
      reject(signal.reason);
    }, { once: true });
  });
}

function sleep(ms: number): Promise<void> {
  return new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, ms));
}

Now the function itself. Read the three numbered comments first; they match the requirements.

async function fetchPrice(productId: string): Promise<Quote> {
  // Step 1: cancel any older call for the same product; only the newest one matters.
  inFlight.get(productId)?.abort(new Error('superseded by a newer call'));
  const controller = new AbortController();
  inFlight.set(productId, controller);

  try {
    // Step 2: up to 3 attempts, each limited to 1 second.
    for (let attempt = 1; attempt <= 3; attempt++) {
      controller.signal.throwIfAborted();          // superseded while we slept? stop now
      try {
        const signal = AbortSignal.any([controller.signal, AbortSignal.timeout(1000)]);
        const priceCents = await callPricingService(productId, signal);
        lastKnownPrice.set(productId, priceCents);
        return { productId, priceCents, source: 'live' };
      } catch (err) {
        if (controller.signal.aborted) throw err;  // superseded: do not retry
        console.log(`  attempt ${attempt} failed: ${(err as Error).name}`);
        if (attempt < 3) await sleep(100 * 2 ** (attempt - 1)); // backoff: 100 ms, then 200 ms
      }
    }
    // Step 3: every attempt failed; fall back to the last known price.
    const cached = lastKnownPrice.get(productId);
    if (cached === undefined) throw new Error(`no price available for product ${productId}`);
    return { productId, priceCents: cached, source: 'cache' };
  } finally {
    if (inFlight.get(productId) === controller) inFlight.delete(productId);
  }
}

Finally, three scenarios that exercise every path (paste them at the bottom of the same file):

// Scenario 1: the pricing service hangs.
let start = Date.now();
console.log('scenario 1: pricing service hung');
console.log(await fetchPrice('42'), `after ${Date.now() - start} ms`);

// Scenario 2: the pricing service is healthy.
pricingMode = 'healthy';
start = Date.now();
console.log('scenario 2: pricing service healthy');
console.log(await fetchPrice('42'), `after ${Date.now() - start} ms`);

// Scenario 3: a slow call is superseded by a newer call for the same product.
pricingMode = 'slow';
start = Date.now();
console.log('scenario 3: a newer call supersedes an older one');
const older = fetchPrice('42');
await sleep(100);
