const newer = fetchPrice('42');
const [olderResult, newerResult] = await Promise.allSettled([older, newer]);
console.log('  older:', olderResult.status === 'rejected' ? olderResult.reason.message : olderResult.value);
console.log('  newer:', newerResult.status === 'fulfilled' ? newerResult.value : newerResult.reason);
console.log(`  after ${Date.now() - start} ms`);
scenario 1: pricing service hung
  attempt 1 failed: TimeoutError
  attempt 2 failed: TimeoutError
  attempt 3 failed: TimeoutError
{ productId: '42', priceCents: 1299, source: 'cache' } after 3307 ms
scenario 2: pricing service healthy
{ productId: '42', priceCents: 1349, source: 'live' } after 82 ms
scenario 3: a newer call supersedes an older one
  older: superseded by a newer call
  newer: { productId: '42', priceCents: 1349, source: 'live' }
  after 602 ms

How it works

Trace scenario 1, the hung service, through the code:

  1. No older call exists, so inFlight.get('42')?.abort(...) does nothing. (?. means "only call this if the left side is not undefined".) We create a controller and remember it.
  2. Attempt 1. AbortSignal.any([...]) builds one signal that aborts when either our controller aborts or 1 second passes. After 1 second the timeout fires and the mock rejects with a TimeoutError.
  3. The catch checks whether we were superseded. We were not, so it logs the failure and sleeps 100 ms.
  4. Attempt 2 fails the same way after 1 second; we sleep 200 ms. Attempt 3 fails; there is no sleep after the last attempt.
  5. The loop ends. The cache has 1299 for product 42, so we return it, marked source: 'cache'.
  6. finally removes our controller from inFlight. Total: 1000 + 100 + 1000 + 200 + 1000 = 3,300 ms, and we measured 3,307 ms. That meets "never longer than about 3 seconds".

Scenario 3, the superseded call:

  1. The older call starts and waits on a 500 ms answer.
  2. 100 ms later the newer call starts, and its first line aborts the older call's controller with the reason "superseded by a newer call".
  3. The older call's mock rejects with that reason. The catch sees controller.signal.aborted and rethrows instead of retrying; retrying a deliberate cancellation would defeat the point.
  4. The older call's finally sees that inFlight now holds the newer controller and leaves it alone. Without that === check, the old call would delete the new call's entry.
  5. The newer call answers 500 ms after it started, about 600 ms into the scenario.

Production scenario

This exact shape protects a real checkout page. When the pricing service (normally about 40 ms) is down, checkout still works with the last known price and the order is flagged for review. When a shopper changes the quantity three times in a second, only the last price request survives. Part 30 — Reliability Engineering takes the same ideas further: deadlines across several calls, retry budgets, and circuit breakers.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Retrying non-retryable errors. A 404 product not found will not get better on attempt 2. A real version only retries timeouts, network errors and 5xx responses.
  • Retrying writes. Retrying a price read is safe. Retrying "charge the card" is not, unless the call carries an idempotency key (Part 29 — Distributed Transactions).
  • Stale cache. A week-old fallback price may be wrong. Real systems store the time the price was cached and refuse very old ones.
  • Unref'd timers. AbortSignal.timeout does not keep the process alive by itself. A server keeps itself alive, but a script whose only pending work is such a timeout exits early with a "Detected unsettled top-level await" warning, which is why the mock's "hung" mode uses a real one-minute timer.

Practical exercise

Change fetchPrice so it only retries on TimeoutError and gives up at once on any other error. Then add a maxAgeMs option to the cache: a cached price older than 10 minutes must not be used. Acceptance criteria: a mock that rejects with new Error('product not found') causes exactly one attempt, and a cached price stored 11 minutes ago is rejected with a clear error.

2.7 Async debugging guide: symptom, cause, fix

2.7.1 Reading the clues

Concept

Asynchronous bugs rarely announce themselves. You see a symptom, such as "the response came back before the data was saved", and work back to the cause. A handful of causes explain almost every beginner async bug; learn their symptoms and you will fix in minutes what used to take an afternoon.

How it works

Symptom you see Most likely cause Fix Where in this Part
A value is Promise { <pending> } where you expected data Missing await await the call 2.3, 2.4
"All done" is logged before the work finishes await inside forEach, or a .then that does not return for...of or Promise.all(map(...)); return the inner promise 2.3, 2.4
A .then step receives undefined Arrow function with braces and no return Add return, or drop the braces 2.3
The process exits with an error thrown from your code, but your try/catch "should" have caught it Thrown inside a callback, or a floating promise rejected Pass errors to the callback; await or .catch every promise 2.2, 2.4
The catch with a fallback does not run return promise instead of return await promise inside try return await 2.4
Every request slows down whenever one heavy request runs Synchronous CPU work blocking the event loop Move it to a worker thread or background job; stream big files 2.1
One slow dependency makes the whole service hang No timeout on an external call AbortSignal.timeout(ms) or withTimeout 2.5
Duplicate emails or charges after a partial failure Promise.all used for best-effort work, then retried Promise.allSettled; retry only the failures 2.3
"Too many connections" during a batch job Unbounded Promise.all over a huge list A concurrency limit (mapWithLimit, p-limit) 2.3
Screen shows results for an old search Out-of-order responses Cancel superseded calls with AbortController 2.5, 2.6
The callback's success path runs after an error Missing return after callback(err) return callback(err) 2.2

Practical example

A teammate's endpoint returns {"order": {}} instead of the order. Here is the code:

async findOne(id: string) {
  const order = this.repo.findOne({ where: { id } });
  return { order };
}

Use the table. order is really a pending promise, and JSON.stringify turns a Promise into {} because it has no plain data properties. The cause is the missing await before this.repo.findOne(...); the fix is one word. The lint rule @typescript-eslint/require-await flags this async function with no await in it, and a senior turns such rules on from day one.

Try it yourself

Name the cause and the fix for each, using the table.

  1. A test prints saved: 0 rows and then, a moment later, three INSERT log lines.
  2. A script logs TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'email') inside a callback right after it logged user lookup failed.
  3. During a flash sale, product pages, login and checkout all slow to 900 ms, and the slowdown matches the times a merchant uploads a 40 MB spreadsheet.

Answers.

  1. The rows are saved inside a forEach(async ...) (or a .then that does not return), so the count is read before the inserts finish. Use for...of with await, or await Promise.all(rows.map(save)).
  2. The callback logged the error but did not return, so it carried on and used the missing user. Write if (err) return console.error(...).
  3. The spreadsheet is parsed synchronously on the main thread, blocking the event loop for everyone. Parse it in a worker thread (Part 9.20) or a background job (Part 35), and stream it instead of loading 40 MB at once.

Senior Checklist — Part 2

  • I understand the concept
  • I can explain it simply
  • I can implement it
  • I understand its failure modes
  • I understand its scaling limitations
  • I understand its security implications
  • I understand its operational implications
  • I can choose between alternatives
  • I can explain the tradeoffs
  • I can answer senior-level interview questions
  • I can explain why one thread can serve thousands of concurrent requests
  • I can trace a small async script line by line and predict its output before running it
  • I never do heavy CPU work in a request handler (it blocks everyone)
  • I can spot a missing return and a double callback in callback code, and I promisify callback APIs at the edge
  • I know the three rules of .then (return a value, return a promise, throw)
  • I know that promise callbacks run before timer callbacks
  • I use async/await by default and know it returns a promise
  • I use Promise.all for independent work and allSettled for best-effort work
  • I put a concurrency limit on large batches against shared resources
  • I never use await inside forEach, and I use return await inside try/catch
  • I put a timeout on every call that leaves the process, and I clear my timers
  • I prefer AbortController for real cancellation of abandoned work
  • I never write an empty catch block

Part 2 — Key Takeaways

  • Node runs your JavaScript on one thread and never blocks it on I/O; a long synchronous computation blocks every request.
  • setTimeout(fn, 0) runs after the current synchronous code, not immediately — "async" means "later when the thread is free", and a timer's delay is a minimum, not a guarantee.
  • Predict the output before you run it: tracing what the one thread does, moment by moment, is the fastest way to learn async code.
  • Callbacks still work but nest into pyramids and hide control-flow bugs (missing return, double calls, uncatchable throws); promises and async/await replaced them.
  • A promise is a handle to a future value with three states (pending, fulfilled, rejected); .then chains flatten the pyramid and one .catch handles the whole chain.
  • Inside .then, return a value, return a promise, or throw; forgetting to return is the most common chaining bug.
  • Promise.all for "all must succeed" (and parallel speedup on independent work); Promise.allSettled for independent best-effort work; a concurrency limit for big batches.
  • Serial awaits on independent operations are the most common performance bug; group them with Promise.all.
  • Never use await inside forEach; use for...of (serial) or Promise.all(map(...)) (parallel), and never leave a promise floating.
  • Every external call needs a timeout; a hung dependency with no timeout causes cascading outages.
  • Prefer AbortController and AbortSignal.timeout for real cancellation; a timeout wrapper stops you waiting but the work may keep running.
  • Never swallow errors in an empty catch; separate expected failures from unexpected ones and handle each on purpose.

Part 3 — How the Web Works

Before you build a backend, you need a clear picture of what a backend actually does. A backend is a program that sits on a computer somewhere, waits for requests to arrive over the internet, and sends back responses. That is the whole job, at the highest level. Everything else — databases, caches, authentication — is detail in service of that one loop: receive a request, produce a response.

This Part explains the web from the ground up for a beginner: what a client is and what a server is, what the browser does, what an HTTP request and response look like, status codes and headers, URLs, JSON, and what people mean when they say "REST API". Part 10 covers the networking stack (TCP, DNS, TLS) in full depth and Part 14 covers API design as a craft; this Part is the on-ramp, the mental model you need before those make sense. We keep it concrete: every example is a real request you could send with a browser or with curl.

3.1 Client and server

3.1.1 The request-response model

Concept

The web runs on a simple pattern: a client asks, a server answers. The client sends a request; the server sends back a response. That single exchange — one request, one response — is the atom of the web. A web page loading is dozens of these exchanges; using an app is thousands.

The client is whatever makes the request: a web browser, a mobile app, another server, or a tool like Postman or curl. The server is the program that listens for requests and produces responses — that program is your backend.

Why does it exist?

Your data and logic cannot live only on the user's device. If an e-commerce site kept every product and every order on each shopper's phone, no two shoppers would see the same catalog, nobody could place an order anyone else could fulfil, and a lost phone would lose the business. So the shared truth lives on a server, and clients talk to it over the network. The request-response model is the agreed way for millions of clients to safely share one source of truth.

Mental model

Imagine a restaurant with a kitchen behind a serving hatch. You (the client) write your order on a slip and pass it through the hatch (the request). The kitchen (the server) prepares the dish and passes it back (the response). You cannot walk into the kitchen; you can only pass slips and receive plates. Many customers use the same hatch and the same kitchen. The hatch is the network; the slip format is HTTP; the kitchen's rules for which slips it accepts are your API.

How it works

sequenceDiagram
    participant B as Browser
    participant S as Server
    B->>S: GET /products/42 (request)
    S->>S: look up product 42 in the database
    S-->>B: 200 OK + product JSON (response)
    B->>B: render the product on the page
  1. The client builds a request: a method (what it wants to do), a URL (what it wants to act on), headers (extra information), and sometimes a body (data it is sending).
  2. The request travels over the internet to the server (Part 10 explains how — DNS in 10.3, TCP in 10.4, TLS in 10.12).
  3. The server reads the request, does its work (query a database, call another service), and builds a response.
  4. The response travels back: a status code (how it went), headers, and usually a body (the data).
  5. The client uses the response — the browser renders it, the mobile app updates the screen, another server continues its logic.

The connection is usually closed or reused after the exchange, and the server forgets about you. This is what "stateless" means (3.5): each request stands alone and carries everything the server needs to handle it.

Practical example

When you open https://shop.example.com/products/42 in a browser, the browser sends roughly this request:

GET /products/42 HTTP/1.1
Host: shop.example.com
Accept: text/html
User-Agent: Mozilla/5.0 ...

The server responds with roughly:

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Type: text/html; charset=utf-8
Content-Length: 5120

<!doctype html><html>... the product page ...</html>

That page then triggers more requests — for the stylesheet, the images, and often a call to an API like GET /api/products/42 that returns JSON for the dynamic parts. A single page load can be fifty request-response exchanges.

See it yourself: a real server and a real client

Reading request text is one thing; watching your own server receive one is another. This is a complete web server in about twenty lines, using only Node's built-in node:http module (no framework). Save it as server.ts and run node server.ts (Node 22.18 or newer runs TypeScript directly; on older versions use npx tsx server.ts).

import { createServer } from 'node:http';

const products: Record<string, { id: string; name: string; priceCents: number }> = {
  '42': { id: '42', name: 'Dental Mirror', priceCents: 1299 },
};

const server = createServer((req, res) => {
  // 1. Read the request: method, path, headers.
  console.log(`server got: ${req.method} ${req.url}`);
  console.log(`  host=${req.headers.host}  accept=${req.headers.accept}`);

  // 2. Do the work.
  const id = req.url?.split('/')[2];                 // "/products/42" -> "42"
  const product = id ? products[id] : undefined;

  // 3. Build the response: status code, headers, body.
  if (!product) {
    res.writeHead(404, { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' });
    res.end(JSON.stringify({ error: { code: 'not_found', message: `product ${id} not found` } }));
    return;
  }
  res.writeHead(200, { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' });
  res.end(JSON.stringify(product));
});

server.listen(4020, () => console.log('listening on http://localhost:4020'));

Leave it running. In a second terminal, run this client, which uses fetch (built into Node 18 and newer, and into every browser):

for (const path of ['/products/42', '/products/999']) {
  const res = await fetch(`http://localhost:4020${path}`, {
    headers: { Accept: 'application/json' },
  });
  console.log(`client got: ${res.status} ${res.statusText}`);
  console.log(`  content-type=${res.headers.get('content-type')}`);
  console.log(`  body=${await res.text()}`);
}
client got: 200 OK
  content-type=application/json
  body={"id":"42","name":"Dental Mirror","priceCents":1299}
client got: 404 Not Found
  content-type=application/json
  body={"error":{"code":"not_found","message":"product 999 not found"}}

And the server's terminal shows the other side of the same two exchanges:

listening on http://localhost:4020
server got: GET /products/42
  host=localhost:4020  accept=application/json
server got: GET /products/999
  host=localhost:4020  accept=application/json

Match the two outputs up. Every request the client sent appears once on the server. Every response has the three parts from step 4 above: a status code, headers, and a body. Your future NestJS controllers do exactly this; the framework just writes the createServer part for you (Part 6).

Reading curl -v output

curl is the command-line tool every backend engineer uses to poke an API. With -v (verbose) it prints the whole conversation. With the server above still running:

curl -sv http://localhost:4020/products/42
* Host localhost:4020 was resolved.
* IPv6: ::1
* IPv4: 127.0.0.1
*   Trying [::1]:4020...
* Connected to localhost (::1) port 4020
> GET /products/42 HTTP/1.1
> Host: localhost:4020
> User-Agent: curl/8.7.1
> Accept: */*
> 
* Request completely sent off
< HTTP/1.1 200 OK
< Content-Type: application/json
< Date: Thu, 05 Nov 2026 10:51:20 GMT
< Connection: keep-alive
< Keep-Alive: timeout=5
< Transfer-Encoding: chunked
< 
{ [63 bytes data]
* Connection #0 to host localhost left intact
{"id":"42","name":"Dental Mirror","priceCents":1299}

The three kinds of line are easy to tell apart once you know the code:

  • Lines starting with * are curl talking to you: it looked up localhost (DNS, Part 10.3) and opened a connection.
  • Lines starting with > are the request curl sent: the request line (GET /products/42 HTTP/1.1), then headers, then a blank line that means "headers finished".
  • Lines starting with < are the response: the status line (HTTP/1.1 200 OK), headers, a blank line, then the body.

Two headers you did not write appeared by themselves. Node added Date, and because our code never said how long the body was, Node sent it in pieces and said so with Transfer-Encoding: chunked instead of Content-Length (Part 10.10 explains chunked transfer). Connection: keep-alive means the connection stays open for the next request instead of being torn down (Part 10.9). The -s flag only hides curl's progress bar. Use curl -i when you want just the response status, headers and body without the * and > lines.

Production scenario

A product API server handles 3,000 requests per second. Each request is independent: it arrives, the server reads it, queries PostgreSQL, builds a response and sends it, all in a few milliseconds, then forgets about it. Because each request is self-contained, the business can run twenty identical copies of the server behind a load balancer (Part 88.7), and any copy can handle any request — there is no "your" server that remembers you. This is only possible because of the stateless request-response model.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • The request never arrives (network drops). The client sees a timeout and may retry.
  • The response never arrives even though the server did the work. The client cannot tell "did it fail?" from "did the reply get lost?" — which is exactly why writes need idempotency (Part 14.12 and Part 29 — Distributed Transactions).
  • The server is slow, so the client waits. A client should have a timeout (Part 2).
  • A large body (a big upload or a huge response) takes time and memory; servers stream large bodies rather than loading them whole (Part 9.23).
  • A client sends a request to the wrong port or the server is not running. fetch rejects with TypeError: fetch failed and curl prints Failed to connect. That is a connection error, not an HTTP error: there is no status code because no response ever came.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior keeps the request-response atom in mind constantly, especially its failure mode: the client can never be sure whether a request that got no response actually happened. That single fact drives idempotency keys, retries, and the design of every write endpoint. They also lean on statelessness as the thing that makes horizontal scaling possible — keep the server forgetful between requests, push any shared state into the database or cache, and you can run as many copies as you need.

Common mistakes

  • Assuming a request always reaches the server and a response always comes back; the network loses both.
  • Keeping per-client state in the server's memory, which breaks the moment you run more than one copy.
  • Not setting client timeouts, so a slow server hangs the client.

Interview questions

  1. What is the request-response model? A client sends a request, a server sends back one response; each exchange is independent and self-contained.
  2. Why is statelessness important for scaling? If the server keeps no per-client memory between requests, any copy can handle any request, so you can run many copies behind a load balancer.
  3. Why can't a client be sure a write succeeded if it gets no response? The failure could be before the work (safe to retry) or after the work but the reply was lost (retry would duplicate); this is why writes need idempotency.

Practical exercise

Using curl (or a browser's network tab), make a GET request to any public API and identify in the output: the method, the URL, three request headers, the status code, and three response headers. Acceptance criteria: you can label each part and say in one sentence what it is for.

Worked answer to the exercise

Using the server from "See it yourself" and curl -sv http://localhost:4020/products/42:

Part Value in our output What it is for
Method GET What the client wants to do: read
URL (path) /products/42 Which resource to act on
Request header Host: localhost:4020 Which site the client wants; one server can host many sites
Request header User-Agent: curl/8.7.1 Which program sent the request
Request header Accept: */* Which response formats the client can handle (*/* means "anything")
Status code 200 OK How it went: success
Response header Content-Type: application/json The body is JSON, so parse it as JSON
Response header Date: ... When the server produced the response
Response header Connection: keep-alive The connection stays open for reuse

Try it yourself

  1. With the server running, what status code does curl -i http://localhost:4020/products/7 print, and which line of the server code produced it?
  2. Stop the server (Ctrl+C) and run the fetch client again. What happens, and is there a status code?
  3. A colleague says "the server remembered me between my two requests". Using the server code above, explain why that is not true.

Answers.

  1. HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found, produced by res.writeHead(404, ...) because product 7 is not in products.
  2. fetch rejects with TypeError: fetch failed (the cause says the connection was refused). There is no status code: no server answered, so no response exists. Your client code must handle this case separately from a 4xx or 5xx.
  3. The handler only reads what is in the current request (req.method, req.url, req.headers). It stores nothing about the client between calls. Each request stands alone; that is statelessness.

3.2 HTTP: the language of the web

3.2.1 Requests, methods and URLs

Concept

HTTP (HyperText Transfer Protocol) is the set of rules for how clients and servers phrase requests and responses. It defines the shape of a request (method, URL, headers, body) and a response (status code, headers, body). Almost every backend you build speaks HTTP. Understanding its pieces is understanding the web.

Why does it exist?

Clients and servers are written by different people in different languages on different machines. They need a shared, agreed format so a browser written by one company can talk to a server written by another. HTTP is that shared format — a universal envelope that everyone understands.

Mental model

HTTP is like the format of a physical letter. There is an action on the outside (the method: deliver, return, update), an address (the URL), information on the envelope (the headers: who it is from, what language, how urgent), and the contents inside (the body). The post office (the network) does not care what is inside; it just follows the envelope. A server reads the envelope and the contents and writes a reply in the same format.

How it works — the methods

The HTTP method (also called the verb) says what the client wants to do. The common ones:

Method Means Changes data? Idempotent? Typical use
GET Read something No Yes Fetch a product, a list, a profile
POST Create something, or do an action Yes No Create an order, log in, trigger a job
PUT Replace something entirely Yes Yes Replace a whole resource
PATCH Change part of something Yes Usually Update one field
DELETE Remove something Yes Yes Delete a resource

Two properties in that table matter a lot. "Changes data" (the technical word is "safe" for methods that do not) tells you whether the request is a read or a write; reads can be cached and retried freely. "Idempotent" means doing it twice has the same effect as doing it once. GET, PUT and DELETE are idempotent — deleting an already-deleted thing still ends with it deleted. POST is not — posting the same order twice creates two orders. This is why retrying a lost GET is safe but retrying a lost POST needs care (Part 71.13 covers the "unknown outcome").

Here is idempotency as a tiny simulation. A client sends a request, the reply is lost on the way back, and the client retries once.

type Order = { productId: string; quantity: number };

const orders = new Map<string, Order>();
let nextId = 1001;

// What a POST /v1/orders handler does: always create a NEW order with a new id.
function handlePost(body: Order): string {
  const id = `ord_${nextId++}`;
  orders.set(id, body);
  return id;
}

// What a PUT /v1/orders/:id handler does: store THIS order under THIS id.
function handlePut(id: string, body: Order): void {
  orders.set(id, body);
}

const body = { productId: '42', quantity: 2 };

// The client sends the request, the reply is lost, so it retries once.
handlePost(body);
handlePost(body);
console.log('after POST + retry:', orders.size, 'orders', [...orders.keys()]);

orders.clear();
handlePut('ord_2001', body);
handlePut('ord_2001', body);
console.log('after PUT + retry: ', orders.size, 'order ', [...orders.keys()]);
after POST + retry: 2 orders [ 'ord_1001', 'ord_1002' ]
after PUT + retry:  1 order  [ 'ord_2001' ]

The POST retry created a second order: the customer is charged twice. The PUT retry wrote the same thing to the same place: no harm done. Real APIs make POST safe to retry with an idempotency key, a unique id the client sends in a header so the server can recognize a repeat (Part 14.12 and Part 71.12).

How it works — the URL

A URL (Uniform Resource Locator) is the address of what the client wants to act on. Its parts:

https://api.shop.example.com:443/v1/products/42?color=red&size=l#reviews
\___/   \____________________/\_/\_____________/\_____________/\______/
scheme        host           port   path          query        fragment
  • Scheme: https (encrypted) or http (not). Always https in production.
  • Host: which server to reach (api.shop.example.com). DNS turns this into an IP address (Part 10).
  • Port: which door on the server (443 for https, 80 for http, often omitted because they are the defaults).
  • Path: which resource (/v1/products/42). This is what your backend routes on.
  • Query string: optional parameters after ?, as key=value pairs joined by &. Used for filtering, pagination, search.
  • Fragment: after #, used only by the browser (to jump to a section); it is never sent to the server.

Taking a URL apart with new URL()

You never need to split a URL by hand with string functions. JavaScript has a built-in URL class (in Node and in browsers) that parses one for you.

const url = new URL('https://api.shop.example.com:8443/v1/products/42?color=red&size=l#reviews');

console.log('protocol:', url.protocol);
console.log('hostname:', url.hostname);
console.log('port:    ', url.port);
console.log('pathname:', url.pathname);
console.log('search:  ', url.search);
console.log('color:   ', url.searchParams.get('color'));
console.log('hash:    ', url.hash);

// Default ports are hidden: 443 is the default for https, so url.port is empty.
console.log('default port shown as:', JSON.stringify(new URL('https://api.shop.example.com:443/').port));
protocol: https:
hostname: api.shop.example.com
port:     8443
pathname: /v1/products/42
search:   ?color=red&size=l
color:    red
hash:     #reviews
default port shown as: ""

The names differ slightly from the diagram (protocol for scheme, hash for fragment), but the pieces are the same. searchParams.get('color') reads one query parameter, already decoded.

Percent-encoding, step by step

A query string uses & to separate parameters and = to separate a name from its value. So what happens when a user searches for gloves & masks?

const search = 'gloves & masks';

// WRONG: pasting user input straight into a URL.
const broken = new URL(`https://api.shop.example.com/v1/products?q=${search}&page=2`);
console.log('broken q   =', JSON.stringify(broken.searchParams.get('q')));
console.log('broken keys=', [...broken.searchParams.keys()]);

// RIGHT: let URLSearchParams do the encoding.
const params = new URLSearchParams({ q: search, page: '2' });
const fixed = new URL(`https://api.shop.example.com/v1/products?${params}`);
console.log('fixed url  =', fixed.href);
console.log('fixed q    =', JSON.stringify(fixed.searchParams.get('q')));

// For a single path segment, use encodeURIComponent.
console.log(encodeURIComponent('Dr. Shah / Clinic #2'));
broken q   = "gloves "
broken keys= [ 'q', ' masks', 'page' ]
fixed url  = https://api.shop.example.com/v1/products?q=gloves+%26+masks&page=2
fixed q    = "gloves & masks"
Dr.%20Shah%20%2F%20Clinic%20%232

In the broken version, the & inside the search text was read as "new parameter here", so the server received q = "gloves " and a strange parameter called " masks". The user searched for one thing and got results for another. URLSearchParams encoded the & as %26 (and the spaces as +), so the value arrived intact. For a value that goes into the path, such as a clinic name, encodeURIComponent turns / into %2F and # into %23; without it, the / would create an extra path segment and the # would cut the URL short, because everything after # is the fragment and never reaches the server.

The path is the heart of API design. /products/42 names "product number 42". /products names the collection. Good APIs use nouns for paths and methods for actions, so GET /products/42 reads product 42 and DELETE /products/42 removes it.

The request body

POST, PUT and PATCH usually carry a body — the data being sent. For APIs the body is almost always JSON (3.4). A GET has no body; its inputs go in the path and query string.

POST /v1/orders HTTP/1.1
Host: api.shop.example.com
Content-Type: application/json
Content-Length: 65

{"items":[{"productId":"42","quantity":2}],"couponCode":"SAVE10"}

Practical example

Here is a small API's endpoints expressed as method-plus-path, the way you will design them:

GET    /v1/products            -> list products (with ?category=&page=)
GET    /v1/products/42         -> get product 42
POST   /v1/products            -> create a product (admin)
PATCH  /v1/products/42         -> update some fields of product 42
DELETE /v1/products/42         -> delete product 42
POST   /v1/orders             -> place an order
GET    /v1/orders/ord_1001    -> get one order

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Using GET to change data (for example GET /deleteUser?id=5) is a classic mistake: search engines and browsers prefetch and cache GETs, so a crawler could delete your data. Reads must be safe; writes must use POST/PUT/PATCH/DELETE.
  • Very long URLs (huge query strings) can be truncated by proxies; there is a practical limit around 2,000 to 8,000 characters. Large inputs belong in a POST body.
  • Query-string injection: query values are user input and must be validated like any other (Part 39 — Security).
  • Unencoded characters in a URL (spaces, &, non-ASCII) break parsing; they must be percent-encoded (%20 for a space).

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior maps each operation to the method whose safety and idempotency match it: reads are GET, creates are POST, full replacements are PUT, partial updates are PATCH, removals are DELETE. They never hide a write behind a GET, because safe methods get retried, prefetched and cached by machinery outside their control. They design paths as nouns and keep them stable, because a URL printed in a mobile app or an integration is a contract that is expensive to change (Part 24 on compatibility).

Common mistakes

  • Changing data with GET, inviting accidental deletions by crawlers and prefetchers.
  • Putting actions in the path (/getProduct, /createOrder) instead of using the method on a noun.
  • Forgetting to percent-encode special characters in URLs.
  • Cramming large data into the query string instead of the body.

Interview questions

  1. What does it mean for an HTTP method to be idempotent, and which methods are? Doing it twice has the same effect as once; GET, PUT, DELETE are idempotent, POST is not. It matters for safe retries.
  2. Why should a read never use POST and a write never use GET? Reads should be safe and cacheable; writes must not be, because GETs are prefetched and cached by browsers, crawlers and proxies, which would trigger the write unexpectedly.
  3. What are the parts of a URL and which part never reaches the server? Scheme, host, port, path, query, fragment; the fragment (after #) stays in the browser.
  4. Where do a GET request's inputs go, versus a POST? GET inputs go in the path and query string; POST inputs go in the body.

Practical exercise

Design the endpoints (method + path) for a simple "notes" API that supports listing notes, getting one note, creating a note, editing a note's text, and deleting a note. Mark which are idempotent. Acceptance criteria: reads use GET, writes use the correct write method, paths are nouns, and no write is hidden behind a GET.

Worked answer to the exercise

GET    /v1/notes            -> list notes (paginated: ?limit=20&cursor=...)   idempotent: yes (and safe)
GET    /v1/notes/{id}       -> get one note                                   idempotent: yes (and safe)
POST   /v1/notes            -> create a note                                  idempotent: no
PATCH  /v1/notes/{id}       -> edit the note's text                           idempotent: yes, for "set text to X"
DELETE /v1/notes/{id}       -> delete a note                                  idempotent: yes

Two details are worth a sentence each. PATCH that sets the text to a given value is idempotent; a PATCH that appends to the text would not be, which is why the table above said "usually". And a second DELETE of the same note may return 404 instead of 204, but the state of the server (the note is gone) is the same, so it still counts as idempotent.

Try it yourself

  1. In https://shop.example.com/v1/orders?status=paid&page=3#top, which parts reach the server?
  2. Which is the better design for "cancel order 1001": GET /v1/orders/1001/cancel, POST /v1/orders/1001/cancel, or DELETE /v1/orders/1001? Why?
  3. What does new URLSearchParams({ email: 'a+b@example.com' }).toString() produce, and why does the + matter?

Answers.

  1. The scheme, host, path /v1/orders and query ?status=paid&page=3 all reach the server (the scheme and host decide where and how to connect). The fragment #top never leaves the browser.
  2. POST /v1/orders/1001/cancel (or POST /v1/orders/1001/cancellations). Cancelling changes data, so it must not be a GET. DELETE would suggest the order record disappears, but a cancelled order still exists with status cancelled, and you will need it for refunds and reports.
  3. email=a%2Bb%40example.com. In a query string a raw + means a space, so without encoding the server would read a b@example.com, a different (and invalid) address.

3.3 Responses: status codes and headers

3.3.1 Status codes

Concept

Every HTTP response starts with a three-digit status code that says, at a glance, how the request went. 200 means "OK". 404 means "not found". 500 means "the server broke". The first digit groups them into five families, and knowing those families lets you understand any status code you have never seen.

Why does it exist?

The client needs a fast, standard way to know the outcome without parsing the whole response. A mobile app that gets a 401 knows to prompt for login; one that gets a 429 knows to slow down; one that gets a 503 knows to retry later. Status codes are the shared vocabulary of outcomes, so clients can react correctly without reading English error messages.

Mental model

Status codes are like the response you get when you mail a letter. 2xx is "delivered and handled". 3xx is "the recipient moved; here is the new address". 4xx is "there is something wrong with your letter — wrong address, missing postage, you are not allowed to send this". 5xx is "the post office itself broke". The first digit tells you whose fault it is and what to do next.

How it works — the five families

1xx  Informational   rare; "continue"                     (you can mostly ignore)
2xx  Success         the request worked
3xx  Redirection     go somewhere else for the resource
4xx  Client error    the client did something wrong
5xx  Server error    the server failed

The ones you will use constantly:

Code Name Meaning When your backend sends it
200 OK Success with a body A successful GET or update
201 Created A new resource was created A successful POST that creates something
204 No Content Success, no body A successful DELETE
301 Moved Permanently Resource has a new URL forever Permanent redirects
302 Found Temporary redirect Temporary redirects
304 Not Modified Your cached copy is still good Conditional GET with ETags (Part 10.11)
400 Bad Request The request is malformed or fails validation Invalid JSON, missing required field
401 Unauthorized You are not authenticated No or invalid login token
403 Forbidden You are authenticated but not allowed A normal user hitting an admin endpoint
404 Not Found The resource does not exist Product id that is not in the database
409 Conflict The request conflicts with current state Duplicate unique value, version conflict
422 Unprocessable Entity Well-formed but semantically invalid Business-rule validation failure
429 Too Many Requests You are being rate-limited Client exceeded its request quota
500 Internal Server Error The server threw an unexpected error An unhandled exception
502 Bad Gateway An upstream server gave a bad response A proxy's backend failed
503 Service Unavailable The server is temporarily down or overloaded Maintenance, overload, failing health check
504 Gateway Timeout An upstream server timed out A downstream dependency hung

The 401-versus-403 distinction trips up beginners. 401 means "I do not know who you are" — log in. 403 means "I know who you are, and you may not do this." The 400-versus-422 distinction: 400 is "I could not even understand your request" (broken JSON); 422 is "I understood it, but it breaks a rule" (ordering a negative quantity).

Reading a status code you have never seen

You will meet codes that are not in the table. Do not panic and do not guess from the number alone; use the family first, then the name. Here is the method applied to three real codes:

  1. 413 Content Too Large. First digit 4: the client did something wrong. The name says the body was too big. So the fix is on the client side: send a smaller file, or upload it a different way. Retrying the same request will fail the same way.
  2. 307 Temporary Redirect. First digit 3: go somewhere else. The Location header says where. Unlike 302, a 307 promises the client will repeat the same method and body at the new address, which matters for a POST.
  3. 507 Insufficient Storage. First digit 5: the server's problem. The client did nothing wrong. It may be worth retrying later, and someone on the server side should be alerted.

The official list is the IANA HTTP Status Code Registry (most codes are defined in RFC 9110), and MDN's "HTTP response status codes" page explains each one in plain language. Part 14.4 discusses which codes a well-designed API actually uses.

Practical example

POST /v1/orders           -> 201 Created   (order placed, body has the new order)
GET  /v1/orders/missing   -> 404 Not Found (no such order)
POST /v1/orders (no auth) -> 401 Unauthorized
DELETE /v1/orders/1 (not yours) -> 403 Forbidden
POST /v1/orders (qty: -1) -> 422 Unprocessable Entity (validation)
(server bug)              -> 500 Internal Server Error

Production scenario

A monitoring dashboard watches the ratio of 5xx responses. Normally it sits near zero. One afternoon it spikes to 8 percent, all 503s from one service. Because status codes are standard, the alert fired automatically and the on-call engineer immediately knew the server was failing (5xx = server's fault), not that clients were sending bad requests (which would be 4xx). The clear separation of "client's fault" (4xx) from "server's fault" (5xx) is what makes this kind of automatic alerting possible, and it is why returning the correct code matters operationally, not just aesthetically.

Failure scenario

An API returned 200 OK with a body like {"error": "not found"} for everything, including failures. The mobile app could not tell success from failure without parsing the body, caches stored error responses as if they were real data, and the monitoring dashboard showed a perfect 5xx rate of zero while users were seeing constant errors. A whole outage went undetected for an hour because the status codes lied. The fix — return 404, 422, 500 correctly — made the errors visible to every layer that speaks HTTP. The lesson: the status code is not decoration; infrastructure depends on it being honest.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Returning 200 for errors (the lie above) breaks caching, monitoring and client logic.
  • Returning 500 for a client's bad input pages the on-call for what is actually a 400; separate client faults from server faults.
  • Returning 404 versus 403 for a resource the user may not see: sometimes 404 is deliberately used to avoid revealing that a resource exists (Part 74.12).
  • Retrying 4xx is usually pointless (the request is wrong and will stay wrong); retrying 5xx and 429 can help (the server may recover).

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior is precise with status codes because machines, not just humans, read them: load balancers route on them, caches decide what to store by them, clients decide whether to retry by them, and dashboards alert on them. They keep the discipline that 5xx means "our bug or outage" and 4xx means "the caller's mistake", so the 5xx rate is a clean signal of system health. They also return a consistent error body shape alongside the code (Part 14), so clients get both a machine-readable status and a human-readable reason.

Common mistakes

  • Returning 200 with an error in the body.
  • Using 500 for validation failures, which should be 400 or 422.
  • Mixing up 401 (not authenticated) and 403 (not allowed).
  • Retrying 4xx responses, which will keep failing.

Interview questions

  1. What is the difference between the 4xx and 5xx families? 4xx is the client's fault (bad request); 5xx is the server's fault (it failed). This split drives monitoring, alerting and retry decisions.
  2. 401 vs 403? 401 means not authenticated (who are you?); 403 means authenticated but not permitted.
  3. 400 vs 422? 400 means the request could not be understood (malformed); 422 means it was understood but violates a rule.
  4. Which status codes are worth retrying? 5xx and 429 (the server may recover; back off first); 4xx generally not, because the request itself is wrong.

Practical exercise

For a "create order" endpoint, list the status code you would return for each of: success, missing auth token, a normal user trying to create an order for another user, an unknown product id, a negative quantity, and an unexpected database error. Acceptance criteria: each maps to the correct code and you can justify the 400-vs-422 and 401-vs-403 choices.

Worked answer to the exercise

Situation Code Why
Order created 201 Created A new resource exists; send its URL in Location and the order in the body
No auth token 401 Unauthorized We do not know who the caller is
Normal user creating an order for another user 403 Forbidden We know who they are; they are not allowed to do this
Unknown product id in the body 422 Unprocessable Entity The request is well formed and the URL /v1/orders exists; the content breaks a rule ("the product must exist"). Some teams use 400 or 404 here; whichever you choose, be consistent and say so in the error body
Negative quantity 422 Unprocessable Entity (or 400) Valid JSON, invalid value. Many NestJS apps return 400 for all validation failures, because that is what ValidationPipe does by default (Part 6); that is also acceptable if it is consistent
Unexpected database error 500 Internal Server Error Our fault, not the client's; log it with the request id and alert on the rate

The justification the exercise asked for: 401 and 403 differ in whether we know who you are; 400 and 422 differ in whether we could understand the request at all.

Try it yourself

Give the status code for each response from an e-commerce API.

  1. GET /v1/products/42 and the product exists.
  2. DELETE /v1/cart/items/7 succeeded and there is nothing useful to send back.
  3. A client sent {"quantity": 2 (the closing brace is missing).
  4. A client sent 500 requests in one minute and the limit is 100.
  5. POST /v1/users with an email that is already registered.
  6. Your API called the payment provider and the provider did not answer within 10 seconds.
  7. The product page moved from /products/42 to /p/dental-mirror-42 for good.

Answers. 1: 200 OK. 2: 204 No Content. 3: 400 Bad Request, because the JSON cannot be parsed at all. 4: 429 Too Many Requests, ideally with a Retry-After header saying when to try again. 5: 409 Conflict, because the request conflicts with existing data (a unique email). 6: 504 Gateway Timeout is the most precise (a server you depend on did not answer in time); some teams use 503. Either way it is a 5xx, because the client did nothing wrong. 7: 301 Moved Permanently with Location: /p/dental-mirror-42.

3.3.2 Headers

Concept

Headers are key-value pairs attached to a request or response that carry information about the message rather than the message itself. "What format is the body?" "Who is sending this?" "Is the client allowed to cache this?" "Here is my authentication token." Headers answer all of these.

Why does it exist?

The body carries the data; everything else the client and server need to know about that data has to live somewhere. Headers are that somewhere — metadata that both sides understand without it polluting the actual content. Authentication, content type, caching, compression, language and dozens of other concerns are negotiated through headers.

How it works — the headers you will meet most

Header On Purpose
Content-Type both What format the body is (application/json, text/html, image/png)
Content-Length both Size of the body in bytes
Authorization request Credentials, usually Bearer <token> (Part 74.3)
Accept request What formats the client can handle in the response
Accept-Encoding request Compression the client supports (gzip, br)
Content-Encoding response How the body is compressed
Cache-Control response Whether and how long to cache (Part 10.11, Part 26)
ETag response A version fingerprint for conditional requests
Set-Cookie response Tells the browser to store a cookie
Cookie request Sends stored cookies back
Location response Where to go, on a 201 (new resource) or 3xx (redirect)
X-Request-Id both A trace id to follow one request across services (Part 77.4)

Content-Type is the one you must always get right. When your API returns JSON, it must send Content-Type: application/json, or clients may misinterpret the bytes. When a client sends a JSON body, it must send the same header, or your server may not parse it.

See it yourself: setting and reading headers

This single script starts a small server, sends it a request with headers, and prints what each side sees. It shows three facts beginners often get wrong.

import { createServer } from 'node:http';
import { randomUUID } from 'node:crypto';

const server = createServer((req, res) => {
  // Node gives you request header names in lowercase, whatever case the client used.
  console.log('server sees:', {
    'content-type': req.headers['content-type'],
    'x-user-id': req.headers['x-user-id'],
  });

  res.setHeader('Content-Type', 'application/json');
  res.setHeader('Cache-Control', 'no-store');               // private data: never cache
  res.setHeader('X-Request-Id', req.headers['x-request-id'] ?? randomUUID());
  res.statusCode = 201;
  res.end(JSON.stringify({ id: 'ord_1001', status: 'pending' }));
});
await new Promise<void>((resolve) => server.listen(4021, resolve));

const res = await fetch('http://localhost:4021/v1/orders', {
  method: 'POST',
  headers: {
    'Content-Type': 'application/json',
    'X-User-Id': 'usr_1',            // anyone can type this; it proves nothing
    'X-Request-Id': 'req-7f3c',
  },
  body: JSON.stringify({ items: [{ productId: '42', quantity: 2 }] }),
});

console.log('client sees:', res.status, {
  'content-type': res.headers.get('CONTENT-TYPE'),          // lookups ignore case too
  'cache-control': res.headers.get('cache-control'),
  'x-request-id': res.headers.get('x-request-id'),
});
server.close();
server sees: { 'content-type': 'application/json', 'x-user-id': 'usr_1' }
client sees: 201 {
  'content-type': 'application/json',
  'cache-control': 'no-store',
  'x-request-id': 'req-7f3c'
}
  1. Header names ignore case. The client wrote X-User-Id; Node's req.headers stores it as x-user-id. The client asked for CONTENT-TYPE; res.headers.get found Content-Type. Always read request headers in lowercase in Node.
  2. Any client can send any header. The server happily received x-user-id: usr_1. Nothing checked it. If your code trusted that header, anyone with curl could become user 1 by typing curl -H "X-User-Id: usr_1" .... Identity must come from something the server can verify, like a signed token (Part 74.3).
  3. The request id travels both ways. The server echoed the client's X-Request-Id, or made a new one with randomUUID() if there was none. Log that id on every line, and you can follow one request through every service it touches.

Practical example

A request with headers and the matching response:

POST /v1/orders HTTP/1.1
Host: api.shop.example.com
Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGci...        <- who the client is
Content-Type: application/json            <- the body is JSON
Accept: application/json                   <- send me JSON back
Accept-Encoding: gzip                      <- I can decompress gzip
X-Request-Id: 7f3c-...                      <- trace id

{"items":[{"productId":"42","quantity":2}]}
HTTP/1.1 201 Created
Content-Type: application/json
Content-Encoding: gzip                      <- the body is gzipped
Location: /v1/orders/ord_1001               <- the new order's URL
Cache-Control: no-store                     <- do not cache this
X-Request-Id: 7f3c-...                       <- same trace id echoed back

{"id":"ord_1001","status":"pending","totalCents":2000}

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Wrong or missing Content-Type so JSON is treated as plain text and parsing fails.
  • Trusting client-supplied headers (like a X-User-Id the client sets) for security decisions; clients can send any header, so authentication must come from a verified token, not a self-declared header (Part 39 — Security).
  • Huge or many headers can be rejected by proxies and servers (limits are often 8 to 16 KB in total; Node's default is 16 KB).
  • Case and duplicates: header names are case-insensitive; some headers can appear more than once.
  • Leaking information in headers (server version, stack details) helps attackers; strip revealing headers in production.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior treats headers as a contract and a security surface at once. They always set Content-Type correctly, always propagate a request-id header for tracing across services, and never trust a client-set header for anything that matters — identity and permissions come from a verified token, period. They use Cache-Control deliberately (a private, user-specific response must be no-store; a public, shareable one can be cached), because a wrong cache header either leaks one user's data to another or wastes a caching opportunity (Part 26).

Common mistakes

  • Forgetting Content-Type: application/json, breaking clients and parsers.
  • Trusting a client-provided header (X-User-Id, X-Role) for authorization.
  • Caching a user-specific response because of a loose Cache-Control, leaking data across users.
  • Leaking server internals through verbose headers.

Interview questions

  1. What is the role of the Content-Type header? It tells the receiver what format the body is in, so it can parse it correctly; JSON APIs must set application/json.
  2. Why must you never trust a client-set header for authorization? Clients can send any header value, so a self-declared X-User-Id or X-Role is forgeable; identity must come from a server-verified token.
  3. Where does the authentication token usually go, and in what format? In the Authorization request header, typically as Bearer <token>.
  4. Why propagate an X-Request-Id header? To trace a single request across multiple services and logs, which is essential for debugging distributed systems (Part 42 — Observability).

Practical exercise

Inspect the headers of a real API response in your browser's network tab. Identify the Content-Type, any caching header, and whether the response sets a cookie. Then explain why a response containing your personal account data should have Cache-Control: no-store. Acceptance criteria: you can read the headers and justify the caching choice for private data.

Worked answer to the exercise

Open DevTools (F12 or Cmd+Option+I), go to the Network tab, reload the page, click an API request (filter by "Fetch/XHR"), and look at Response Headers. On a typical shop's product API you might find content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8, cache-control: public, max-age=60 (anyone may cache this for 60 seconds) and no set-cookie. On a login response you would find a set-cookie with HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax. (Part 135.10 is a full tour of these panels.)

The caching justification: a response with your account data must say Cache-Control: no-store. Otherwise a shared cache (a company proxy, a CDN) could store it and hand your address and orders to the next person who requests the same URL. Public product data can be cached; private data never.

Try it yourself

  1. Your API returns JSON but forgot Content-Type. Node's default for res.end(string) is no content type at all. What might a client do?
  2. Write the curl command that sends Authorization: Bearer abc123 to GET http://localhost:3000/v1/me.
  3. Which header tells the browser where a newly created order lives?

Answers. 1: It may treat the body as plain text or bytes; a strict client refuses to parse it, and a browser may even guess the type ("sniffing"), which is a security risk. Always set it. 2: curl -H "Authorization: Bearer abc123" http://localhost:3000/v1/me. 3: Location, sent with 201 Created.

3.4 JSON: the data format of APIs

3.4.1 What JSON is and how to use it safely

Concept

JSON (JavaScript Object Notation) is a text format for structured data. It looks like JavaScript objects and arrays — curly braces for objects, square brackets for arrays, with strings, numbers, booleans and null. It is the format almost every modern API uses for request and response bodies, because it is human-readable, compact enough, and supported by every programming language.

Why does it exist?

A server written in TypeScript needs to send data to a mobile app written in Swift or Kotlin, or to a browser, or to another server written in Go. They need a shared text format that all of them can produce and parse. JSON won that role because it is simple, maps cleanly onto the basic data types every language has, and is easy for humans to read when debugging.

How it works

{
  "id": "ord_1001",
  "status": "pending",
  "totalCents": 2000,
  "paid": false,
  "shippedAt": null,
  "items": [
    { "productId": "42", "quantity": 2 },
    { "productId": "99", "quantity": 1 }
  ],
  "customer": { "id": "usr_7", "email": "alice@example.com" }
}

The allowed value types are: string (double quotes only — single quotes are invalid JSON), number, boolean (true/false), null, object ({}), and array ([]). That is the whole list. Notably missing: dates (sent as strings, usually ISO 8601 like "2026-11-04T09:30:00Z"), comments (not allowed), and undefined (not a JSON value).

In JavaScript and TypeScript you convert between JSON text and objects with two built-in functions:

// Object -> JSON text (for sending in a response or request body)
const order = { id: 'ord_1001', totalCents: 2000 };
const text = JSON.stringify(order);   // '{"id":"ord_1001","totalCents":2000}'

// JSON text -> object (for reading a request body or a file)
const parsed = JSON.parse(text);      // { id: 'ord_1001', totalCents: 2000 }

Step by step: a full round trip, and what gets lost

Most JSON bugs happen at the edges: a value that does not survive the trip from object to text and back. Run this and compare each line with your guess.

const order = {
  id: 'ord_1001',
  createdAt: new Date('2026-11-04T09:30:00Z'),
  couponCode: undefined,                 // not set
  discountRate: NaN,                     // a bad calculation
  total() { return 2000; },              // a method
};

const text = JSON.stringify(order);
console.log(text);

const back = JSON.parse(text);
console.log(typeof back.createdAt, back.createdAt);   // a string, not a Date
console.log('couponCode' in back);                    // the key is gone entirely

// Big integer ids lose precision as JSON numbers.
console.log(JSON.parse('{"id": 9007199254740993}').id);
console.log(JSON.parse('{"id": "9007199254740993"}').id);

// A circular reference cannot be turned into JSON.
const customer: { name: string; lastOrder?: unknown } = { name: 'Alice' };
customer.lastOrder = { id: 'ord_1001', customer };
try {
  JSON.stringify(customer);
} catch (err) {
  console.log((err as Error).message.split('\n')[0]);
}

// Malformed JSON: single quotes are not allowed.
try {
  JSON.parse("{'id': 'ord_1001'}");
} catch (err) {
  console.log((err as Error).name + ': ' + (err as Error).message);
}
{"id":"ord_1001","createdAt":"2026-11-04T09:30:00.000Z","discountRate":null}
string 2026-11-04T09:30:00.000Z
false
9007199254740992
9007199254740993
Converting circular structure to JSON
SyntaxError: Expected property name or '}' in JSON at position 1 (line 1 column 2)

Line by line:

  1. The Date became a string in ISO 8601 format, in UTC (the Z). That is good for sending, but after JSON.parse it is still a string. If you want a Date back, convert it yourself: new Date(back.createdAt).
  2. couponCode: undefined disappeared, key and all. A client that checks 'couponCode' in body will see false. If "no coupon" matters, send null, which JSON does keep.
  3. NaN became null. So did Infinity. A silent null in a price field is how a bad calculation slips into a client's screen as "$0.00". Validate numbers before you send them.
  4. The method total() vanished. JSON carries data, never behaviour.
  5. The big id changed from ...993 to ...992. JavaScript numbers hold integers exactly only up to 9,007,199,254,740,991 (Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER). PostgreSQL bigint ids can be larger, which is why such ids should travel as strings (the next line kept every digit).
  6. Circular data throws. This happens in practice when you try to send an ORM entity whose relations point back at each other; map it to a plain response object first.
  7. Single quotes are invalid JSON. The error says position 1, the ' right after {. This is the error your server sees when a client hand-writes JSON badly, and the right response is 400 Bad Request.

Practical example — parsing untrusted input safely

Here is the single most important JSON habit for a backend engineer: data parsed from the outside world is unknown and must be validated before you trust it. JSON.parse tells you the text was valid JSON; it does not tell you the data has the shape you expect.

function parseOrderRequest(bodyText: string): { productId: string; quantity: number } {
  let raw: unknown;
  try {
    raw = JSON.parse(bodyText);           // throws on malformed JSON -> 400
  } catch {
    throw new Error('body is not valid JSON');
  }
  // Validate the shape; do not trust it just because it parsed.
  if (
    typeof raw !== 'object' || raw === null ||
    typeof (raw as any).productId !== 'string' ||
    typeof (raw as any).quantity !== 'number' ||
    !Number.isInteger((raw as any).quantity) ||
    (raw as any).quantity <= 0
  ) {
    throw new Error('invalid order: productId (string) and positive integer quantity required');
  }
  return { productId: (raw as any).productId, quantity: (raw as any).quantity };
}

In a real NestJS backend you do not write this by hand — you use DTOs with class-validator (Part 6), which does exactly this validation declaratively. But understanding what the framework does for you is the point: parse, then validate, then trust.

Where the body text comes from

parseOrderRequest takes the body as a string, but a server does not receive one neat string. The body arrives over the network in chunks (pieces of bytes), and a plain node:http server has to collect them. This helper is what NestJS and Express do for you before your controller runs, including the most important protection: a size limit.

import type { IncomingMessage } from 'node:http';

// Collect the request body (it arrives in chunks), refusing anything over maxBytes.
async function readBody(req: IncomingMessage, maxBytes = 10_000): Promise<string> {
  let size = 0;
  const chunks: Buffer[] = [];
  for await (const chunk of req) {
    size += chunk.length;
    if (size > maxBytes) throw new Error('body too large');
    chunks.push(chunk);
  }
  return Buffer.concat(chunks).toString('utf8');
}

for await reads the stream one chunk at a time (an asynchronous loop, Part 130.4). The size check runs on every chunk, so a 50 MB upload is refused after the first 10 KB instead of being held in memory. The worked example in 3.7 uses this exact helper, and you will see it reject a 20 KB body with 413 Content Too Large.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Malformed JSON from a client throws on JSON.parse; catch it and return 400, do not let it crash the request.
  • Valid JSON, wrong shape (missing fields, wrong types) parses fine but is invalid for your purpose; you must validate separately.
  • Numbers losing precision: JSON numbers are floating-point, so a huge integer id (a 64-bit database id) can lose precision. Send such ids as strings.
  • JSON.stringify drops undefined and functions and turns Date into an ISO string; parsing it back gives you a string, not a Date, which surprises people.
  • Deeply nested or huge JSON can be slow to parse and can be used as a denial-of-service (a 50 MB body, or an object nested 100,000 levels deep); limit body size at the gateway (Part 39 — Security).
  • Circular references (an object that contains itself) make JSON.stringify throw.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior treats every incoming JSON body as hostile until validated: parse it (catching malformed input as a 400), then validate its shape and values against a schema before a single field reaches business logic. They cap body size to stop oversized-payload attacks. They send large integer ids as strings to avoid precision loss. They know JSON.stringify loses undefined, functions and Date types, so they map domain objects to explicit response shapes (Part 1) rather than stringifying raw internal objects, which both fixes the type issues and prevents leaking internal fields.

Common mistakes

  • Trusting parsed JSON without validating its shape ("it parsed, so it must be fine").
  • Letting JSON.parse throw crash the request instead of returning 400.
  • Sending 64-bit ids as JSON numbers and losing precision.
  • Expecting a Date back after JSON.parse (you get a string).
  • No body-size limit, allowing huge-payload denial-of-service.

Interview questions

  1. What value types does JSON support? String (double-quoted), number, boolean, null, object, array. No dates (sent as strings), no comments, no undefined.
  2. Why must you validate JSON after parsing it? JSON.parse only confirms the text is valid JSON, not that the data has the expected fields and types; untrusted input must be validated before use.
  3. Why might a large integer id be wrong after a round-trip through JSON? JSON numbers are floating-point, so integers beyond ~9 quadrillion lose precision; send them as strings.
  4. What happens to a Date and to undefined when you JSON.stringify and JSON.parse? A Date becomes an ISO string and does not come back as a Date; undefined properties are dropped entirely.

Practical exercise

Write safeParse(text) that returns a discriminated union { ok: true, value } | { ok: false, error }, returning an error for malformed JSON instead of throwing. Then extend it to validate that the parsed value is an object with a string email and a positive integer quantity. Acceptance criteria: malformed JSON, a non-object, and a valid-JSON-but-wrong-shape input each return a clear { ok: false } with a useful message.

Worked answer to the exercise

type ParseResult<T> = { ok: true; value: T } | { ok: false; error: string };
type OrderRequest = { email: string; quantity: number };

function safeParse(text: string): ParseResult<unknown> {
  try {
    return { ok: true, value: JSON.parse(text) };
  } catch (err) {
    return { ok: false, error: `malformed JSON: ${(err as Error).message}` };
  }
}

function parseOrderRequest(text: string): ParseResult<OrderRequest> {
  const parsed = safeParse(text);
  if (!parsed.ok) return parsed;

  const raw = parsed.value;
  if (typeof raw !== 'object' || raw === null || Array.isArray(raw)) {
    return { ok: false, error: 'body must be a JSON object' };
  }
  const { email, quantity } = raw as Record<string, unknown>;
  if (typeof email !== 'string' || !email.includes('@')) {
    return { ok: false, error: 'email must be a string containing @' };
  }
  if (typeof quantity !== 'number' || !Number.isInteger(quantity) || quantity <= 0) {
    return { ok: false, error: 'quantity must be a positive integer' };
  }
  return { ok: true, value: { email, quantity } };
}

const inputs = [
  '{"email":"alice@example.com","quantity":2}',
  '{"email":"alice@example.com","quantity":2',      // missing closing brace
  '[1, 2, 3]',                                       // valid JSON, not an object
  '{"email":"alice@example.com","quantity":"2"}',    // quantity is a string
  '{"email":"alice@example.com","quantity":-1}',     // negative
];
for (const input of inputs) console.log(parseOrderRequest(input));
{ ok: true, value: { email: 'alice@example.com', quantity: 2 } }
{
  ok: false,
  error: "malformed JSON: Expected ',' or '}' after property value in JSON at position 41 (line 1 column 42)"
}
{ ok: false, error: 'body must be a JSON object' }
{ ok: false, error: 'quantity must be a positive integer' }
{ ok: false, error: 'quantity must be a positive integer' }

Three choices in this answer are worth copying:

  • Two layers. safeParse only answers "is this JSON?"; parseOrderRequest answers "is this an order?". They map to two different status codes: malformed JSON is 400, a wrong shape is 400 or 422 (3.3.1).
  • Array.isArray(raw) is checked. typeof [1, 2, 3] is 'object', so without that check an array would slip through as "an object".
  • "2" is rejected. A string that looks like a number is not a number. Silently converting it would hide a broken client. (NestJS's ValidationPipe can be told to convert types on purpose with transform: true; that is a decision, not an accident.)

Try it yourself

  1. What does JSON.stringify({ items: [] , note: null, gift: undefined }) produce?
  2. Is "hello" (with the quotes) valid JSON on its own? Is hello?
  3. A client sends {"priceCents": 12.5}. It parses fine. Is it valid for a field that stores integer cents, and which check from the worked answer would catch it?

Answers. 1: {"items":[],"note":null}; the empty array and null survive, undefined is dropped. 2: "hello" is valid JSON (a lone string is a complete JSON value), so JSON.parse('"hello"') returns hello; bare hello is not valid and throws. 3: It is not valid; half a cent does not exist. Number.isInteger(12.5) is false, so the integer check catches it.

3.5 REST APIs

3.5.1 What a REST API is

Concept

A REST API is a style of building web APIs where you expose your data as resources (nouns like products, orders, users), each with a URL, and you act on them with the standard HTTP methods (GET to read, POST to create, PATCH to update, DELETE to remove). "REST" stands for Representational State Transfer, but for a beginner the useful definition is simpler: an API organized around nouns and the HTTP methods, speaking JSON, using status codes honestly, and keeping each request self-contained.

Why does it exist?

Before REST became the common style, every API invented its own conventions, so learning one taught you nothing about the next. REST is a set of conventions widely enough agreed that a developer who has used one REST API can guess how another works: GET /things lists, GET /things/5 fetches one, POST /things creates, and the status codes mean what they always mean. That predictability is the value. Part 14 covers REST design as a craft and Part 15 covers the alternatives (GraphQL, gRPC) and when they win; here we establish the baseline.

Mental model

A REST API is like a well-organized library with a strict filing system. Every book (resource) has a shelf location (URL). The actions are standardized: borrow (GET), add a new book (POST), update a record (PATCH), remove a book (DELETE). Because every library uses the same system, once you know it you can walk into any branch and find your way. REST is that shared filing system for data over HTTP.

How it works — the resource pattern

The core idea is: pick your nouns, give each a URL for the collection and for an individual item, and use the methods consistently.

Collection URL:  /v1/products
Item URL:        /v1/products/42

GET    /v1/products       -> list products        (200, array of products)
POST   /v1/products       -> create a product     (201, the new product + Location header)
GET    /v1/products/42    -> get product 42        (200 product, or 404)
PUT    /v1/products/42    -> replace product 42    (200, or 404)
PATCH  /v1/products/42    -> update some fields     (200, or 404)
DELETE /v1/products/42    -> delete product 42      (204, or 404)

Relationships become nested paths:

GET    /v1/orders/ord_1/items        -> the items in order ord_1
POST   /v1/orders/ord_1/items        -> add an item to order ord_1
GET    /v1/users/usr_7/orders        -> that user's orders

Filtering, sorting and pagination go in the query string, never in the path:

GET /v1/products?category=dental&sort=price&page=2&limit=20
GET /v1/orders?status=paid&from=2026-01-01&to=2026-01-31

Pagination

A list endpoint must never return "all" rows — a GET /orders on a table with ten million rows would try to send ten million objects and fall over. Lists are paginated. The two common styles:

Offset pagination:  ?page=2&limit=20   (simple; slow and unstable deep in large tables)
Cursor pagination:  ?cursor=eyJ...&limit=20  (stable and fast; the professional default)

Part 14.5 covers why cursor pagination is preferred for large tables; for now, remember that every list endpoint is paginated and returns a bounded page plus a way to get the next one.

Practical example — a consistent response shape

A small but important discipline: make your responses predictable. A single resource returns the object; a list returns a page object with the items and pagination info; an error returns a consistent error shape.

// GET /v1/products/42
{ "id": "42", "name": "Dental Mirror", "priceCents": 1299 }

// GET /v1/products?limit=2
{
  "items": [
    { "id": "42", "name": "Dental Mirror", "priceCents": 1299 },
    { "id": "43", "name": "Probe", "priceCents": 899 }
  ],
  "nextCursor": "eyJpZCI6IjQzIn0",
  "total": 1284
}

// Any error
{
  "error": { "code": "not_found", "message": "Product 999 not found" }
}

Production scenario

A company's mobile app, website, and a partner integration all talk to the same REST API. Because the API follows REST conventions, the mobile team, the web team and the partner each understood it from the endpoint list alone, with minimal hand-holding. When the company added a new "wishlists" feature, they added GET/POST /v1/wishlists and GET /v1/users/{id}/wishlists following the same pattern, and every consumer immediately knew how to use it. The consistency is what let three independent teams move fast against one API.

Failure scenario

An early API grew without conventions: POST /getProducts, GET /product_delete?id=5, some endpoints returning {data: ...} and others returning the object directly, errors sometimes 200 with an error body and sometimes 500. Every new integration took weeks because nothing was predictable, a crawler that prefetched GET /product_delete?id=5 links deleted products, and the client code was full of special cases. Refactoring to consistent REST conventions — nouns, correct methods, honest status codes, one response shape — cut integration time from weeks to a day and ended the accidental deletions. The lesson: conventions are not pedantry; they are what makes an API usable and safe.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Verbs in the path (/createOrder) instead of methods on nouns; works, but throws away the conventions that make REST valuable.
  • Unbounded lists with no pagination, which fall over on large tables.
  • Inconsistent response shapes across endpoints, forcing clients into special cases.
  • Actions that are not CRUD (create/read/update/delete) — like "refund an order" or "send a password reset" — do not fit neatly; model them as a sub-resource (POST /orders/1/refunds) or accept a pragmatic action endpoint. Dogmatic REST is not the goal; a usable, consistent API is.
  • Breaking changes: once published, a URL and its response shape are a contract; changing them breaks every consumer (Part 24 on versioning and compatibility).

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior uses REST as a strong default because its predictability lowers the cost for everyone who consumes the API, but they are not dogmatic — when an operation is genuinely an action rather than a CRUD operation, they model it pragmatically rather than contorting it. They paginate every list from day one, keep response shapes consistent, use status codes honestly, and treat every published endpoint as a long-lived contract. They also know when REST is the wrong tool: a mobile app that needs to fetch deeply nested data in one round trip may be better served by GraphQL, and internal service-to-service calls may be better as gRPC (Part 15).

Common mistakes

  • Verbs in paths instead of methods on nouns.
  • List endpoints with no pagination.
  • Inconsistent response and error shapes.
  • Changing a published URL or response shape and breaking consumers.
  • Forcing every operation into strict CRUD even when it is awkward.

Interview questions

  1. What makes an API "RESTful" in practical terms? Resources as nouns with URLs, standard HTTP methods for actions, JSON bodies, honest status codes, consistent shapes, and self-contained (stateless) requests.
  2. How do you handle filtering and pagination in REST? In the query string (?status=paid&limit=20&cursor=...), never in the path; every list is paginated.
  3. How would you model "refund an order" in a REST API? As a sub-resource action, for example POST /orders/{id}/refunds, since a refund is a thing you create against an order.
  4. Why is a published URL a contract, and what does that imply? Clients (apps, integrations) depend on it; changing it breaks them, so changes must be backward-compatible or versioned (Part 24).

Practical exercise

Design a REST API for a food-delivery service covering restaurants, menus, orders and order status. Include collection and item URLs, the correct methods, how to filter restaurants by cuisine and location, how to paginate, and how to model "mark an order as delivered". Acceptance criteria: nouns in paths, correct methods and status codes, pagination on every list, and a sensible model for the status-change action.

Worked answer to the exercise

GET    /v1/restaurants?cuisine=thai&lat=12.97&lng=77.59&radiusKm=5&limit=20&cursor=...
                                                    -> 200, a page of restaurants
GET    /v1/restaurants/{restaurantId}               -> 200 restaurant, or 404
GET    /v1/restaurants/{restaurantId}/menu-items?limit=50&cursor=...
                                                    -> 200, a page of menu items
POST   /v1/orders                                   -> 201 + Location, or 422 (item not on that menu)
GET    /v1/orders/{orderId}                         -> 200 order (includes its current status), or 404
GET    /v1/users/{userId}/orders?status=active&limit=20&cursor=...
                                                    -> 200, a page of that user's orders
PATCH  /v1/orders/{orderId}  {"status":"delivered"} -> 200, 409 if the order is not out for delivery

The status change is a PATCH on the order, because "delivered" is a new value of a field the order already has. The 409 Conflict protects the order's state machine: an order that was never picked up cannot jump to delivered. Some teams prefer POST /v1/orders/{orderId}/deliveries, which records who delivered it and when; both are defensible if used consistently. Location goes in the query string because it filters the collection; it is not a resource of its own.

3.6 What the browser does

3.6.1 From URL to rendered page, and why the backend engineer cares

Concept
