They commit early and often on their branch precisely because committed work is recoverable from the reflog, and they treat --hard as a command that deserves a git status and a second look.

Common mistakes

  • Using reset --hard to "undo" and losing uncommitted work.
  • Amending a commit that was already pushed, then force-pushing over a shared branch.
  • Deleting a leaked secret with a new commit and not rotating it.
  • Panicking and recloning the repository instead of checking the reflog.

Interview questions

  1. What is the difference between git revert and git reset? Revert adds a new commit that undoes an earlier one, safe on shared history; reset moves the branch pointer back, rewriting history, safe only for unpushed commits.
  2. You committed to the wrong branch and have not pushed. How do you fix it? Note the commit, git reset --soft HEAD~1 (or git reset HEAD~1) on the wrong branch, switch to the right branch, and commit there; or cherry-pick the commit and reset the wrong branch.
  3. How do you recover a commit after a bad reset --hard? Find it in git reflog and reset or branch to it.
  4. You pushed a commit containing an API key. What do you do? Rotate the key immediately; then remove it from the code; rewriting history is secondary and needs coordination.

Practical exercise

In a practice repository, perform each undo once: discard an unstaged edit with restore; unstage a file with restore --staged; change the last commit's message with --amend; undo two local commits with reset --soft HEAD~2 and recommit them as one; revert a commit; then do reset --hard HEAD~1 and recover the commit with reflog. Acceptance criteria: git log --oneline after each step shows what you expected, and you can say for each command whether it rewrote history or added to it.

5.8 Reading history: log, show, diff and blame

5.8.1 Answering "what changed, when, and why?"

Concept

Git's history is only useful if you can read it. A few commands answer almost every question about the past: git log (which commits, by whom, with filters), git show (what one commit changed), git diff between any two points (what differs between branches or commits), and git blame (which commit last changed each line of a file). Together they let you find when a bug was introduced and, from a good commit message (5.1), why.

Why does it exist?

Because most debugging starts with "this worked last week". If you can list what changed in the area since last week, you usually find the cause in minutes. And when code looks strange, the commit that introduced it often explains the reason ("Use string for amounts: JavaScript numbers lose precision above 2^53"), which stops you from "fixing" something that was deliberate.

Mental model

Imagine a building's maintenance log. log lists every visit. show opens one visit's report. diff compares the building's floor plans from two dates. blame puts a sticky note on every wall saying which visit last moved it.

How it works

git log --oneline --graph --decorate -15    # last 15 commits as a graph with branch names
git log --author="Asha" --since="2 weeks ago" --oneline
git log --oneline -- src/orders/            # only commits that touched this folder
git log -p -2 -- src/orders/orders.service.ts   # the last 2 commits to this file, with their diffs
git log -S "deliveryFee" --oneline          # commits that added or removed the text "deliveryFee"
git log --grep="TSB-4422" --oneline         # commits whose message mentions a ticket

git show 5e0f311                            # one commit: message plus the full diff
git show --stat 5e0f311                     # one commit: message plus the list of files changed
git show 5e0f311:src/orders/orders.service.ts   # the whole file as it was in that commit

git diff main..feat/add-coupons             # what is different between the two branch tips
git diff main...feat/add-coupons            # what the feature branch changed since it left main (three dots)
git diff HEAD~3 -- package.json             # how package.json changed over the last 3 commits

git blame -L 40,60 src/orders/orders.service.ts   # who last changed lines 40 to 60, and in which commit

The two-dot and three-dot forms of diff differ in a way that matters for reviews: three dots compares the feature branch with the point where it left main, which is exactly what a pull request shows; two dots compares the two tips, so changes that landed on main since then also appear.

Practical example — "discounts are wrong since yesterday"

Support reports that order totals with coupons are ₹40 too high since yesterday afternoon. The totals code lives in src/checkout/.

git switch main && git pull
git log --since="2 days ago" --oneline -- src/checkout/
# 3f9c1a2 Apply delivery fee after discount (TSB-4510)
# 8b77e01 Rename CartTotals fields

git show 3f9c1a2
# -  const total = subtotal - discount + deliveryFee;
# +  const total = subtotal + deliveryFee - discount;   <- same result... unless discount is capped later
# +  return capDiscount(total, coupon);                  <- the cap now includes the delivery fee

Two commands found the change, the line, the ticket and the author. The next step is a conversation with the author and a test that pins the expected total (Part 6 adds tests), not a guess.

Practical example — "why is this a string?"

git blame -L 18,18 src/orders/order.entity.ts
# 9a1d4e7 (Ravi K 2026-03-02) @Column({ type: 'bigint' }) totalMinor!: string;
git show 9a1d4e7 --stat
# Store money as integer minor units in a string
#
# JavaScript numbers lose precision above 2^53, and PostgreSQL bigint
# arrives in node-postgres as a string. Keep it a string end to end.

The "weird" string type is deliberate, and the commit message says why. Without it, someone would "fix" it to number and introduce rounding bugs.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Blame shows the last change, not the original author: a formatting commit (Prettier over the whole repo) makes every line look like it was written by one person; git blame -w ignores whitespace, and a .git-blame-ignore-revs file listing formatting commits (with git blame --ignore-revs-file) skips them.
  • Renamed or moved files hide older history; git log --follow -- path follows a single file across renames.
  • Huge histories: git log -p without a limit or path scrolls forever; always add -n, a path or a date.
  • Squash-merged pull requests (5.10) turn a branch's many commits into one on main; the detail lives in the pull request on the Git host.
  • Blame is for understanding, not for blaming people: the point is to find context.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior reads history before changing strange code, and before debugging a regression they list what changed in the area since it last worked. They search history by text (-S), by ticket (--grep) and by path, and they read the commit and the pull request behind a line before deciding it is a bug. For regressions with no obvious suspect, Part 90.9 shows git bisect, which finds the breaking commit by binary search. And they write commit messages with this future reader in mind.

Common mistakes

  • Debugging a regression without first looking at what changed.
  • Reading git diff main..branch (two dots) as the pull request's change.
  • Treating git blame output as an accusation instead of a lead.
  • Never using git log -- path, scrolling the whole history instead.

Interview questions

  1. How do you find which commits changed a given file in the last week? git log --since="1 week ago" --oneline -- path.
  2. How do you find the commit that introduced a particular line or string? git blame for the line, or git log -S "text" for when the text was added or removed.
  3. What is the difference between main..feature and main...feature in git diff? Two dots compares the tips; three dots compares the feature tip with the merge base, which is what a pull request shows.
  4. Why does git blame sometimes point at a formatting commit? It shows the last change to each line; ignore whitespace with -w or list formatting commits in an ignore-revs file.

Practical exercise

In any real repository you have cloned (an open-source NestJS project works well): list the last 10 commits as a graph; find the commits that touched one specific file; pick one and show its diff; find the commit that last changed one line with blame; and find when a specific function name first appeared with log -S. Acceptance criteria: for one line of code, you can state who changed it, when, in which commit, and quote the reason from the commit message or pull request.

5.9 Stash and tags

5.9.1 Shelving unfinished work, and naming releases

Concept

Two small tools you will use every week. git stash puts your uncommitted changes on a shelf so your working directory is clean, and later puts them back. Tags are permanent names for specific commits, used to mark releases (v1.4.0), so anyone can find exactly what code was deployed.

Why does it exist?

Stash exists because work gets interrupted. You are halfway through a feature when someone asks you to check a bug on main; your changes are not ready to commit, and switching branches with them is confusing (5.2). Tags exist because commit hashes are unmemorable: "what did we ship on Tuesday?" should have an answer like v1.4.0, not 9c1e2f0.

Mental model

Stash is a drawer: you sweep the papers off your desk into it, help a colleague on a clean desk, then tip the drawer back out. A tag is a bookmark with a label, permanently clipped to one page of the history book.

How it works

git stash push -m "coupon form half done"     # shelve tracked changes with a description
git stash push -u -m "with new files"        # -u: include untracked (new) files too
git stash list                               # stash@{0}: On feat/coupons: coupon form half done
git stash show -p stash@{0}                  # what is in it
git stash pop                                # reapply the newest stash and remove it from the list
git stash apply stash@{1}                    # reapply a specific stash, keep it in the list
git stash drop stash@{1}                     # delete one stash

git tag                                      # list tags
git tag -a v1.4.0 -m "Release 1.4.0: coupons" # an annotated tag on the current commit (recommended)
git tag -a v1.3.2 5e0f311 -m "Hotfix"         # tag an older commit
git push origin v1.4.0                       # tags are NOT pushed by git push; push them explicitly
git show v1.4.0                              # the tag's message and the commit it points to
git switch --detach v1.3.2                   # look at the code exactly as released (read-only "detached HEAD")

Release tags usually follow semantic versioning, MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH (4.6): bump PATCH for fixes, MINOR for new backward-compatible features, MAJOR for breaking changes. Many CI pipelines (Part 46) build and deploy when a tag matching v* is pushed.

Detached HEAD sounds alarming but only means "you are looking at a commit, not at a branch". You can look around and run the code. If you want to keep changes made there, create a branch first: git switch -c fix/from-v1.3.2.

Practical example — an interruption

# On feat/coupons, half-way through. A teammate asks: "does main still build?"
git status                                   # 3 modified files, 1 new file
git stash push -u -m "coupon form half done"
git switch main && git pull
npm ci && npm run build                      # check main: it builds
git switch feat/coupons
git stash pop                                # your 4 changes are back, exactly as they were
git status

Practical example — tagging a release and finding it later

git switch main && git pull
git log --oneline -1                         # 7b1c9d2 Merge pull request #142 from feat/coupons
git tag -a v1.4.0 -m "Release 1.4.0: coupons at checkout"
git push origin v1.4.0
# Three weeks later: "what changed between the last two releases?"
git log --oneline v1.3.2..v1.4.0             # every commit in 1.4.0 that was not in 1.3.2

Edge cases & failure modes

  • stash pop conflicts when the branch changed since you stashed; resolve like a merge conflict (5.4). The stash stays in the list until you drop it.
  • Forgotten stashes pile up for months; git stash list regularly, and prefer a work-in-progress commit on your branch for anything longer than an hour.
  • Untracked files are not stashed without -u, so new files stay behind and appear on the other branch.
  • Tags not pushed: git push does not send tags; CI never sees the release.
  • Moving a published tag (deleting and re-creating v1.4.0 on another commit) confuses everyone who fetched it; create v1.4.1 instead.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior uses stash for short interruptions and a work-in-progress commit on their own branch for anything longer, because commits are visible, named and recoverable, while stashes are easy to forget. They tag every release with an annotated tag from CI rather than by hand, follow semantic versioning, and never move a published tag.

Common mistakes

  • Using stash as long-term storage.
  • Forgetting -u and leaving new files behind.
  • Creating tags locally and never pushing them.
  • Re-pointing a released tag.

Interview questions

  1. When would you use git stash? To clean the working directory briefly (switch branches, pull, test something) without committing half-done work.
  2. How do you see what changed between two releases? git log v1.3.2..v1.4.0 (and git diff with the same range for the code).
  3. What is a detached HEAD, and is it dangerous? HEAD points at a commit instead of a branch; it is safe to look around, but create a branch before committing work you want to keep.

Practical exercise

In a practice repository, make changes to one tracked file and create one new file, stash both with a message, confirm the working directory is clean, switch branches and back, and pop the stash. Then tag the current commit as v0.1.0 (annotated), make two more commits, tag v0.2.0, and list the commits between the tags. Acceptance criteria: the new file survived the stash, and git log --oneline v0.1.0..v0.2.0 shows exactly your two commits.

5.10 Keeping your branch up to date and handling review feedback

5.10.1 From "changes requested" to merged

Concept

A pull request rarely merges as first written. Reviewers ask for changes, main moves on while you wait, and CI may fail. This section is the routine for all three: bring main's new commits into your branch, push follow-up commits that answer review comments, and finish with the merge style your team uses (merge commit, squash, or rebase), then clean up.

Why does it exist?

Because a branch that falls behind main collects conflicts, and a pull request that ignores feedback never merges. Beginners often panic at "This branch is out-of-date with the base branch" or at twenty review comments. Both are normal, and both have a simple, repeatable answer.

Mental model

Imagine handing in an essay draft. The teacher's comments come back, and meanwhile the class syllabus was updated. You read the new syllabus, adjust your essay to fit it, address each comment, and hand it back with a short note saying what you changed. You do not start a new essay.

How it works

Bring main into your branch (the merge approach from 5.4, which never rewrites your history):

git switch feat/add-coupons
git fetch origin                        # download new commits without changing your files
git merge origin/main                   # bring main's new commits into your branch
# resolve any conflicts (73.4), run the tests, then:
git push                                # your pull request now includes the merge

Part 90.5 covers the alternative, git rebase origin/main, which replays your commits on top of main for a straight history. It rewrites your branch's commits, so it needs git push --force-with-lease and should only be used on a branch nobody else is working on.

Answer review comments with new commits:

# Reviewer: "Validate that the coupon code is uppercase letters and digits only."
# ... edit src/coupons/dto/apply-coupon.dto.ts ...
git add src/coupons/dto/apply-coupon.dto.ts
git commit -m "Validate coupon code format (review)"
git push                                # the pull request updates; reply to the comment: "Done in 3e1a9f0"

Small follow-up commits make re-review easy: the reviewer looks at only what changed since their last look.

Merge styles you will see as buttons on GitHub or GitLab:

Style What lands on main Good for
Merge commit All your commits plus a merge commit Keeping every step of the branch
Squash and merge One commit containing the whole pull request Clean main history; one commit per feature, easy to revert
Rebase and merge Your commits replayed onto main, no merge commit Straight history with individual commits

Many teams choose squash: one pull request, one commit on main, with the pull request title as its message. Your branch's "fix typo" commits then never clutter main.

After the merge, clean up:

git switch main
git pull                                # get the merged result
git branch -d feat/add-coupons          # delete the local branch (-d refuses if it is not merged)
git fetch --prune                       # forget remote branches that were deleted on the host

After a squash merge, git branch -d may refuse because your branch's individual commits are not on main (only their squashed copy is). If the pull request shows "merged", it is safe to use git branch -D.

Practical example — an out-of-date branch with a CI failure

Your pull request shows two problems: "This branch has conflicts that must be resolved" and a failing test.

git switch feat/add-coupons
git fetch origin
git merge origin/main
# CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in src/checkout/checkout.service.ts
# open the file, keep both changes correctly (73.4), remove the markers
git add src/checkout/checkout.service.ts
git commit                              # completes the merge with the default message
npm test                                # the failing test: main renamed CartTotals.total to totalMinor
# ... update your code to use totalMinor ...
git commit -am "Use totalMinor after main's rename"
npm test                                # green
git push

The conflict and the CI failure had the same cause: main moved. Merging it in early and often keeps both small.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Force-pushing to a shared branch after a rebase overwrites a teammate's commits; use merge on shared branches, and --force-with-lease only on your own (Part 47).
  • "Fixing" review comments by opening a new pull request loses the discussion; push to the same branch.
  • Huge pull requests get shallow reviews and many conflicts; split them (5.3).
  • Resolving a conflict without running the tests can produce code that compiles and is wrong (5.4).
  • Branch protection rules on main (required reviews, required CI checks) are why the merge button stays grey; that is the gate working, not a bug.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior keeps their branch close to main by merging it in daily on long pieces of work, answers each review comment either with a commit or a short reason, and makes re-review cheap with small follow-up commits. They know their team's merge style and why it was chosen, they never force-push a branch someone else uses, and they clean up merged branches so git branch stays readable. They treat review as a conversation that improves the code, giving and receiving it with the same respect.

Common mistakes

  • Letting a branch fall weeks behind main.
  • Answering review comments with silence or with an unrelated rewrite.
  • Rebasing and force-pushing a shared branch.
  • Never deleting merged branches.

Interview questions

  1. Your pull request is out of date with main. What do you do? Fetch, merge origin/main into the branch (or rebase if it is only yours), resolve conflicts, run tests, push.
  2. What is the difference between squash merge and a merge commit? Squash puts the whole pull request as one commit on main; a merge commit keeps all branch commits plus a merge commit.
  3. Why push follow-up commits instead of amending during review? The reviewer can see exactly what changed since their last review, and no force-push is needed.

Practical exercise

With a partner (or two clones of one repository acting as two people): both branch from main; person A changes a function and merges a pull request; person B changes the same function on their branch, opens a pull request, sees the conflict, merges origin/main into their branch, resolves it, gets one review comment, answers it with a follow-up commit, and merges with squash. Acceptance criteria: main's history shows one commit for each pull request, and both local clones end with the feature branches deleted.

5.11 Worked example: one feature from ticket to merged pull request

This example follows one small change through every step of this Part, with the exact commands and the output you would see. The ticket: TSB-4510 — "Reject coupon codes with lowercase letters; the API should return a clear 400."

1. Start from an up-to-date main (5.2)

cd ~/work/shop-api
git switch main
git pull
# Updating 5e0f311..7b1c9d2
# Fast-forward
#  src/checkout/checkout.service.ts | 14 ++++++++------
git switch -c fix/TSB-4510-coupon-code-format

2. Make the change in small commits (5.1)

# ... edit src/coupons/dto/apply-coupon.dto.ts: add @Matches(/^[A-Z0-9]{4,16}$/) ...
git status
# modified:   src/coupons/dto/apply-coupon.dto.ts
git diff                                 # read your own change before staging it
git add src/coupons/dto/apply-coupon.dto.ts
git commit -m "Validate coupon code format in ApplyCouponDto (TSB-4510)"

# ... add a test in test/coupons.e2e-spec.ts for a lowercase code -> 400 ...
git add test/coupons.e2e-spec.ts
git commit -m "Test that lowercase coupon codes are rejected"
git log --oneline -3
# c42b8e1 Test that lowercase coupon codes are rejected
# 91f0a3d Validate coupon code format in ApplyCouponDto (TSB-4510)
# 7b1c9d2 Merge pull request #142 from feat/coupons

3. Oops: fix the last commit before pushing (5.7)

You notice the test file also contains a stray console.log.

# ... remove the console.log ...
git add test/coupons.e2e-spec.ts
git commit --amend --no-edit             # fold the fix into the last commit: not pushed yet, so this is fine

4. Push and open the pull request (5.3)

git push -u origin fix/TSB-4510-coupon-code-format
# remote: To create a merge request for fix/TSB-4510-coupon-code-format, visit:
# remote:   https://gitlab.example.com/shop/shop-api/-/merge_requests/new?...

The description, following 5.3: what (reject codes that are not uppercase letters and digits, 4 to 16 characters), why (support saw "coupon not found" for save50; users need a clear validation message), how to test (POST /v1/checkout/coupon with save50 returns 400 with a message), risk (low; codes in the database are all uppercase, checked with a query).

5. Main moves, and there is a conflict (5.10, 5.4)

While you wait for review, a teammate merges a change to the same DTO file.

git fetch origin
git merge origin/main
# Auto-merging src/coupons/dto/apply-coupon.dto.ts
# CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in src/coupons/dto/apply-coupon.dto.ts
git status
# both modified:   src/coupons/dto/apply-coupon.dto.ts

The file shows both versions:

<<<<<<< HEAD
  @Matches(/^[A-Z0-9]{4,16}$/, { message: 'Coupon codes use capital letters and digits, 4 to 16 characters' })
  code!: string;
=======
  @Transform(({ value }) => (typeof value === 'string' ? value.trim() : value))
  @IsString()
  code!: string;
>>>>>>> origin/main

Both changes are wanted: your format check and your teammate's trimming. The correct result keeps both:

  @Transform(({ value }) => (typeof value === 'string' ? value.trim() : value))
  @IsString()
  @Matches(/^[A-Z0-9]{4,16}$/, { message: 'Coupon codes use capital letters and digits, 4 to 16 characters' })
  code!: string;
git add src/coupons/dto/apply-coupon.dto.ts
git commit                                # completes the merge
npm test                                  # always after resolving (73.4)
# Tests: 48 passed, 48 total
git push

6. Review feedback (5.10)

The reviewer asks: "Should we uppercase the code for the user instead of rejecting save50?" After a short discussion on the pull request, the team agrees: uppercase it in the transform, and still reject anything with other characters.

# ... change the transform to value.trim().toUpperCase(); update the test: save50 -> accepted, save-50 -> 400 ...
git commit -am "Uppercase coupon codes before validation (review)"
git push

7. Merge and clean up (5.10)

CI is green and the reviewer approves. The team uses squash merge, so main gets one commit titled "Validate and normalise coupon code format (TSB-4510)".

git switch main
git pull
git log --oneline -2
# e8d04c6 Validate and normalise coupon code format (TSB-4510)
# 2a6b3f9 Trim coupon codes in ApplyCouponDto
git branch -D fix/TSB-4510-coupon-code-format   # -D: squash merge means the branch's own commits are not on main
git fetch --prune

8. Release (5.9)

At the end of the week, CI tags the release from main:

git tag -a v1.4.1 -m "Release 1.4.1: coupon code validation"
git push origin v1.4.1
git log --oneline v1.4.0..v1.4.1          # what went into this release

What to notice

Step Command family Section
Up-to-date start, branch per ticket switch, pull 5.2
Small commits with reasons add, commit, diff 5.1
Fixing an unpushed commit commit --amend 5.7
Sharing for review push -u, pull request 5.3
Main moved: merge it in, resolve, test fetch, merge, conflict markers 5.4, 5.10
Review answered with a commit commit, push 5.10
Merge style and cleanup squash, branch -D, fetch --prune 5.10
Release tag -a, push origin <tag> 5.9

Practical exercise

Do this whole example yourself on the API from Part 6, or any small project with a remote on GitHub or GitLab.

Acceptance criteria:

  • A branch named after a ticket, at least two focused commits, and one --amend before the first push.
  • A conflict created deliberately from a second clone, resolved so both changes survive, with tests run afterwards.
  • One review comment answered with a follow-up commit.
  • A squash merge, the local branch deleted, and an annotated release tag pushed.
  • git log --oneline on main reads like a short, clear story of the project.

Senior Checklist — Part 5

  • I understand the concept
  • I can explain it simply
  • I can implement it
  • I understand its failure modes
  • I understand its scaling limitations
  • I understand its security implications
  • I understand its operational implications
  • I can choose between alternatives
  • I can explain the tradeoffs
  • I can answer senior-level interview questions
  • I understand the working directory, staging area and repository
  • I write small, focused commits with messages that explain why
  • I keep secrets and node_modules out of git with .gitignore
  • I branch off up-to-date main and keep branches short-lived
  • I push, open small reviewable pull requests, and write useful descriptions
  • I can resolve a merge conflict and re-run tests afterward
  • I know the safe undo commands and that the reflog can rescue almost anything
  • I can set up Git's identity and SSH access on a new machine and check both
  • I choose restore, amend, reset or revert by asking whether anyone else has seen the commits
  • I can find what changed, when and why with log, show, diff and blame
  • I use stash for short interruptions and annotated, pushed tags for releases
  • I keep my branch up to date with main, answer review comments with follow-up commits, and clean up after merging

Part 5 — Key Takeaways

  • Git records your project's history as commits; a change moves from the working directory, through the staging area (git add), into the permanent history (git commit).
  • Commit messages are documentation: explain why, make focused commits, and keep secrets and node_modules out of history with .gitignore (committed secrets must be rotated).
  • Branches are cheap movable pointers that let people work in parallel; always branch off up-to-date main and keep branches short-lived to avoid painful merges.
  • A remote is the shared central copy; you push and pull to sync, and pull requests provide the review-and-check gate before code enters main.
  • Keep pull requests small so they get genuine review; write descriptions covering what, why, how to test and risk.
  • Merge conflicts are Git correctly refusing to guess when two changes overlap; resolve by writing the correct final content, removing the markers, and re-running tests.
  • Prevent most conflicts by keeping branches short and pulling main often; git merge --abort gives a clean slate when a merge goes wrong.
  • The daily loop — pull, branch, commit, push, PR, review, merge, clean up — is the rhythm of professional development; Part 47 adds the advanced tools on top of it.
  • Use safe tools on shared history (git revert, not rewriting published commits), and remember the reflog can recover almost anything, so Git is never something to fear.
  • Set up your identity and SSH key once per machine, and check them before your first push.
  • Before debugging a regression, read what changed: git log -- path, git show, git blame.
  • Stash is a drawer for minutes, a commit is safe storage for hours; tags name releases and must be pushed.

Part 6 — Your First Backend

This is the Part where everything in Volume 0 comes together. You know TypeScript (Part 1), asynchronous code (Part 2), how the web works (Part 3), the command line (Part 4), and Git (Part 5). Now you build a real backend: a small NestJS API that stores products in PostgreSQL, with a controller, a service, a DTO with validation, and a repository that talks to the database. By the end you will have an API you can run, call with curl, and extend.

We build a products API for an e-commerce store — list products, get one, create one — because it is the simplest thing that still exercises every layer: an HTTP endpoint, input validation, business logic, and database persistence. The code here is deliberately production-shaped, not a toy: real validation, real error handling, real SQL. It is small enough to hold in your head and real enough that the habits transfer directly to the systems the rest of the book describes.

This Part is a guided build. Later Parts go deep on each piece: NestJS dependency injection in Part 12 (12.7), API design in Part 14, PostgreSQL in Parts 16–20, TypeORM and Sequelize in Part 21, security in Part 39, testing in Part 40. Sections 6.5 to 6.9 then grow the project step by step: better validation, consistent errors, tests, and running everything with Docker. Here we assemble a working whole so those deep dives have something concrete to attach to.

6.1 Project setup

6.1.1 What NestJS is and creating the project

Concept

NestJS is a framework for building backend applications in Node.js and TypeScript. A framework gives you structure and ready-made pieces (routing, validation, dependency injection, database integration) so you do not reinvent them. NestJS organizes an application into modules, controllers (which handle HTTP requests) and services (which hold business logic), with a system that wires them together for you called dependency injection.

Why does it exist?

You could build an HTTP server with raw Node, but you would hand-write routing, request parsing, validation, error handling, and the plumbing to connect everything — and every project would do it differently. NestJS provides a consistent, well-trodden structure so a team can build and a new engineer can navigate any NestJS codebase. It is opinionated in a way that pays off: the conventions mean less bikeshedding and more shared understanding. Part 12 covers its architecture and dependency injection in depth; here we use it.

Mental model

Think of NestJS as a pre-built office building with the plumbing, wiring and room layout already in place. You do not lay pipes or run cables; you move into rooms designed for specific purposes — the controller is the reception desk that receives visitors (requests), the service is the back office that does the actual work, the repository is the records department that talks to the filing system (the database). The building (the framework) handles how they connect; you furnish each room with your logic.

How it works — creating the project

NestJS has a command-line tool that scaffolds a project. Using Node 22 and NestJS 10:

npm i -g @nestjs/cli          # install the Nest CLI globally
nest new products-api          # scaffold a new project (choose npm when prompted)
cd products-api
npm run start:dev              # run in watch mode: restarts on file changes
# the API is now running on http://localhost:3000

The generated project structure (the parts that matter):

products-api/
├── src/
│   ├── main.ts                 # the entry point: creates and starts the app
│   ├── app.module.ts           # the root module: wires everything together
│   ├── app.controller.ts       # a sample controller (we will replace it)
│   └── app.service.ts          # a sample service
├── test/                       # end-to-end tests
├── package.json                # dependencies and scripts
├── tsconfig.json               # TypeScript configuration
└── .env                        # our config (we add this; it is gitignored)

The entry point, main.ts, is where the application starts. The generated version is minimal; we will enhance it shortly with validation and configuration.

// src/main.ts (generated)
import { NestFactory } from '@nestjs/core';
import { AppModule } from './app.module';

async function bootstrap() {
  const app = await NestFactory.create(AppModule);
  await app.listen(3000);
}
bootstrap();

NestFactory.create(AppModule) builds the whole application from the root module, wiring together every controller and service. app.listen(3000) starts the HTTP server on port 3000. The async/await here is exactly what you learned in Part 2 — starting a server is asynchronous.

The three building blocks

Controller   receives HTTP requests, returns responses. Thin: no business logic.
Service      holds business logic. Called by controllers. Knows nothing about HTTP.
Module       groups related controllers and services and declares their wiring.

This separation is the single most important convention in NestJS and in good backend design generally (Part 11, Part 12). The controller's job is to translate between HTTP and your logic — parse the request, call a service, shape the response. The service's job is the actual work, with no knowledge of HTTP. Keeping them separate means your logic is testable without a web server and your HTTP handling is thin and uniform.

Practical example — the generated hello endpoint

The scaffold comes with a working endpoint you can call right now:

// src/app.controller.ts (generated)
import { Controller, Get } from '@nestjs/common';
import { AppService } from './app.service';

@Controller()                             // this class handles requests
export class AppController {
  constructor(private readonly appService: AppService) {}  // service injected

  @Get()                                  // handles GET /
  getHello(): string {
    return this.appService.getHello();    // delegates to the service
  }
}
curl http://localhost:3000
# Hello World!

Notice the pattern already: the @Get() decorator maps a method to an HTTP route, the controller delegates to an injected service, and the service (not the controller) does the work. Every endpoint you build follows this shape.

Decorators

Those @Controller(), @Get() annotations are decorators — special markers starting with @ that attach metadata to classes and methods. NestJS reads them to know "this class handles requests" and "this method handles GET /". You do not need to understand how decorators work internally yet; treat them as labels that tell NestJS what each piece is for. We will use @Controller, @Get, @Post, @Body, @Param, @Injectable and a few others.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior values the controller/service separation from the first file, keeping controllers thin (translate HTTP, delegate, shape the response) and putting all real logic in services that know nothing about HTTP. This is not ceremony; it is what makes the logic testable in isolation and keeps the HTTP layer uniform and swappable. They let the framework's conventions do their job rather than fighting them, because a codebase that follows NestJS conventions is one any NestJS engineer can read. They run in watch mode (start:dev) during development for the fast feedback loop.

Common mistakes

  • Putting business logic in the controller, making it untestable without a web server and tangling HTTP concerns with domain logic.
  • Fighting the framework's conventions and inventing a bespoke structure nobody else can follow.
  • Not separating configuration from code (we fix this with @nestjs/config next).

Interview questions

  1. What are the roles of a controller and a service in NestJS? The controller handles HTTP (routing, parsing requests, shaping responses) and stays thin; the service holds business logic and knows nothing about HTTP.
  2. Why keep controllers thin? So business logic is testable without a web server and the HTTP layer stays uniform; it separates concerns cleanly.
  3. What is a module in NestJS? A unit that groups related controllers and services and declares how they are wired together.

Practical exercise

Scaffold a new NestJS project, run it in watch mode, and call the default endpoint with curl. Then add a second @Get('health') endpoint that returns { status: 'ok' }, delegating to a service method. Acceptance criteria: curl http://localhost:3000/health returns the JSON, and the logic lives in a service, not the controller.

6.1.2 Configuration and starting PostgreSQL

Concept

Before touching the database, we set up two things properly: configuration from environment variables (Part 4) and a running PostgreSQL instance. We use @nestjs/config to load a .env file and Docker to run PostgreSQL without installing it on your machine.

How it works — configuration

npm install @nestjs/config
// src/app.module.ts -- register config so process.env and ConfigService work
import { Module } from '@nestjs/common';
import { ConfigModule } from '@nestjs/config';

@Module({
  imports: [
    ConfigModule.forRoot({
      isGlobal: true,          // available everywhere without re-importing
    }),
  ],
})
export class AppModule {}
# .env  -- gitignored (Part 4); holds our config
DATABASE_HOST=localhost
DATABASE_PORT=5432
DATABASE_USER=shop
DATABASE_PASSWORD=shop_dev_password
DATABASE_NAME=shop
PORT=3000

How it works — running PostgreSQL with Docker

Docker runs PostgreSQL in a container so you do not install it directly (Part 43 covers containers properly). One command starts a PostgreSQL 17 database matching the .env above:

docker run --name shop-db \
  -e POSTGRES_USER=shop \
  -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=shop_dev_password \
  -e POSTGRES_DB=shop \
  -p 5432:5432 \
  -d postgres:17
# verify it is running and connect to it:
docker ps                                    # shows the running container
docker exec -it shop-db psql -U shop -d shop # open a SQL prompt inside it
# at the psql prompt: \dt lists tables, \q quits

psql is PostgreSQL's command-line client — a shell for running SQL (Part 17 — SQL Mastery). You will use it constantly to inspect data and test queries.

Fail fast on missing config

Validate required configuration at startup, as Part 4 urged, so a missing value is caught immediately with a clear message rather than deep in a request later.

// A small check you can run in main.ts before listen():
const required = ['DATABASE_HOST', 'DATABASE_USER', 'DATABASE_PASSWORD', 'DATABASE_NAME'];
for (const key of required) {
  if (!process.env[key]) throw new Error(`Missing required env var: ${key}`);
}

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior sets up configuration and a reproducible local database before writing feature code, because getting these foundations right prevents a category of "works on my machine" problems. They run the same PostgreSQL major version locally as in production (17 here) so they never hit a version-specific surprise at deploy time. They validate required config at startup. They keep .env gitignored and provide a .env.example so a new teammate can get running in minutes. These are small investments that pay off every day of the project's life.

Common mistakes

  • Hardcoding the database connection in code instead of reading it from config.
  • Running a different PostgreSQL version locally than in production, hitting version-specific bugs late.
  • Not validating config at startup, getting confusing runtime failures.
  • Committing .env with the database password.

Interview questions

  1. Why run the same database major version locally as in production? To avoid version-specific behavior differences that would only surface at deploy time.
  2. Why validate configuration at startup? A missing value caught at boot is a trivial, clear failure; the same value missing at runtime is a confusing production incident.
  3. What is psql? PostgreSQL's command-line client for running SQL and inspecting the database.

Practical exercise

Install @nestjs/config, create a .env with the database settings, start PostgreSQL 17 in Docker, and connect to it with psql to confirm it works. Add a startup check that throws if DATABASE_PASSWORD is missing. Acceptance criteria: the app reads config from .env, you can open a psql prompt against the containerized database, and removing DATABASE_PASSWORD makes the app fail fast at startup.

6.2 The controller, service and DTO

6.2.1 Building the products endpoints

Concept

Now we build the real feature: a products resource with endpoints to list products, get one by id, and create one. We follow the REST conventions from Part 3 and the controller/service split from 6.1. The new piece is the DTO (Data Transfer Object) with validation — the object that describes and checks the shape of incoming request data.

Why does it exist?

Every request body from a client is untrusted (Part 3): it might be missing fields, have wrong types, or contain malicious values. Before any of that data reaches your business logic or your database, it must be validated. A DTO with validation decorators declares exactly what a valid request looks like, and NestJS automatically rejects anything that does not match with a 400 — so your service only ever receives well-formed data. This is the "parse, don't trust" principle from Part 1, built into the framework.

Mental model

The DTO and its validator are the security checkpoint at the entrance. Before a visitor (a request body) is allowed into the building (your logic), the checkpoint verifies their papers: are the required fields present? Are the types right? Are the values in allowed ranges? Anyone who fails is turned away at the door with a clear reason (400 Bad Request), and only verified visitors proceed. Your inner logic never has to wonder whether its input is valid, because nothing invalid gets past the door.

How it works — generate the module

The Nest CLI scaffolds a module, controller and service together:

nest generate resource products
# choose: REST API, and "yes" to generate CRUD entry points
# this creates src/products/ with a module, controller, service, DTOs and entity

The DTO with validation

npm install class-validator class-transformer
// src/products/dto/create-product.dto.ts
import { IsInt, IsString, Length, Min, IsOptional, Max } from 'class-validator';

export class CreateProductDto {
  @IsString()
  @Length(1, 200)                       // name must be 1 to 200 characters
  name!: string;

  @IsInt()
  @Min(0)                               // price in cents, never negative (Part 1)
  @Max(100_000_00)                      // sanity cap: $100,000
  priceCents!: number;

  @IsString()
  @Length(3, 3)                          // ISO 4217 currency code: "USD", "INR", "EUR"
  @IsOptional()
  currency?: string;
}

Each decorator is a rule. @IsString() requires a string; @Length(1, 200) bounds its length; @IsInt() and @Min(0) require a non-negative integer. @IsOptional() marks a field as allowed to be absent. If a request violates any rule, NestJS returns a 400 with a message naming the failing field — you write zero validation code in your controller.

Turning validation on globally

Validation only runs if you enable the validation pipe. Do it once in main.ts so it applies to every endpoint:

// src/main.ts
import { NestFactory } from '@nestjs/core';
import { ValidationPipe } from '@nestjs/common';
import { AppModule } from './app.module';

async function bootstrap() {
  const app = await NestFactory.create(AppModule);
  app.useGlobalPipes(
    new ValidationPipe({
      whitelist: true,              // strip properties not in the DTO
      forbidNonWhitelisted: true,   // reject requests with unknown properties
      transform: true,              // convert payloads to DTO class instances
    }),
  );
  await app.listen(Number(process.env.PORT ?? 3000));
}
bootstrap();

whitelist: true strips any field the client sends that is not in the DTO — so a client cannot sneak extra fields (like isAdmin: true) into your logic. forbidNonWhitelisted goes further and rejects such requests outright. These two settings are a meaningful security measure (Part 39 — Security, mass-assignment protection), and they are one line each.

The controller

// src/products/products.controller.ts
import { Controller, Get, Post, Body, Param, ParseUUIDPipe } from '@nestjs/common';
import { ProductsService } from './products.service';
import { CreateProductDto } from './dto/create-product.dto';
import { Product } from './product.entity';   // the entity class, defined in 74.3.2

@Controller('products')                    // all routes start with /products
export class ProductsController {
  constructor(private readonly products: ProductsService) {}

  @Get()                                   // GET /products
  list(): Promise<Product[]> {
    return this.products.findAll();
  }

  @Get(':id')                              // GET /products/:id
  getOne(@Param('id', ParseUUIDPipe) id: string): Promise<Product> {
    return this.products.findOne(id);       // ParseUUIDPipe rejects non-UUID ids with 400
  }

  @Post()                                  // POST /products
  create(@Body() dto: CreateProductDto): Promise<Product> {
    return this.products.create(dto);       // dto is already validated by the pipe
  }
}

Notice how thin the controller is. Each method extracts input (@Body, @Param), calls the service, and returns the result. No business logic, no validation code (the DTO and pipes handle it), no database access. The @Param('id', ParseUUIDPipe) even validates that the id is a valid UUID before the method runs, returning 400 otherwise. This is a thin controller done right.

HTTP status codes, for free and on purpose

NestJS returns sensible status codes automatically: 200 for a successful GET, 201 for a successful POST. When your service throws a NestJS exception, it maps to the right code: NotFoundException becomes 404, ConflictException becomes 409, BadRequestException becomes 400 (Part 3 on status codes). You throw a meaningful exception; the framework returns the correct HTTP response.

Practical example — calling it

# create a product (POST with a JSON body)
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/products \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"name":"Dental Mirror","priceCents":1299}'
# 201 Created, returns the new product with its generated id

# validation in action: a negative price is rejected
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/products \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"name":"Bad","priceCents":-5}'
# 400 Bad Request: "priceCents must not be less than 0"

# an unknown field is rejected (whitelist)
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/products \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"name":"X","priceCents":100,"isAdmin":true}'
# 400 Bad Request: "property isAdmin should not exist"

# list products
curl http://localhost:3000/products

You wrote no validation logic, yet bad input, negative prices and injected fields are all rejected with clear messages and the correct 400 status. That is the framework and the DTO working together.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Forgetting to enable the ValidationPipe means DTO decorators do nothing and invalid data flows straight through — a silent, dangerous gap. Enable it globally in main.ts.
  • Not setting whitelist lets clients send extra fields that may reach your logic or ORM (mass assignment, Part 39).
  • Business logic creeping into the controller makes it untestable and tangles concerns; keep it in the service.
  • Returning raw entities can leak internal fields; map to a response shape for anything sensitive (Part 1).
  • Missing Content-Type: application/json on the request means the body is not parsed as JSON (Part 3).

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior treats the DTO as the contract and the validation boundary: everything crossing into the application is validated there, so no unvalidated data ever reaches business logic or the database. They enable whitelist and forbidNonWhitelisted as a default security posture. They keep controllers thin and let the framework map exceptions to status codes, which keeps error handling uniform across the whole API. They map entities to explicit response shapes when any field should not be exposed. The result is an API where input validation, status codes and field exposure are handled consistently by design, not sprinkled ad hoc through handlers.

Common mistakes

  • Not enabling the validation pipe, so DTO rules silently do nothing.
  • Validating by hand inside controllers instead of using DTOs.
  • Omitting whitelist, allowing extra/injected fields.
  • Fat controllers with business logic.
  • Returning raw database entities and leaking internal fields.

Interview questions

  1. What is a DTO and why validate it? A Data Transfer Object describing the expected shape of request data; validating it rejects malformed or malicious input at the boundary before it reaches business logic.
  2. What do whitelist and forbidNonWhitelisted do and why do they matter? whitelist strips unknown properties; forbidNonWhitelisted rejects requests containing them — together they prevent clients from injecting unexpected fields (mass assignment).
  3. How does a service signal a 404 to the client in NestJS? By throwing NotFoundException, which the framework maps to a 404 response.
  4. Why keep the controller thin? So logic is testable without HTTP and error handling stays uniform; the controller only translates between HTTP and the service.

Practical exercise

Build the products controller, service (stubbed to return in-memory data for now) and CreateProductDto, enable the global ValidationPipe with whitelist and forbidNonWhitelisted, and verify with curl that a valid create returns 201, a negative price returns 400, and an unknown field returns 400. Acceptance criteria: no validation code appears in the controller, and all three cases behave correctly.

6.3 Basic SQL and connecting to PostgreSQL

6.3.1 A little SQL first

Concept

PostgreSQL is a relational database: it stores data in tables of rows and columns, and you talk to it with SQL (Structured Query Language). Before we have NestJS talk to it, you should see the raw SQL, because the ORM we use later just generates SQL for you, and you cannot debug what you do not understand. Part 17 — SQL Mastery teaches SQL properly; this is the minimum to follow the rest of this Part.

Why does it exist?

Your products need to persist — survive a server restart, be shared across every copy of the server, be queried and filtered efficiently. An in-memory array does none of that. A relational database gives you durable, queryable, concurrent storage with guarantees (a row is either fully written or not at all) that are the foundation of correct backends. SQL is the universal language for working with it.

How it works — the four basic statements

Connect with psql (6.1) and run these. Every backend engineer knows these four cold:

-- CREATE a table: define the shape of the data.
CREATE TABLE products (
  id          uuid PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),  -- unique id, auto-generated
  name        text NOT NULL,                                -- required
  price_cents integer NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),    -- required, non-negative
  currency    char(3) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'USD',
  created_at  timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()            -- when the row was created
);

-- INSERT: add a row.
INSERT INTO products (name, price_cents) VALUES ('Dental Mirror', 1299)
  RETURNING id, name, price_cents, created_at;   -- RETURNING gives back the new row

-- SELECT: read rows.
SELECT id, name, price_cents FROM products;                    -- all products
SELECT id, name, price_cents FROM products WHERE id = $1;      -- one by id ($1 is a parameter)
SELECT * FROM products WHERE price_cents < 2000 ORDER BY name; -- filtered and sorted

-- UPDATE and DELETE: change or remove rows.
UPDATE products SET price_cents = 1399 WHERE id = $1;
DELETE FROM products WHERE id = $1;

Key ideas visible here. A primary key (id) uniquely identifies each row. Constraints (NOT NULL, CHECK (price_cents >= 0)) make invalid rows impossible to insert — the database enforces "price is never negative" so even a buggy application cannot violate it, which is a stronger guarantee than application validation alone. Parameters ($1) are how you safely pass values into a query; never build SQL by gluing strings together with user input, which causes SQL injection (Part 39 — Security, 39.20).

SQL injection: the one security rule to internalize now

-- NEVER do this (string concatenation with user input):
--   "SELECT * FROM products WHERE name = '" + userInput + "'"
-- A userInput of  '; DROP TABLE products; --  destroys your table.

-- ALWAYS do this (parameterized query): the value can never be treated as code.
SELECT * FROM products WHERE name = $1;   -- the driver sends $1 separately from the SQL

The ORM and the database driver parameterize for you, which is one of the main reasons to use them. But you must understand the rule, because the day you write a raw query, this is on you.

Practical example — do it by hand once

In psql, create the table, insert two products, select them back, update one price, and delete one. Seeing the raw rows and the RETURNING output demystifies what the ORM will do. When the ORM later behaves unexpectedly, you will reach for psql and the raw SQL to see the truth.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior always understands the SQL their ORM generates, because the ORM is a convenience over SQL, not a replacement for understanding it, and performance problems live at the SQL level (Part 19 on indexes and query plans). They push invariants into the database as constraints (NOT NULL, CHECK, unique indexes, foreign keys) rather than relying only on application checks, because the database is the last line of defense and enforces the rule for every writer, including future code and manual fixes. They treat parameterization as non-negotiable and would flag any string-concatenated query in review instantly.

Common mistakes

  • Building SQL by concatenating user input (SQL injection).
  • Relying only on application validation and leaving the database able to hold invalid rows (no CHECK, no NOT NULL).
  • Using the ORM without ever understanding the SQL it generates, then being helpless when it is slow or wrong.

Interview questions

  1. What is SQL injection and how do you prevent it? Treating user input as part of the SQL text, letting an attacker run their own SQL; prevent it with parameterized queries ($1), never string concatenation.
  2. Why put constraints like NOT NULL and CHECK in the database instead of only in the application? The database enforces them for every writer and is the last line of defense; application checks can be bypassed or forgotten.
  3. What are the four basic SQL statements? INSERT (create), SELECT (read), UPDATE (change), DELETE (remove).

Practical exercise

In psql, create the products table with the constraints shown, insert two products (one via RETURNING), select them ordered by price, update one price, and try to insert a product with a negative price to see the CHECK constraint reject it. Acceptance criteria: the negative-price insert fails at the database level, proving the constraint protects you regardless of application code.

6.3.2 Connecting NestJS to PostgreSQL with a repository

Concept

Now we connect the API to the database so products persist. We use TypeORM, an ORM (Object-Relational Mapper) that lets you work with database rows as TypeScript objects. You define an entity (a class mapped to a table), and TypeORM gives you a repository with methods like find, findOne and save that generate the SQL for you. The service uses the repository instead of an in-memory array.

Why does it exist?

Writing raw SQL for every operation and manually mapping rows to objects is repetitive and error-prone. An ORM handles the common cases — insert this object, find by id, update these fields — generating correct, parameterized SQL and mapping rows to typed objects automatically. It lets you stay in TypeScript for straightforward data access while dropping to raw SQL for the complex queries where you need control (Part 21 — ORM Engineering covers TypeORM and Sequelize in depth, including when to use the query builder or raw SQL).

Mental model

The repository is a translator between your TypeScript world (objects) and the database world (rows and SQL). You hand it a product object and say "save this"; it writes the INSERT. You say "find the product with this id"; it writes the SELECT and hands you back a product object. You think in objects; it thinks in SQL; the entity is the dictionary that maps between the two.

How it works — install and configure

npm install @nestjs/typeorm typeorm pg
// src/app.module.ts -- wire TypeORM to PostgreSQL using config from .env
import { Module } from '@nestjs/common';
import { ConfigModule } from '@nestjs/config';
import { TypeOrmModule } from '@nestjs/typeorm';
import { Product } from './products/product.entity';
import { ProductsModule } from './products/products.module';

@Module({
  imports: [
    ConfigModule.forRoot({ isGlobal: true }),
    TypeOrmModule.forRoot({
      type: 'postgres',
      host: process.env.DATABASE_HOST,
      port: Number(process.env.DATABASE_PORT ?? 5432),
      username: process.env.DATABASE_USER,
      password: process.env.DATABASE_PASSWORD,
      database: process.env.DATABASE_NAME,
      entities: [Product],
      synchronize: true,   // AUTO-CREATE tables from entities -- DEV ONLY, see below
    }),
    ProductsModule,
  ],
})
export class AppModule {}

A critical warning on synchronize: true: it makes TypeORM automatically change your database schema to match your entities, which is convenient in development but dangerous in production, where it can drop columns and lose data. In production you use migrations (versioned, reviewed schema change scripts, Part 23) and set synchronize: false. Learn it now so you never ship synchronize: true.

The entity

// src/products/product.entity.ts
import { Entity, PrimaryGeneratedColumn, Column, CreateDateColumn } from 'typeorm';

@Entity('products')                            // maps to the "products" table
export class Product {
  @PrimaryGeneratedColumn('uuid')              // id uuid PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid()
  id!: string;

  @Column({ type: 'text' })
  name!: string;

  @Column({ type: 'integer', name: 'price_cents' })  // maps to the price_cents column
  priceCents!: number;

  @Column({ type: 'char', length: 3, default: 'USD' })
  currency!: string;

  @CreateDateColumn({ name: 'created_at', type: 'timestamptz' })
  createdAt!: Date;
}

The entity is the TypeScript mirror of the table. Each @Column maps a class property to a database column. Note name: 'price_cents' maps the camelCase TypeScript property to the snake_case database column — a common convention (Part 1 on mapping).

The repository in the service

// src/products/products.service.ts
import { Injectable, NotFoundException } from '@nestjs/common';
import { InjectRepository } from '@nestjs/typeorm';
import { Repository } from 'typeorm';
import { Product } from './product.entity';
import { CreateProductDto } from './dto/create-product.dto';

@Injectable()
export class ProductsService {
  constructor(
    @InjectRepository(Product) private readonly repo: Repository<Product>,
  ) {}

  async findAll(): Promise<Product[]> {
    // In a real API this is paginated (Part 8.5); 74.5 adds a cursor.
    return this.repo.find({ order: { createdAt: 'DESC' }, take: 100 });
  }

  async findOne(id: string): Promise<Product> {
    const product = await this.repo.findOne({ where: { id } });
    if (!product) throw new NotFoundException(`Product ${id} not found`);
    return product;
  }

  async create(dto: CreateProductDto): Promise<Product> {
    const product = this.repo.create({
      name: dto.name,
      priceCents: dto.priceCents,
      currency: dto.currency ?? 'USD',
    });
    return this.repo.save(product);   // generates the INSERT ... RETURNING
  }
}
// src/products/products.module.ts -- register the entity's repository
import { Module } from '@nestjs/common';
import { TypeOrmModule } from '@nestjs/typeorm';
import { Product } from './product.entity';
import { ProductsController } from './products.controller';
import { ProductsService } from './products.service';

@Module({
  imports: [TypeOrmModule.forFeature([Product])],  // makes the Product repository injectable
  controllers: [ProductsController],
  providers: [ProductsService],
})
export class ProductsModule {}

That is the whole vertical slice. The async code (Part 2), the typed objects (Part 1), the HTTP layer (Part 3), and now persistence are all connected. this.repo.save(product) generates a parameterized INSERT and returns the saved row with its generated id and timestamp; this.repo.findOne({ where: { id } }) generates a parameterized SELECT. The SQL is safe (parameterized) and typed (returns Product objects).

Practical example — end to end with persistence

# create a product; it is now stored in PostgreSQL
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/products \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"name":"Dental Probe","priceCents":899}'
# 201 Created with a real id and createdAt

# restart the server (Ctrl+C, npm run start:dev) -- the product is still there:
curl http://localhost:3000/products
# the probe is returned, because it lives in the database, not memory

# fetch a missing product:
curl http://localhost:3000/products/00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000000
# 404 Not Found: "Product ... not found"

# verify directly in the database:
docker exec -it shop-db psql -U shop -d shop -c "SELECT name, price_cents FROM products;"

Restarting the server and still seeing the product is the moment persistence becomes real: the data lives in PostgreSQL, independent of any one server process. That is the difference between a toy and a backend.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • synchronize: true in production can drop columns and destroy data on a schema change; use migrations (Part 23) and synchronize: false.
  • No database connection (wrong config, database down) makes the app fail to start or every request error; validate config and handle connection errors (Part 18 on connections).
  • Connection pool exhaustion under load if too many queries run at once (Part 3.2 on pooling); the pool has a limit and requests queue beyond it.
  • N+1 queries when loading related data naively (Part 21) — a classic ORM performance trap.
  • Returning the raw entity exposes every column; map to a response shape if any field is internal (Part 1).
  • Large findAll with no pagination can try to load millions of rows; always paginate list endpoints (Part 3, Part 14.5; 6.5 adds it).

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior uses the ORM for the common cases and keeps a clear line to raw SQL for complex or performance-critical queries, never treating the ORM as a black box. They never ship synchronize: true; schema changes go through reviewed migrations so production data is safe. They paginate every list, watch for N+1 query patterns, and are mindful of the connection pool under load. They push invariants into the database as constraints even when the ORM also validates, because the database is the last line of defense. And they keep the layering clean — controller to service to repository — so each layer is testable and the data access is isolated in one place.

Common mistakes

  • Shipping synchronize: true to production and losing data on a schema change.
  • Treating the ORM as magic and never understanding the SQL it runs.
  • Unpaginated list endpoints that try to load huge tables.
  • N+1 queries from naive related-data loading.
  • Returning raw entities and leaking internal columns.

Interview questions

  1. What is an ORM and what does a repository give you? An Object-Relational Mapper maps database rows to typed objects; the repository provides methods (find, findOne, save) that generate parameterized SQL so you work in objects for common cases.
  2. Why is synchronize: true dangerous in production? It auto-alters the schema to match entities and can drop columns and lose data; production schema changes must go through reviewed migrations.
  3. How does using an ORM help prevent SQL injection? It generates parameterized queries, sending values separately from the SQL text, so input cannot be executed as code.
  4. Why should list endpoints be paginated even with an ORM? An unpaginated query can try to load an entire large table into memory, exhausting resources; pagination bounds the result.
  5. What is the N+1 query problem? Loading a list and then issuing a separate query per item for related data, turning one logical read into many queries; it is a common ORM performance trap.

Practical exercise

Wire TypeORM to your PostgreSQL container, define the Product entity, implement the service with the repository, and verify end to end: create a product via curl, restart the server, and confirm the product persists. Then query the row directly in psql to confirm the ORM wrote what you expected. Finally, set synchronize: false and explain what you would need to do instead (a migration) to change the schema safely. Acceptance criteria: products persist across restarts, the database row matches the API response, and you can articulate why synchronize: true is unsafe for production.

6.4 Where to go next

6.4.1 You have built a backend — now deepen each layer

Concept

You now have a working, persistent, validated REST API with clean layering. That is a genuine backend, not a toy. Every system in the rest of this book is this same shape — controllers, services, repositories, a database — scaled up, hardened, and made reliable. This closing section maps what you built to the deep-dive Parts so you know where to go to make each piece production-grade.

How it works — the map

What you built here          Where it goes deep
-------------------------    --------------------------------------------------
