REST endpoints & status      Part 14 (API design), Part 3 (HTTP)
DTO validation & security    Part 8.10 (validation), Part 39 (security, injection, mass assignment)
PostgreSQL & SQL             Parts 16–20 (relational model, SQL, internals, indexes, transactions)
TypeORM repository           Part 21 (TypeORM, Sequelize and raw SQL in depth)
Schema changes (migrations)  Part 23 (migrations), Part 24 (compatibility)
Testing this API             Part 40 (unit, integration and e2e testing)
Running it in production     Parts 43–46 (Docker, Kubernetes, cloud, CI/CD)
Making it reliable           Part 30 (reliability), Part 42 (observability), Part 48 (SRE)
Scaling it                   Parts 22, 26, 27 and 35 (database scaling, caching, messaging, background jobs)

A short list of what to add next, in order

To take this products API from "works on my laptop" toward production, the natural next steps, each covered in a later Part:

  1. Pagination on the list endpoint (Part 3, Part 14.5; built in 6.5) — never return an unbounded list.
  2. Tests (Part 40; first versions in 6.7) — unit-test the service, integration-test the endpoints against a test database.
  3. Migrations (Part 23; first one in 6.8) — replace synchronize with versioned schema changes.
  4. Error handling and logging (Part 14.9, Part 77.2; first version in 6.6) — structured logs, a consistent error format, a request id.
  5. Authentication (Part 82.1 to 22.3) — who can create products?
  6. Caching (Part 26) — cache hot reads in Redis.
  7. Containerization and deployment (Parts 43–46; a first Docker setup in 6.8) — Docker image, CI/CD pipeline, run it on real infrastructure.
  8. Observability (Part 42) — metrics, traces and dashboards so you can see how it behaves in production.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior sees this small API and the largest system they have ever built as the same thing at different scales: requests come in, are validated, are processed by logic that is kept separate from the HTTP layer, and are persisted to a store with real guarantees. The additions — caching, queues, multiple services, replication, observability — are responses to specific pressures (load, reliability, team size), added when the pressure is real, not by default. The discipline you practiced here — thin controllers, validated input, clean layering, understanding the SQL, keeping secrets and schema changes safe — is the same discipline that keeps a system with a thousand engineers maintainable. Master the small version and the large version is familiar.

Common mistakes

  • Thinking production systems are a different kind of thing; they are this, hardened and scaled.
  • Adding complexity (microservices, queues, caches) before there is a real need for it (Part 11 on choosing an architecture).
  • Skipping the fundamentals (tests, migrations, observability) because the happy path works.

Interview questions

  1. What is the basic shape of a backend request, from HTTP to storage? Request arrives, is validated at the boundary, is handled by a service (business logic, no HTTP knowledge), which uses a repository to persist to or read from the database, and a response is returned with an appropriate status code.
  2. What would you add to a working CRUD API to make it production-ready, and in what order? Pagination, tests, migrations, structured logging and error handling, authentication, then caching, deployment and observability as load and reliability needs grow.
  3. Why not start with microservices, queues and caching? They add operational complexity that should be justified by real pressure (load, team size, reliability); the simplest architecture that meets the requirement wins (Part 11).

Practical exercise

Take your products API and add one production step from the list: implement cursor-based pagination on the list endpoint (Part 14.5; 6.5 shows one way), returning a bounded page and a nextCursor. Then write one unit test for the service's findOne that asserts it throws NotFoundException for a missing id. Acceptance criteria: the list endpoint never returns more than the page size, and the test passes, giving you a first taste of the hardening steps the rest of the book covers.

6.5 Step 1: Better validation, update and pagination

6.5.1 Validating every input: bodies, path parameters, query strings and configuration

Concept

So far only the request body of POST /products is validated. A real API has more inputs: the body of an update, path parameters (/products/:id), query strings (?limit=20&cursor=...) and the configuration the app reads at start-up. Each one is untrusted text until it is checked. In this step we add an update endpoint with a partial DTO, a validated query DTO for a paginated list, and a check of the configuration at boot.

Why does it exist?

Because every input an API does not validate is a place where bad data gets in. A query string ?limit=1000000 asks your database for a million rows. ?limit=abc becomes NaN deep in your service. An update body with {"priceCents": "free"} must be rejected exactly like a create body would be. And a missing DATABASE_PASSWORD should stop the app at start-up with a clear message, not fail the first request at 3 a.m.

Mental model

Think of the DTO checkpoint from 6.2 as one door in a building with several entrances. The front door (the body) has a guard. The side door (query strings) and the loading bay (path parameters) need guards too, or people just walk in that way. And the building's own power supply (configuration) is checked before the doors open at all.

How it works

Update with a partial DTO. An update usually changes only some fields. PartialType (from @nestjs/mapped-types, installed by nest generate resource) creates a class where every field of CreateProductDto is optional but keeps all its validation rules:

// src/products/dto/update-product.dto.ts
import { PartialType } from '@nestjs/mapped-types';
import { CreateProductDto } from './create-product.dto';

// Every field optional; when present, the same rules as CreateProductDto apply.
export class UpdateProductDto extends PartialType(CreateProductDto) {}

Query strings are text. Everything in a URL arrives as a string, so ?limit=20 is "20". With transform: true on the ValidationPipe (6.2), @Type(() => Number) from class-transformer converts it before the rules run:

// src/products/dto/list-products.query.ts
import { Type } from 'class-transformer';
import { IsInt, IsOptional, IsString, Max, Min } from 'class-validator';

export class ListProductsQuery {
  @IsOptional()
  @Type(() => Number)          // "20" -> 20 before validation
  @IsInt()
  @Min(1)
  @Max(100)                    // never more than 100 rows per page
  limit: number = 20;          // default when the client sends nothing

  @IsOptional()
  @IsString()
  cursor?: string;             // opaque: the client sends back what we gave it
}

Cursor pagination. Part 14.5 compares offset pagination (?page=3) with cursor pagination. A cursor says "continue after this item", so pages stay correct while new products are added, and the database does not have to skip thousands of rows. Our cursor is the last item's createdAt and id, encoded so the client treats it as an opaque token:

// src/products/cursor.ts
import { BadRequestException } from '@nestjs/common';

export type Cursor = { createdAt: string; id: string };

export const encodeCursor = (c: Cursor): string =>
  Buffer.from(`${c.createdAt}|${c.id}`).toString('base64url');

export function decodeCursor(raw: string): Cursor {
  const [createdAt, id] = Buffer.from(raw, 'base64url').toString('utf8').split('|');
  if (!createdAt || !id || Number.isNaN(Date.parse(createdAt))) {
    throw new BadRequestException({ code: 'INVALID_CURSOR', message: 'The cursor is not valid' });
  }
  return { createdAt, id };
}

The service uses TypeORM's query builder for the "after this item" condition, and fetches one extra row to know whether there is a next page:

// src/products/products.service.ts (additions)
import { decodeCursor, encodeCursor } from './cursor';
import { ListProductsQuery } from './dto/list-products.query';
import { UpdateProductDto } from './dto/update-product.dto';

async list(q: ListProductsQuery): Promise<{ items: Product[]; nextCursor: string | null }> {
  const qb = this.repo.createQueryBuilder('p')
    .orderBy('p.createdAt', 'DESC')
    .addOrderBy('p.id', 'DESC')               // tie-breaker: two products can share a timestamp
    .take(q.limit + 1);                        // one extra row tells us if there is another page
  if (q.cursor) {
    const c = decodeCursor(q.cursor);
    qb.where('(p.createdAt, p.id) < (:createdAt, :id)', { createdAt: c.createdAt, id: c.id });
  }
  const rows = await qb.getMany();
  const items = rows.slice(0, q.limit);
  const last = items[items.length - 1];
  const nextCursor = rows.length > q.limit && last
    ? encodeCursor({ createdAt: last.createdAt.toISOString(), id: last.id })
    : null;
  return { items, nextCursor };
}

async update(id: string, dto: UpdateProductDto): Promise<Product> {
  const product = await this.findOne(id);      // 404 if it does not exist (74.3)
  Object.assign(product, dto);                 // only fields that passed validation are present (whitelist)
  return this.repo.save(product);              // UPDATE ... WHERE id = $n
}

async remove(id: string): Promise<void> {
  const result = await this.repo.delete({ id });
  if (result.affected === 0) throw new NotFoundException(`Product ${id} not found`);
}

The controller stays thin:

// src/products/products.controller.ts (additions)
import { Delete, HttpCode, Patch, Query } from '@nestjs/common';
import { ListProductsQuery } from './dto/list-products.query';
import { UpdateProductDto } from './dto/update-product.dto';

@Get()                                                    // replaces the earlier list()
list(@Query() q: ListProductsQuery) {
  return this.products.list(q);                           // { items, nextCursor }
}

@Patch(':id')                                             // PATCH: change some fields
update(@Param('id', ParseUUIDPipe) id: string, @Body() dto: UpdateProductDto) {
  return this.products.update(id, dto);
}

@Delete(':id')
@HttpCode(204)                                            // success with no body
remove(@Param('id', ParseUUIDPipe) id: string) {
  return this.products.remove(id);
}

Configuration validated at boot. 6.1 checked a list of names by hand. ConfigModule.forRoot accepts a validate function that receives the raw environment and returns the validated config, or throws. Using class-validator, the same tool as the DTOs:

// src/config/env.validation.ts
import { plainToInstance, Type } from 'class-transformer';
import { IsInt, IsString, Max, Min, MinLength, validateSync } from 'class-validator';

class Env {
  @IsString() DATABASE_HOST!: string;
  @Type(() => Number) @IsInt() @Min(1) @Max(65535) DATABASE_PORT: number = 5432;
  @IsString() DATABASE_USER!: string;
  @IsString() @MinLength(8) DATABASE_PASSWORD!: string;
  @IsString() DATABASE_NAME!: string;
  @Type(() => Number) @IsInt() PORT: number = 3000;
}

export function validateEnv(raw: Record<string, unknown>): Env {
  const env = plainToInstance(Env, raw);
  const errors = validateSync(env, { skipMissingProperties: false });
  if (errors.length) {
    const list = errors.map((e) => `${e.property}: ${Object.values(e.constraints ?? {}).join(', ')}`).join('\n');
    throw new Error(`Invalid configuration:\n${list}`);          // the app refuses to start, with the reason
  }
  return env;
}
// src/app.module.ts (change)
ConfigModule.forRoot({ isGlobal: true, validate: validateEnv }),

Practical example — trying every door

curl -s 'http://localhost:3000/products?limit=2' | jq
# { "items": [ {..."name":"Dental Probe"...}, {..."name":"Dental Mirror"...} ], "nextCursor": "MjAyNi0xMS0wNVQwOToz..." }

curl -s "http://localhost:3000/products?limit=2&cursor=MjAyNi0xMS0wNVQwOToz..." | jq '.items | length'
# 2  (the next page; nextCursor is null on the last page)

curl -s -i 'http://localhost:3000/products?limit=1000' | head -n 1
# HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request          ("limit must not be greater than 100")

curl -s -i 'http://localhost:3000/products?limit=abc' | head -n 1
# HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request          ("limit must be an integer number")

curl -s -X PATCH http://localhost:3000/products/<id> -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"priceCents":1499}' | jq .priceCents
# 1499

curl -s -i -X PATCH http://localhost:3000/products/<id> -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"priceCents":"free"}' | head -n 1
# HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request

DATABASE_PASSWORD= npm run start:dev
# Error: Invalid configuration:
# DATABASE_PASSWORD: DATABASE_PASSWORD must be longer than or equal to 8 characters

Every door now has a guard, and every guard answers with 400 and a reason.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Query values are strings: without @Type(() => Number) and transform: true, @IsInt() rejects "20", or worse, code compares "20" > 100 as strings.
  • Unbounded limits: no @Max means a client (or a bug) can ask for every row in the table.
  • Offset pagination drift: with ?page=2, a product added between page requests shifts items, so users see duplicates or miss items; cursors avoid it (Part 14.5).
  • Timestamp precision: PostgreSQL stores microseconds, JavaScript Date keeps milliseconds; two products created in the same millisecond still sort correctly because of the id tie-breaker, but a cursor built from a rounded timestamp can skip a row created in that same millisecond. Many teams store created_at with millisecond precision (timestamptz(3)) to make cursors exact.
  • PATCH with an empty body passes validation and changes nothing; decide whether that is fine or a 400.
  • Configuration typos like DATABSE_HOST leave the real variable missing; validation catches the missing one.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior lists every input of an endpoint (body, path, query, headers, configuration) and puts a validator on each, with bounds on anything that controls how much work the server does. They prefer cursor pagination for lists users scroll through, keep cursors opaque so the format can change later, and always add a tie-breaker to the sort. They make the app refuse to start with bad configuration, because a loud failure at deploy time is cheap and a quiet one in production is not.

Common mistakes

  • Validating bodies but not query strings and path parameters.
  • Pagination without an upper limit.
  • Sorting only by created_at without a unique tie-breaker.
  • Reading process.env.X all over the code instead of validated config.

Interview questions

  1. Why does ?limit=20 need @Type(() => Number)? Query strings arrive as text; the transform converts it so numeric rules apply and the code receives a number.
  2. Why cursor pagination instead of page numbers for a product list? Stable pages while data changes, and no large OFFSET scans; Part 14.5 compares both.
  3. Why add id to the ORDER BY? Two rows can share a timestamp; a unique tie-breaker gives a total order, so cursors never skip or repeat rows.
  4. What should happen when a required environment variable is missing? The app refuses to start with a clear message naming the variable.

Practical exercise

Add PATCH /products/:id, DELETE /products/:id, and the paginated GET /products with limit and cursor. Insert 45 products with a loop in a shell script (4.11) calling curl.

Acceptance criteria:

  • Walking the list with limit=20 returns 20, 20 and 5 items, then nextCursor: null, with no product repeated or missing.
  • limit=0, limit=101, limit=abc and a corrupted cursor each return 400.
  • PATCH with {"priceCents":-1} returns 400; DELETE of a missing id returns 404; a successful DELETE returns 204 with no body.
  • Starting the app with DATABASE_PORT=abc fails at boot with a message naming DATABASE_PORT.

6.6 Step 2: Consistent errors, a request ID and logs

6.6.1 One error shape for every failure

Concept

Right now our API's errors come in several shapes. A validation failure returns NestJS's default { "statusCode": 400, "message": [...], "error": "Bad Request" }. A missing product returns { "statusCode": 404, "message": "Product ... not found" }. A database error, such as a duplicate value in a unique column, returns a bare 500 with "Internal server error". In this step we give every error one shape, with a stable machine-readable code, a list of issues for field problems, and a requestId that links the response to the server's log line.

The shape follows the problem details standard for HTTP APIs (RFC 9457, the successor of RFC 7807) that the rest of this book uses (Part 14.9, and Part 140.6 for how a frontend consumes it):

{
  "type": "about:blank",
  "title": "Validation failed",
  "status": 400,
  "code": "VALIDATION_FAILED",
  "issues": [{ "path": "priceCents", "message": "priceCents must not be less than 0" }],
  "requestId": "7f3c9a1e-5b2d-4c8e-9f10-2a6b3c4d5e6f"
}

Why does it exist?

Because clients write code against errors, not only against successes. A frontend that wants to show "price must not be negative" under the price field needs the field name in a predictable place. A mobile app that wants to say "this product already exists" needs a code it can compare, not an English sentence that changes when someone edits the copy. Support staff need a request ID a customer can read out, so they can find the exact log line. Without one shape, every client grows a pile of special cases, and every special case is a bug waiting to happen.

Mental model

Imagine a hospital where every department writes test results on a different kind of paper, in a different layout. Doctors waste time finding the number they need, and sometimes read the wrong line. A standard form, the same boxes in the same places for every test, with a reference number at the top, makes every result quick to read and easy to trace. The error shape is that standard form.

How it works

flowchart LR
    req["Request"] --> mw["Request ID middleware"]
    mw --> pipe["ValidationPipe"]
    pipe -->|"invalid"| ve["BadRequestException with VALIDATION_FAILED and issues"]
    pipe -->|"valid"| ctl["Controller and service"]
    ctl -->|"not found"| nf["NotFoundException with PRODUCT_NOT_FOUND"]
    ctl -->|"duplicate sku"| db["QueryFailedError code 23505"]
    ctl -->|"bug"| bug["Any other error"]
    ve --> filter["ApiExceptionFilter"]
    nf --> filter
    db --> filter
    bug --> filter
    filter --> res["problem+json with code, issues, requestId"]
  1. A request ID is assigned by a small middleware to every request (or taken from an incoming X-Request-Id header if it looks safe), set on the response header, and included in every log line for that request.
  2. The ValidationPipe is told how to build its error, with exceptionFactory, so validation failures carry code and issues with field paths.
  3. Services throw exceptions with codes: new NotFoundException({ code: 'PRODUCT_NOT_FOUND', message: ... }).
  4. A global exception filter catches everything, recognises known error types (NestJS HttpExceptions, PostgreSQL's unique-violation error), turns unknown errors into a generic 500 without leaking details, logs them with the request ID, and writes the one shape.

To make the duplicate case real, products get a SKU (stock keeping unit), a short unique product code, with a unique constraint in the database:

// src/products/product.entity.ts (addition)
@Column({ type: 'text', unique: true })      // creates a UNIQUE constraint: two products cannot share a SKU
sku!: string;
// src/products/dto/create-product.dto.ts (addition)
@IsString()
@Matches(/^[A-Z0-9-]{3,32}$/, { message: 'sku must be 3 to 32 capital letters, digits or hyphens' })
sku!: string;

Practical example — the code

The request ID middleware:

// src/common/request-id.middleware.ts
import { Injectable, NestMiddleware } from '@nestjs/common';
import type { NextFunction, Request, Response } from 'express';
import { randomUUID } from 'node:crypto';

@Injectable()
export class RequestIdMiddleware implements NestMiddleware {
  use(req: Request, res: Response, next: NextFunction) {
    const incoming = req.header('x-request-id');
    const id = incoming && /^[\w-]{8,64}$/.test(incoming) ? incoming : randomUUID(); // never trust arbitrary input
    req.headers['x-request-id'] = id;
    res.setHeader('X-Request-Id', id);
    next();
  }
}
// src/app.module.ts (addition): apply the middleware to every route
import { MiddlewareConsumer, Module, NestModule } from '@nestjs/common';
import { RequestIdMiddleware } from './common/request-id.middleware';

@Module({ /* imports as before */ })
export class AppModule implements NestModule {
  configure(consumer: MiddlewareConsumer) {
    consumer.apply(RequestIdMiddleware).forRoutes('*');
  }
}

Validation errors with field paths:

// src/common/validation.ts
import { BadRequestException, ValidationPipe, type ValidationError } from '@nestjs/common';

const toIssues = (errors: ValidationError[], parent = ''): { path: string; message: string }[] =>
  errors.flatMap((e) => {
    const path = parent ? `${parent}.${e.property}` : e.property;
    const own = Object.values(e.constraints ?? {}).map((message) => ({ path, message }));
    return [...own, ...toIssues(e.children ?? [], path)];          // nested objects keep a dotted path
  });

export const validationPipe = new ValidationPipe({
  whitelist: true,
  forbidNonWhitelisted: true,
  transform: true,
  exceptionFactory: (errors) =>
    new BadRequestException({ code: 'VALIDATION_FAILED', message: 'Validation failed', issues: toIssues(errors) }),
});

The filter that writes every error:

// src/common/api-exception.filter.ts
import { ArgumentsHost, Catch, ExceptionFilter, HttpException, HttpStatus, Logger } from '@nestjs/common';
import type { Request, Response } from 'express';
import { QueryFailedError } from 'typeorm';

type Issue = { path?: string; message: string };

@Catch()                                                  // no argument: catch everything
export class ApiExceptionFilter implements ExceptionFilter {
  private readonly log = new Logger('ApiExceptionFilter');

  catch(exception: unknown, host: ArgumentsHost) {
    const ctx = host.switchToHttp();
    const req = ctx.getRequest<Request>();
    const res = ctx.getResponse<Response>();
    const requestId = String(req.headers['x-request-id'] ?? '');

    let status = HttpStatus.INTERNAL_SERVER_ERROR;
    let code = 'INTERNAL_ERROR';
    let title = 'Something went wrong';
    let issues: Issue[] = [];

    if (exception instanceof HttpException) {
      status = exception.getStatus();
      const body = exception.getResponse() as string | { code?: string; message?: string | string[]; issues?: Issue[] };
      code = typeof body === 'object' && body.code ? body.code : HttpStatus[status] ?? 'HTTP_ERROR';   // e.g. NOT_FOUND
      title = typeof body === 'string' ? body : Array.isArray(body.message) ? 'Bad request' : body.message ?? title;
      issues = typeof body === 'object' && body.issues ? body.issues : [];
    } else if (exception instanceof QueryFailedError && (exception.driverError as { code?: string }).code === '23505') {
      status = HttpStatus.CONFLICT;                      // 23505: PostgreSQL unique_violation
      code = 'PRODUCT_SKU_TAKEN';
      title = 'A product with this SKU already exists';
      issues = [{ path: 'sku', message: title }];
    } else {
      // A bug or an outage: log everything, tell the client nothing internal.
      this.log.error({ msg: 'unhandled error', requestId, path: req.url, err: (exception as Error)?.stack ?? String(exception) });
    }

    if (status < 500) this.log.warn({ msg: 'request failed', requestId, status, code, path: req.url });
    res.status(status).type('application/problem+json').json({ type: 'about:blank', title, status, code, issues, requestId });
  }
}

Two things to notice. The duplicate check is done by the database, not by a SELECT before the INSERT: two requests at the same moment could both pass a SELECT, but only one can pass a unique constraint (Part 20 explains why). And the 500 branch logs the stack trace but returns only INTERNAL_ERROR and the request ID: stack traces and SQL in responses help attackers, not users.

Wiring it all in one place, so tests (6.7) can use exactly the same setup as main.ts:

// src/configure-app.ts
import type { INestApplication } from '@nestjs/common';
import { validationPipe } from './common/validation';
import { ApiExceptionFilter } from './common/api-exception.filter';

export function configureApp(app: INestApplication): INestApplication {
  app.useGlobalPipes(validationPipe);
  app.useGlobalFilters(new ApiExceptionFilter());
  app.enableShutdownHooks();                       // close the database pool cleanly on SIGTERM (72.3)
  return app;
}
// src/main.ts
import { NestFactory } from '@nestjs/core';
import { AppModule } from './app.module';
import { configureApp } from './configure-app';

async function bootstrap() {
  const app = configureApp(await NestFactory.create(AppModule));
  await app.listen(Number(process.env.PORT ?? 3000));
}
bootstrap();

And the service passes the new field through and gives its not-found error a code:

// src/products/products.service.ts (changes)
// in create():
const product = this.repo.create({ name: dto.name, priceCents: dto.priceCents, sku: dto.sku, currency: dto.currency ?? 'USD' });
// in findOne():
if (!product) throw new NotFoundException({ code: 'PRODUCT_NOT_FOUND', message: `Product ${id} not found` });

Practical example — what clients see now

curl -s -X POST http://localhost:3000/products -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"name":"Dental Mirror","priceCents":-5,"sku":"dm 1"}' | jq
# { "type": "about:blank", "title": "Validation failed", "status": 400, "code": "VALIDATION_FAILED",
#   "issues": [ { "path": "priceCents", "message": "priceCents must not be less than 0" },
#               { "path": "sku", "message": "sku must be 3 to 32 capital letters, digits or hyphens" } ],
#   "requestId": "0b6f..." }

curl -s -X POST http://localhost:3000/products -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"name":"Dental Mirror","priceCents":1299,"sku":"DM-001"}' -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n'
# 201
curl -s -X POST http://localhost:3000/products -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"name":"Another mirror","priceCents":999,"sku":"DM-001"}' | jq '{status, code, issues}'
# { "status": 409, "code": "PRODUCT_SKU_TAKEN", "issues": [ { "path": "sku", "message": "A product with this SKU already exists" } ] }

curl -s -i http://localhost:3000/products/9b1d5f8e-0000-4000-8000-000000000000 | grep -iE '^HTTP|x-request-id'
# HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found
# X-Request-Id: 5c2e...

And the server log for that 404 has the same request ID, so a support ticket that quotes 5c2e... leads straight to it:

grep 5c2e /var/log/products-api/app.log        # or the terminal running npm run start:dev
# [Nest] 4821  - WARN [ApiExceptionFilter] {"msg":"request failed","requestId":"5c2e...","status":404,"code":"PRODUCT_NOT_FOUND","path":"/products/9b1d..."}

Edge cases & failure modes

  • A SELECT-then-INSERT duplicate check races: two requests both see "no such SKU" and both insert, or one fails with an unhandled 500; rely on the unique constraint and map its error.
  • Leaking internals: returning exception.message from unknown errors can expose SQL, table names or file paths.
  • Inconsistent codes: NOT_FOUND here, PRODUCT_NOT_FOUND there; keep a list of codes in one file and review additions like API changes (Part 24).
  • Error text as an API: clients that match on title break when the wording changes; they must use code.
  • Request IDs from clients can be anything, including very long or malicious strings; validate the format or generate a new one.
  • Logging personal data (emails, addresses, tokens) in error logs; log IDs and codes, not payloads (Part 82.31).

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior designs errors as part of the API contract from the first endpoint: one shape, stable codes, field paths that match the request's field names, a request ID on every response, and no internals in any response. They let the database enforce uniqueness and translate its error into a clear 409, because application-level checks race. They log every 5xx with enough context to debug it and every 4xx briefly, keyed by request ID, so a customer's complaint can be traced in seconds. And they write it once, in a filter and a pipe, so no individual endpoint can get it wrong.

Common mistakes

  • Returning different error shapes from different endpoints.
  • Checking for duplicates with a SELECT before inserting.
  • Sending stack traces or SQL errors to clients.
  • No request ID, so a user's error cannot be found in the logs.
  • Clients parsing English error messages instead of codes.

Interview questions

  1. Why should every API error have the same shape? Clients handle errors with one piece of code: codes to branch on, field paths to show messages in the right place, a request ID to trace the failure.
  2. How do you return 409 for a duplicate SKU without a race condition? A unique constraint in the database, and map PostgreSQL's unique-violation error (23505) to 409 in the exception filter.
  3. What should a 500 response contain? A generic code and the request ID; never stack traces or SQL. The details go to the server log under the same request ID.
  4. What is a request ID for? Linking one request's response, log lines and (later) traces, so a reported error can be found quickly.

Practical exercise

Add the SKU column, the request ID middleware, the validation exceptionFactory, the exception filter and configureApp.

Acceptance criteria:

  • Every error response from the API (validation, bad UUID, not found, duplicate SKU, an unexpected error) has Content-Type: application/problem+json and the fields status, code, issues and requestId.
  • Creating two products with the same SKU at the same moment (two curl commands joined with & in one line) creates exactly one product and returns one 201 and one 409.
  • A deliberately thrown new Error('boom') in a test route returns 500 with code: "INTERNAL_ERROR", no stack trace in the body, and the stack trace in the log under the same request ID.
  • Sending X-Request-Id: my-test-request-123 makes the response and the log use that ID.

6.7 Step 3: Tests

6.7.1 A unit test for the service and an end-to-end test for the API

Concept

A test is code that runs your code and checks the result automatically. We write two kinds. A unit test checks one piece (the ProductsService) on its own, with the database replaced by a small fake, so it runs in milliseconds. An end-to-end (e2e) test starts the whole NestJS application, sends real HTTP requests to it with supertest, and lets it talk to a real PostgreSQL test database, so it checks that all the layers work together. NestJS projects come with Jest (the test runner) and supertest already set up by nest new.

Why does it exist?

Because every change can break something that used to work, and checking by hand with curl after every change is slow and gets skipped. With tests, npm test checks 30 behaviours in a few seconds, every time, and CI (Part 46) runs them on every pull request (Part 5). Tests are also the fastest way to know your validation and error work from 6.5 and 6.6 actually does what you think.

Mental model

Imagine a car factory. A unit test is the bench where a single brake pad is pressed against a gauge: fast, precise, but it says nothing about the whole car. The e2e test is the test track: the whole car, real road, real brakes. You need many bench tests and a few laps of the track.

How it works

Unit test. NestJS's Test.createTestingModule builds a small module for the test. We provide the real ProductsService and replace its repository with a fake object, using getRepositoryToken(Product), the same token @InjectRepository(Product) asks for:

// src/products/products.service.spec.ts  (Jest 29, @nestjs/testing 10)
import { NotFoundException } from '@nestjs/common';
import { Test } from '@nestjs/testing';
import { getRepositoryToken } from '@nestjs/typeorm';
import { Product } from './product.entity';
import { ProductsService } from './products.service';

describe('ProductsService', () => {
  let service: ProductsService;
  const repo = {
    findOne: jest.fn(),
    create: jest.fn((data: Partial<Product>) => data),     // returns what it was given
    save: jest.fn(async (p: Partial<Product>) => ({ id: 'p1', createdAt: new Date(), ...p })),
    delete: jest.fn(),
  };

  beforeEach(async () => {
    jest.clearAllMocks();
    const moduleRef = await Test.createTestingModule({
      providers: [ProductsService, { provide: getRepositoryToken(Product), useValue: repo }],
    }).compile();
    service = moduleRef.get(ProductsService);
  });

  it('throws NotFoundException with a code for a missing product', async () => {
    repo.findOne.mockResolvedValue(null);
    await expect(service.findOne('missing-id')).rejects.toBeInstanceOf(NotFoundException);
    await expect(service.findOne('missing-id')).rejects.toMatchObject({ response: { code: 'PRODUCT_NOT_FOUND' } });
  });

  it('defaults the currency to USD on create', async () => {
    const product = await service.create({ name: 'Dental Mirror', priceCents: 1299, sku: 'DM-001' });
    expect(repo.save).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(1);
    expect(product.currency).toBe('USD');
  });

  it('returns 404 semantics when deleting nothing', async () => {
    repo.delete.mockResolvedValue({ affected: 0, raw: [] });
    await expect(service.remove('missing-id')).rejects.toBeInstanceOf(NotFoundException);
  });
});

Run it with npm test. Jest finds files ending in .spec.ts under src/.

E2E test. The e2e test uses a separate database, shop_test, so tests can delete data freely. Create it once in the PostgreSQL container from 6.1:

docker exec -it shop-db psql -U shop -d shop -c 'CREATE DATABASE shop_test;'

The test database name must be set before AppModule is imported, because TypeOrmModule.forRoot({...}) reads process.env when the module file loads. A Jest setup file does that; dotenv and ConfigModule never overwrite a variable that is already set:

// test/setup-env.ts  (listed in test/jest-e2e.json as "setupFiles": ["<rootDir>/setup-env.ts"])
process.env.DATABASE_NAME = 'shop_test';

The test builds the real AppModule, applies configureApp (6.6) so pipes and filters are exactly as in production, and empties the table before each test:

// test/products.e2e-spec.ts  (run with: npm run test:e2e)
import type { INestApplication } from '@nestjs/common';
import { Test } from '@nestjs/testing';
import * as request from 'supertest';                  // the form the Nest 10 template uses
import { DataSource } from 'typeorm';
import { AppModule } from '../src/app.module';
import { configureApp } from '../src/configure-app';

describe('Products API (e2e)', () => {
  let app: INestApplication;
  let db: DataSource;

  beforeAll(async () => {
    const moduleRef = await Test.createTestingModule({ imports: [AppModule] }).compile();
    app = configureApp(moduleRef.createNestApplication());
    await app.init();
    db = app.get(DataSource);
  });

  beforeEach(() => db.query('TRUNCATE TABLE products'));   // every test starts with an empty table
  afterAll(() => app.close());

  const valid = { name: 'Dental Mirror', priceCents: 1299, sku: 'DM-001' };

  it('creates a product and reads it back', async () => {
    const created = await request(app.getHttpServer()).post('/products').send(valid).expect(201);
    expect(created.body).toMatchObject({ name: 'Dental Mirror', priceCents: 1299, currency: 'USD' });
    await request(app.getHttpServer()).get(`/products/${created.body.id}`).expect(200);
  });

  it('rejects a negative price with VALIDATION_FAILED and a field path', async () => {
    const res = await request(app.getHttpServer()).post('/products').send({ ...valid, priceCents: -5 }).expect(400);
    expect(res.headers['content-type']).toMatch(/application\/problem\+json/);
    expect(res.body).toMatchObject({ code: 'VALIDATION_FAILED', issues: [{ path: 'priceCents' }] });
    expect(res.body.requestId).toEqual(expect.any(String));
  });

  it('returns 409 PRODUCT_SKU_TAKEN for a duplicate SKU', async () => {
    await request(app.getHttpServer()).post('/products').send(valid).expect(201);
    const res = await request(app.getHttpServer()).post('/products').send({ ...valid, name: 'Copy' }).expect(409);
    expect(res.body.code).toBe('PRODUCT_SKU_TAKEN');
  });

  it('pages through products with a cursor, without repeats', async () => {
    for (let i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {
      await request(app.getHttpServer()).post('/products').send({ ...valid, sku: `DM-00${i}` }).expect(201);
    }
    const first = await request(app.getHttpServer()).get('/products?limit=3').expect(200);
    const second = await request(app.getHttpServer()).get(`/products?limit=3&cursor=${first.body.nextCursor}`).expect(200);
    const ids = [...first.body.items, ...second.body.items].map((p: { id: string }) => p.id);
    expect(new Set(ids).size).toBe(5);                 // all five, none twice
    expect(second.body.nextCursor).toBeNull();
  });
});

TRUNCATE here only works because synchronize: true created the table in the test database; 6.8 replaces that with a migration, and the test then runs migrations in beforeAll.

Practical example — a failing test finds a bug

You change findOne to use this.repo.findOneBy({ id }) and accidentally drop the if (!product) line while refactoring. You run the tests:

$ npm test
 FAIL  src/products/products.service.spec.ts
  ProductsService
    ✕ throws NotFoundException with a code for a missing product (6 ms)
    ✓ defaults the currency to USD on create (2 ms)
    ✓ returns 404 semantics when deleting nothing (1 ms)

  ● ProductsService › throws NotFoundException with a code for a missing product
    expect(received).rejects.toBeInstanceOf(expected)
    Received promise resolved instead of rejected
    Resolved to value: null

Without the test, GET /products/<missing> would have returned 200 with an empty body, and a frontend would have shown a blank page. With it, the mistake was caught in 6 milliseconds, before a commit.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • Tests against the development database delete your data with TRUNCATE; always use a separate test database.
  • Different wiring in tests and production: tests that skip configureApp pass with errors that look different in production; share one function (Part 140.10 makes the same point for contract tests).
  • Tests that depend on each other's data fail randomly when run in another order; reset state in beforeEach.
  • Mocking too much: a unit test with every method mocked tests the mocks; keep e2e tests for the real wiring (Part 75.5 discusses what to mock).
  • Slow e2e tests from creating the app per test; create it once in beforeAll.
  • Forgetting await on supertest calls: the test ends before the request does and passes without checking anything.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior writes a few fast unit tests for real logic and a handful of e2e tests for the important paths through the real stack, and makes the error contract part of what the tests assert: status, code, field path and request ID. They run tests against a real PostgreSQL rather than mocking the database for e2e, because constraints like the unique SKU only exist in the real thing (Part 75.7 uses Testcontainers for this). They make tests start from a known state and wire the app the same way as production. And they add a test for every bug they fix, so it cannot return.

Common mistakes

  • Testing only the happy path.
  • E2E tests against the development database.
  • Not using configureApp in tests.
  • Writing tests after the feature ships, or never.

Interview questions

  1. What is the difference between a unit test and an e2e test here? The unit test runs the service alone with a fake repository in milliseconds; the e2e test runs the whole app over HTTP against a real database.
  2. How do you replace a TypeORM repository in a NestJS unit test? Provide { provide: getRepositoryToken(Product), useValue: fake } in Test.createTestingModule.
  3. Why use a real database in e2e tests? Constraints, SQL and the ORM's real behaviour only exist there; a duplicate SKU 409 cannot be tested with a fake.
  4. How do you keep e2e tests independent? Reset the data before each test (TRUNCATE), never rely on another test's records.

Practical exercise

Write the unit and e2e tests above, then add your own.

Acceptance criteria:

  • npm test runs at least 5 unit tests in under 2 seconds; npm run test:e2e runs at least 8 e2e tests against shop_test.
  • E2E tests cover: create, get, update, delete, a validation error with a field path, a bad UUID, a duplicate SKU, and pagination without repeats.
  • Every error test asserts code and requestId, not the English title.
  • Deleting the unique constraint from the entity makes the duplicate-SKU test fail.

6.8 Step 4: Running everything with Docker

6.8.1 A Dockerfile, a first migration and Docker Compose

Concept

So far the API runs with npm run start:dev on your laptop, next to a PostgreSQL container started by hand. In this step we package the API itself as a Docker image, replace synchronize: true with a migration, and describe both containers in one Docker Compose file, so anyone can start the whole system with one command: docker compose up. Part 43 — Docker for Backend Engineers explains every piece in depth; here we build a first working version.

Why does it exist?

Because "it works on my machine" is not a deployment plan. A new teammate should not need the right Node version, the right PostgreSQL version and a page of setup steps; a CI server (Part 46) should run exactly what production runs. An image packages your built code with the exact Node runtime it needs. Compose describes how the API and the database start together. And a migration makes schema changes explicit, reviewed and repeatable, instead of letting the ORM guess (6.3).

Mental model

Imagine shipping a ready meal instead of a recipe. The recipe (your source code plus "install Node 22, run npm ci, build") can go wrong in every kitchen. The ready meal (the image) is cooked once and sealed; any oven that can heat it (any machine with Docker) gets the same dish. Compose is the menu card that says which dishes are served together and in what order.

How it works

A first migration. A migration is a versioned file with the SQL to change the schema (up) and to undo it (down). TypeORM's CLI generates one by comparing your entities with the database. It needs a DataSource file that describes the connection:

// src/data-source.ts: used by the TypeORM CLI and by the app
import 'dotenv/config';                                 // load .env when the CLI runs (npm i dotenv)
import { join } from 'node:path';
import { DataSource } from 'typeorm';
import { Product } from './products/product.entity';

export default new DataSource({
  type: 'postgres',
  host: process.env.DATABASE_HOST,
  port: Number(process.env.DATABASE_PORT ?? 5432),
  username: process.env.DATABASE_USER,
  password: process.env.DATABASE_PASSWORD,
  database: process.env.DATABASE_NAME,
  entities: [Product],
  // From source (CLI, tests) load .ts files; from dist/ load only .js, never the .d.ts files the build emits
  migrations: [join(__dirname, 'migrations', __filename.endsWith('.ts') ? '*.ts' : '*.js')],
  synchronize: false,                                   // from now on, only migrations change the schema
});
{
  "scripts": {
    "typeorm": "typeorm-ts-node-commonjs -d src/data-source.ts",
    "migration:generate": "npm run typeorm -- migration:generate",
    "migration:run": "npm run typeorm -- migration:run"
  }
}
docker exec -it shop-db psql -U shop -d shop -c 'DROP TABLE IF EXISTS products;'   # start clean (dev data only!)
npm run migration:generate -- src/migrations/CreateProducts
# Migration .../src/migrations/1730800000000-CreateProducts.ts has been generated successfully.

The generated file contains the SQL TypeORM will run. Read it before committing, as you would read any code:

// src/migrations/1730800000000-CreateProducts.ts (generated, abridged)
import { MigrationInterface, QueryRunner } from 'typeorm';

export class CreateProducts1730800000000 implements MigrationInterface {
  name = 'CreateProducts1730800000000';
  public async up(queryRunner: QueryRunner): Promise<void> {
    await queryRunner.query(`CREATE TABLE "products" ("id" uuid NOT NULL DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(), "name" text NOT NULL,
      "price_cents" integer NOT NULL, "currency" character(3) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'USD', "sku" text NOT NULL,
      "created_at" TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
      CONSTRAINT "UQ_products_sku" UNIQUE ("sku"), CONSTRAINT "PK_products_id" PRIMARY KEY ("id"))`);
  }
  public async down(queryRunner: QueryRunner): Promise<void> {
    await queryRunner.query(`DROP TABLE "products"`);
  }
}

The generator does not know about the CHECK (price_cents >= 0) constraint from 6.3, because the entity does not declare it. Add it yourself in up: ALTER TABLE "products" ADD CONSTRAINT "chk_price_non_negative" CHECK ("price_cents" >= 0). Generated migrations are a starting point, not a finished product.

The app module now uses the same settings and runs pending migrations at start-up:

// src/app.module.ts (TypeORM part)
TypeOrmModule.forRoot({
  ...dataSource.options,                 // import dataSource from './data-source'
  migrationsRun: true,                   // apply pending migrations when the app starts
}),

Running migrations at start-up is fine for one instance. With several instances starting at once, teams run migrations as a separate step before the new version starts (Part 23, and Part 143.11 shows it in a test stack).

The Dockerfile. A multi-stage build (Part 86.6) compiles TypeScript in a first stage that has all the build tools, then copies only the compiled code and production dependencies into a small final image:

# Dockerfile
FROM node:22-alpine AS build
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
RUN npm ci                                  # exact versions from package-lock.json (72.6)
COPY . .
RUN npm run build && npm prune --omit=dev   # compile to dist/, then drop dev dependencies

FROM node:22-alpine
WORKDIR /app
ENV NODE_ENV=production
COPY --from=build /app/package.json ./
COPY --from=build /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY --from=build /app/dist ./dist
USER node                                   # do not run as root (72.2)
EXPOSE 3000
CMD ["node", "dist/main.js"]
# .dockerignore: keep the build context small and secrets out of the image
node_modules
dist
.env
.git
coverage

Docker Compose. One file starts PostgreSQL, waits until it is ready, then starts the API:

# docker-compose.yml
services:
  db:
    image: postgres:17
    environment:
      POSTGRES_USER: shop
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: shop_dev_password
      POSTGRES_DB: shop
    volumes:
      - db-data:/var/lib/postgresql/data      # data survives docker compose down (not down -v)
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U shop -d shop"]
      interval: 2s
      retries: 30

  api:
    build: .
    environment:
      DATABASE_HOST: db                       # the service name: Compose's network resolves it (Part 86.7)
      DATABASE_PORT: "5432"
      DATABASE_USER: shop
      DATABASE_PASSWORD: shop_dev_password
      DATABASE_NAME: shop
      PORT: "3000"
    ports:
      - "3000:3000"
    depends_on:
      db:
        condition: service_healthy            # start only when PostgreSQL accepts connections
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD", "wget", "-qO-", "http://localhost:3000/health"]
      interval: 5s
      retries: 12

volumes:
  db-data:

Practical example — the whole system in three commands

docker stop shop-db                            # the hand-started database from 74.1 would hold port 5432
docker compose up --build -d                   # build the API image, start db then api, in the background
docker compose ps
# NAME                 SERVICE  STATUS                   PORTS
# products-api-db-1    db       Up 12 seconds (healthy)  5432/tcp
# products-api-api-1   api      Up 6 seconds (healthy)   0.0.0.0:3000->3000/tcp

curl -s -X POST http://localhost:3000/products -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"name":"Dental Mirror","priceCents":1299,"sku":"DM-001"}' | jq '{id, sku}'
docker compose logs api | tail -n 5            # the app's logs, including "Migration CreateProducts... has been executed"
docker compose down                            # stop everything; the db-data volume keeps the products

docker image ls shows the API image at roughly 200 MB (the Node runtime is most of it); the source code, .env and dev dependencies are not in it.

Edge cases & failure modes

  • localhost inside a container means that container; the API reaches the database at db, the Compose service name.
  • Starting before the database is ready: without the health check and condition: service_healthy, the API crashes with "connection refused" on the first start.
  • Secrets in the image: a missing .dockerignore copies .env into the image, where anyone with the image can read it.
  • synchronize: true left on alongside migrations makes the ORM change the schema behind the migrations' back; set it to false.
  • docker compose down -v deletes the volume and all its data; fine for development, never on a real database.
  • Apple Silicon versus x86 servers: images built on an ARM laptop may not run on an x86 server; build in CI (Part 86.15) or use --platform.
  • Port 3000 or 5432 already used by an old process or container (4.10): stop it first.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior wants the whole system to start from a clean machine with one command, because that is what makes onboarding, CI and production boring. They build small images with multi-stage builds, run as a non-root user, keep secrets out of images, and gate start-up on real health checks. They treat migrations as code: generated, read, completed by hand where needed, committed and reviewed. And they know this Compose file is a development and test setup; production adds managed databases, secrets from a secret manager and an orchestrator (Parts 44, 45).

Common mistakes

  • No .dockerignore, so node_modules and .env end up in the image.
  • Running the container as root.
  • No health checks, so start-up order is luck.
  • Trusting generated migrations without reading them.

Interview questions

  1. Why a multi-stage Dockerfile for a NestJS app? The build stage has compilers and dev dependencies; the final image has only compiled code and production dependencies, so it is smaller and safer.
  2. How does the API container find the database in Compose? By the service name (db) on the network Compose creates.
  3. Why replace synchronize: true with migrations before running in containers? Migrations are explicit, reviewed, versioned schema changes that run the same way everywhere; synchronize can drop data.
  4. What does depends_on with condition: service_healthy add? The API starts only after PostgreSQL's health check passes, not merely after its container starts.

Practical exercise

Containerise your API.

Acceptance criteria:

  • docker compose up --build on a machine with only Docker installed starts both services, and both show (healthy).
  • synchronize is false; the schema comes from a committed migration that includes the price CHECK constraint.
  • The final image runs as the node user and contains no .env file (check with docker run --rm --entrypoint ls <image> -la /app).
  • After docker compose down and up again, previously created products are still there.
  • The e2e tests from 6.7 pass against the database in Compose (port 5432 published, DATABASE_NAME=shop_test).

6.9 Putting it together: the finished project

6.9.1 One request through every layer, and what you have built

Concept

You started this Part with a hello endpoint. You now have a small but genuine backend: validated inputs on every door, cursor pagination, consistent errors with request IDs, a unique constraint enforced by the database, unit and e2e tests, a migration, and a Docker setup that starts everything with one command. This section follows one request through all of it and lists the finished project's files, so you can see the whole shape at once.

How it works — one request, end to end

POST /products with a duplicate SKU, inside Docker Compose:

curl (your laptop)
  -> port 3000 published by Compose -> api container, node dist/main.js
  -> RequestIdMiddleware: X-Request-Id 5c2e... assigned            (74.6)
  -> ValidationPipe: body matches CreateProductDto, whitelisted      (74.2, 74.6)
  -> ProductsController.create: thin, calls the service             (74.2)
  -> ProductsService.create: repo.create + repo.save                 (74.3)
  -> TypeORM: INSERT INTO "products" (...) VALUES ($1, ...) RETURNING ...
  -> db container, PostgreSQL 17: unique constraint UQ_products_sku violated, error 23505
  -> ApiExceptionFilter: 409, code PRODUCT_SKU_TAKEN, issues[sku]   (74.6)
  -> logger: WARN request failed requestId=5c2e... code=PRODUCT_SKU_TAKEN
  <- 409 application/problem+json { status, code, issues, requestId }

Every layer has one job, and each job was added in one step of this Part. The e2e test "returns 409 PRODUCT_SKU_TAKEN for a duplicate SKU" (6.7) proves this whole path in about 30 milliseconds.

The finished project

products-api/
├── Dockerfile, .dockerignore, docker-compose.yml          74.8
├── .env (gitignored), .env.example                         74.1
├── src/
│   ├── main.ts, configure-app.ts                           74.1, 74.6
│   ├── app.module.ts                                       74.1, 74.3, 74.6, 74.8
│   ├── data-source.ts, migrations/                         74.8
│   ├── config/env.validation.ts                            74.5
│   ├── common/request-id.middleware.ts                     74.6
│   ├── common/validation.ts, common/api-exception.filter.ts 74.6
│   └── products/
│       ├── products.module.ts, products.controller.ts      74.2, 74.5
│       ├── products.service.ts, products.service.spec.ts   74.3, 74.5, 74.7
│       ├── product.entity.ts, cursor.ts                    74.3, 74.5, 74.6
│       └── dto/ create-product.dto.ts, update-product.dto.ts, list-products.query.ts
└── test/products.e2e-spec.ts                               74.7

Compare it with the tiny products API without a framework in Part 131.7: the same idea (receive, validate, store, respond), now with structure, guarantees and tests.

How a Senior Backend Engineer thinks about this

A senior would recognise this project as a sound starting point, and would also see exactly what is missing for production: authentication (Part 39), idempotency for retried creates (Part 14.12), structured JSON logs, metrics and traces (Part 42), a CI pipeline that runs the tests and builds the image (Part 46), and migrations run as a separate deploy step (Part 23). None of those require changing the shape you built. That is the point of getting the shape right first.

Practical exercise

The capstone for Volume 0: extend the project with one new resource, categories, and link products to it.

Acceptance criteria:

  • A categories table created by a new migration, with a unique slug, and a category_id foreign key on products (Part 16.5 explains foreign keys).
  • POST /categories, GET /categories, and GET /products?category=<slug> with the same cursor pagination as 6.5.
  • Every error uses the shape from 6.6; creating a product with an unknown category returns 422 with code: "CATEGORY_NOT_FOUND" and issues[0].path equal to categoryId.
  • Unit and e2e tests for the new endpoints pass, and the whole system starts with docker compose up --build.
  • The work is done on a branch, in focused commits, merged through a pull request (Part 133.11).

Senior Checklist — Part 6

  • I understand the concept
  • I can explain it simply
  • I can implement it
  • I understand its failure modes
  • I understand its scaling limitations
  • I understand its security implications
  • I understand its operational implications
  • I can choose between alternatives
  • I can explain the tradeoffs
  • I can answer senior-level interview questions
  • I can scaffold a NestJS app and keep controllers thin, logic in services
  • I validate all input with DTOs and a global ValidationPipe (whitelist on)
  • I can write the four basic SQL statements and know why parameterization matters
  • I can connect NestJS to PostgreSQL via a TypeORM entity and repository
  • I know why synchronize:true is unsafe in production and what migrations are for
  • I can trace a request from HTTP through validation, service and repository to the database
  • I know which later Parts harden each layer for production
  • I validate query strings, path parameters and configuration, not only bodies, and bound every limit
  • I can implement cursor pagination with a unique tie-breaker
  • I return one error shape with stable codes, field paths and a request ID, and never leak internals
  • I let the database enforce uniqueness and map its error to a clear 409
  • I can write a NestJS unit test with a fake repository and an e2e test with supertest against a real test database
  • I can generate, read and complete a TypeORM migration
  • I can run the API and PostgreSQL with a multi-stage Dockerfile and Docker Compose with health checks

Part 6 — Key Takeaways

  • A backend is a loop: receive a request, validate it, run logic (kept separate from HTTP), persist to a store with real guarantees, return a response with the right status code.
  • NestJS organizes this into thin controllers (HTTP), services (logic), and repositories (data); keeping them separate makes everything testable and uniform.
  • Validate every incoming request with a DTO and a global ValidationPipe; whitelist and forbidNonWhitelisted block injected fields for free.
  • Understand the SQL even when an ORM writes it; parameterize always (never concatenate user input), and push invariants into the database as constraints.
  • Connect NestJS to PostgreSQL with a TypeORM entity and repository; you work in typed objects while the ORM generates safe, parameterized SQL.
  • Never ship synchronize: true; production schema changes go through reviewed migrations (Part 23).
  • Persistence is what makes it a backend: data lives in the database, surviving restarts and shared across every server instance.
  • Every input is a door: bodies, paths, query strings and configuration all need validation and bounds.
  • Errors are part of the API: one shape, stable codes, field paths, a request ID, and no internals in responses.
  • Tests turn "I checked it with curl once" into "it is checked on every change"; use a real database for e2e.
  • One docker compose up that starts everything is the difference between a project and a recipe.
  • Production systems are this same shape, hardened and scaled; add complexity (caching, queues, services) only when real pressure demands it.
  • The discipline practiced here — thin controllers, validated input, clean layering, safe SQL and schema, secrets out of git — is the same discipline that keeps large systems maintainable.
  • Volume 0 is complete: you can now build, test and run a small correct backend, which is the foundation every later Part builds on.
